🏛️ Backstage Architecture — How Everything Connects

Backstage Platform Architecture — Frontend ↔ Backend ↔ Database
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        🌐 BROWSER — users access backstage.anihpj.com         │
  │  ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │                        REACT FRONTEND (TypeScript + MUI v4)             │  │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ │  │
  │  │  │ Catalog  │ │Create    │ │TechDocs  │ │Search    │ │Settings      │ │  │
  │  │  │ Plugin   │ │Plugin    │ │Plugin    │ │Plugin    │ │Plugin        │ │  │
  │  │  └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘ └──────┬───────┘ │  │
  │  │       │             │            │            │              │          │  │
  │  │  ┌────┴─────────────┴────────────┴────────────┴──────────────┴───────┐ │  │
  │  │  │                    CORE API (configApi, alertApi, errorApi, ...)   │ │  │
  │  │  └────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┘ │  │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                      │  HTTP /api/*                            │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │                        NODE.JS BACKEND (Express + Knex.js)              │  │
  │  │  ┌────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────────────┐ │  │
  │  │  │                    PLUGIN BACKENDS (routers)                        │ │  │
  │  │  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ │ │  │
  │  │  │  │Catalog   │ │Scaffolder│ │TechDocs  │ │Search    │ │Auth      │ │ │  │
  │  │  │  │Backend   │ │Backend   │ │Backend   │ │Backend   │ │Backend   │ │ │  │
  │  │  │  └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘ │ │  │
  │  │  └───────┼────────────┼────────────┼────────────┼────────────┼───────┘ │  │
  │  │          │            │            │            │            │          │  │
  │  │  ┌───────┴────────────┴────────────┴────────────┴────────────┴───────┐ │  │
  │  │  │              coreServices: database, config, logger, scheduler     │ │  │
  │  │  └────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────────┘ │  │
  │  └───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                      │  Knex.js (SQL)                          │
  │  ┌───────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┐  │
  │  │                     🗄️  POSTGRESQL DATABASE                             │  │
  │  │  ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐ ┌──────────────┐   │  │
  │  │  │catalog       │ │scaffolder    │ │techdocs      │ │auth          │   │  │
  │  │  │entities,     │ │tasks,        │ │builds,       │ │sessions,     │   │  │
  │  │  │relations     │ │templates     │ │metadata      │ │tokens        │   │  │
  │  │  └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘ └──────────────┘   │  │
  │  │                        Per-plugin schema namespacing                     │  │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                                                │
  │  📦 EXTERNAL INTEGRATIONS (via plugins):                                       │
  │  GitHub · GitLab · Jenkins · Grafana · PagerDuty · Kubernetes · AWS · GCP     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

🗂️ Backstage Entity Hierarchy — The 7+1 Kinds

anihpj/jobpost Mapped to the Backstage Entity Model
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                              │
  │  🌐 DOMAIN: anihpj                    ← Business area        │
  │     │                                                        │
  │     ├── 🔧 SYSTEM: JobPost            ← Collection of related│
  │     │       components                                      │
  │     │                                                       │
  │     │   ┌── 📦 COMPONENT: jobpost-api  ← Django REST API    │
  │     │   │   ├── type: service                               │
  │     │   │   ├── lifecycle: production                       │
  │     │   │   ├── owner: team-alpha                           │
  │     │   │   ├── dependsOn: [postgres-db, redis-cache]       │
  │     │   │   └── providesApis: [jobpost-openapi]             │
  │     │   │                                                   │
  │     │   ├── 📦 COMPONENT: jobpost-web  ← Django frontend    │
  │     │   │   ├── type: website                               │
  │     │   │   └── dependsOn: [jobpost-api]                    │
  │     │   │                                                   │
  │     │   ├── 🗄️  RESOURCE: postgres-db    ← PostgreSQL 15    │
  │     │   │   └── type: database                              │
  │     │   │                                                   │
  │     │   ├── 🗄️  RESOURCE: redis-cache    ← Redis 7          │
  │     │   │   └── type: cache                                 │
  │     │   │                                                   │
  │     │   └── 📡 API: jobpost-openapi      ← OpenAPI 3.0 spec │
  │     │       └── type: openapi                               │
  │     │                                                       │
  │     ├── 👤 USER: owner                   ← Individual dev   │
  │     └── 👥 GROUP: team-alpha             ← Engineering team │
  │                                                              │
  │  8 ENTITY KINDS: Component · API · Resource · System ·      │
  │  Domain · User · Group · Template (Scaffolder)               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Every service, database, team, and API in your org maps to one of these 7+1 entity kinds. The exam tests your ability to choose the right kind for a given scenario.

🐍 anihpj/jobpost — Your Running Example

Django Application Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                                                              │
  │  anihpj/jobpost — Django Job Board Application               │
  │                                                              │
  │  📁 anihpj/              ← Django project                    │
  │  │  ├── settings.py      ← DB config, Redis, CORS            │
  │  │  ├── urls.py          ← REST API endpoints                │
  │  │  └── wsgi.py          ← Gunicorn entry point              │
  │  │                                                           │
  │  📁 jobpost/             ← Django app                        │
  │  │  ├── models.py        ← Job, Company, Application models  │
  │  │  ├── views.py         ← DRF ViewSets + serializers        │
  │  │  └── admin.py         ← Django admin registration         │
  │  │                                                           │
  │  📁 k8s/                 ← 14 Kubernetes manifests            │
  │  │  ├── deployment.yaml  ← Gunicorn + 3 replicas             │
  │  │  ├── service.yaml     ← ClusterIP:8000                    │
  │  │  ├── ingress.yaml     ← TLS + nginx                       │
  │  │  ├── pvc.yaml         ← PostgreSQL 20Gi                   │
  │  │  └── secret.yaml      ← DB password, Django SECRET_KEY    │
  │  │                                                           │
  │  📁 catalog/             ← 6 Backstage entity YAML files      │
  │  │  ├── component-jobpost-api.yaml                           │
  │  │  ├── component-jobpost-web.yaml                           │
  │  │  ├── resource-postgres.yaml                               │
  │  │  ├── resource-redis.yaml                                  │
  │  │  ├── api-jobpost-openapi.yaml                             │
  │  │  └── system-jobpost.yaml                                  │
  │  │                                                           │
  │  🐳 Dockerfile           ← Multi-stage, distroless, non-root │
  │  ⚙️  docker-compose.yml   ← Local dev with PostgreSQL+Redis   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Every question references this app — you'll learn Backstage concepts through a real Django codebase deployed on Kubernetes.

🔑 Key Backstage Concepts — Quick Reference

Concept What You Must Know
Entity Kinds Component, API, Resource, System, Domain, User, Group (+Template)
catalog-info.yaml apiVersion + kind + metadata(name, annotations, labels) + spec
Config Layers APP_CONFIG_* env vars > app-config.local.yaml > app-config.{env}.yaml > app-config.yaml
MUI Version MUI v4 (makeStyles, @material-ui/core) — NOT v5 (@mui/material)
Database PostgreSQL (prod) / SQLite (dev) — via Knex.js, coreServices.database
Scaffolder Template YAML → cookiecutter → publish → register. Task polling pattern.
TechDocs MkDocs from repo docs/ → build → publish to GCS/S3. Rebuild: UI / Auto / API.
Auth GitHub, Okta, Google, GitLab. SignInPage component in createApp().

✅ Daily Study Checklist

  • 📖 Morning (30 min): Read 5–10 MCQs + review explanations. Focus on one domain per day.
  • 🔬 Afternoon (45 min): Deep-dive the domain of the day — re-read explanations, study diagrams, write catalog-info.yaml from memory.
  • ⚡ Evening (30 min): Quick-fire review — answer 10 random questions from previous domains to maintain retention.
  • 📅 Weekend (2–3 hrs): Full review of the week's domains. Take a 50-question timed mini-test.
  • 🎯 Day Before: Review exam battle plan. Focus on Customizing (32%). Sleep well.
  • 🏁 Exam Morning: 30 min review of entity kinds + MUI v4 imports. Stay calm — you've prepared.

🏠 Master Summary & Implementation RoadmapSTART HERE

🎯 What This Document Is — And How To Use It

This is your complete CBA exam preparation system for Backstage — the CNCF Internal Developer Portal. It's a comprehensive 200-question MCQ bank built around a real Django application (anihpj/jobpost) running on your 6-cluster Kubernetes lab, covering all 4 official Linux Foundation CBA domains.

🔵
Domain 1
Dev Workflow
Q1-Q48 · 24%
🟢
Domain 2
Infrastructure
Q49-Q92 · 22%
🟠
Domain 3
Catalog
Q93-Q136 · 22%
🟣
Domain 4
Customizing
Q137-Q200 · 32%

📝 200 Multiple Choice Questions

Purpose: Build foundational knowledge across all 4 CBA domains. Every question has a detailed explanation with diagrams and real-world context from anihpj/jobpost.

Domains covered: 🔵 Dev Workflow Q1-Q48 (24%) · 🟢 Infrastructure Q49-Q92 (22%) · 🟠 Catalog Q93-Q136 (22%) · 🟣 Customizing Q137-Q200 (32%).

How to use: Answer 20-30 questions per study session. Read the explanation even for correct answers — they contain deeper insights and real-world anihpj/jobpost context. Target: 85%+ correct across all 4 domains before exam day.

🏆 What You'll Master

Backstage core concepts: Software Catalog (entity model, YAML, discovery), Software Templates (scaffolder, actions), TechDocs (MkDocs, publishing), and the Plugin architecture (frontend + backend).

Practical skills: Writing catalog-info.yaml, registering entities, creating golden-path templates, customizing the React UI with MUI v4, deploying Backstage on Kubernetes, and configuring auth providers (GitHub, Okta, etc.).

Exam readiness: Domain-weighted practice (32% Customizing is heaviest), 200 detailed explanations with answer reasoning, and battle-tested exam-day strategies.

✅ Daily Implementation Checklist
  • Morning (30 min): Read 5-10 MCQs + review explanations. Focus on one domain per day.
  • Afternoon (45 min): Deep-dive the domain of the day — re-read explanations, study diagrams, write catalog-info.yaml from memory.
  • Evening (30 min): Quick-fire review — answer 10 random questions from previous domains to maintain retention.
  • Weekend (2-3 hours): Full review of the week's domains. Take a 50-question timed mini-test. Review all wrong answers thoroughly.
  • Day before exam: Review the exam battle plan. Skim all 200 explanations — focus on Customizing (32%) domain. Get a good night's sleep.
  • Exam day morning: 30 minutes before: Quick review of entity kinds and MUI v4 component imports. Stay calm — you've prepared.

🏆 Skills You'll Master — Before vs After This Guide

❌ BEFORE — Common Beginner Pitfalls

  • ❌ "What's an Internal Developer Portal?" — Never heard of Backstage
  • ❌ "Where's my service? What's the deploy status?" — No catalog, no visibility
  • ❌ "I need 10 tabs open to manage one service" — Tool sprawl nightmare
  • ❌ "How do I create a new service?" — No golden path, manual bootstrapping
  • ❌ "Docs are on Confluence — which version is current?" — Stale, scattered docs
  • ❌ "I can't find the right YAML for catalog-info" — Entity model unknown
  • ❌ "How do I add my tool to Backstage?" — Plugin architecture unknown

✅ AFTER — Skills This Guide Builds

  • ✅ Build and run Backstage locally with yarn dev in <2 minutes
  • ✅ Model your entire org as entities: Components, APIs, Resources, Systems
  • ✅ Write catalog-info.yaml and register entities from GitHub
  • ✅ Create golden-path Software Templates for new services
  • ✅ Set up TechDocs with MkDocs — docs that live WITH your code
  • ✅ Install community plugins (GitHub, Grafana, Kubernetes, PagerDuty)
  • ✅ Customize the Backstage React app with Material UI components

⚔️ Exam Day Battle Plan — The First 5 Minutes & Beyond

⏱️ 0-2 min
Setup
Read ALL questions
Mark Easy/Med/Hard
Prioritize Customizing (32%)
⏱️ 2-60 min
Execute
Easy first (build confidence)
Then medium, then hard

🚨 Critical Do's & Don'ts During The CBA Exam
  • ✅ DO: Read the question TWICE — CBA questions often have subtle wording differences ("must" vs "should").
  • ✅ DO: Start with Customizing Backstage (32%) questions — they carry the most weight per question.
  • ✅ DO: Know the entity kinds cold: Component, API, Resource, System, Domain, User, Group, Template.
  • ✅ DO: Eliminate obviously wrong answers first — improves your odds if you need to guess.
  • ❌ DON'T: Spend more than 90 seconds on any single question. Flag it and return.
  • ❌ DON'T: Confuse frontend plugins with backend plugins — they serve different roles.
  • ❌ DON'T: Leave any question blank — there is NO penalty for wrong answers.
  • ❌ DON'T: Overthink "Customizing" questions — the CBA tests FUNDAMENTAL understanding, not deep plugin dev.

📊 Exam Overview & StrategySTRATEGY

CBA Exam Format & Preparation Strategy

Exam: Certified Backstage Associate (CBA) | Duration: 90 minutes | Format: Multiple choice + multiple-select | Passing Score: 75% | Cost: $250 | Certification Validity: 2 years | Prerequisite: None (this guide covers everything)

4 Official LF Domains: Backstage Development Workflow (24%), Backstage Infrastructure (22%), Backstage Catalog (22%), Customizing Backstage (32%). Customizing Backstage at 32% is the HEAVIEST — prioritize plugins, React code, and Material UI components.

Exam Strategy: Read all questions first (2 min) → Start with Customizing (32% weight) → Then Catalog (22%) → Then Infrastructure (22%) → Finish with Dev Workflow (24%) → Return to flagged questions → Leave nothing blank. The exam has NO penalty for wrong answers.

Your 6-cluster lab (kubeadm, kind-cka-lab, kind-cl1, kind-cl2, kind-cl3, minikube) mirrors real production environments. Use ksw to switch contexts. The anihpj/jobpost Django application is deployed across these clusters for hands-on practice.

📚 Pre-Learning: Backstage & Developer Portal FoundationsF.1–F.5

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Building Your Developer Portal Mental Model

F.1 What Problem Does Backstage Solve? — The Developer Experience Crisis — The "tool sprawl" nightmare: 15+ tools, zero integration, 10+ logins. Cognitive load on developers. The Spotify origin story — 1,000+ engineers, 10,000+ components, chaos. How Backstage solves it: ONE URL, ONE login, EVERYTHING connected. anihpj BEFORE and AFTER Backstage (ASCII diagram). Backstage as CNCF Incubating project (donated 2020).

F.2 Backstage Architecture — How It Works Under the Hood — Frontend: React + TypeScript + Material-UI. Backend: Node.js + Express. Plugin system: frontend plugins + backend plugins. Database: PostgreSQL (prod) / SQLite (dev). app-config.yaml: the central configuration hub. The Scaffolder: template execution engine. Catalog processing loop: fetch → parse → validate → enrich → stitch → store. Architecture layered diagram.

F.3 The Software Catalog — Your First Entity Deconstructed — What is an entity? The 7 entity kinds: Component, API, Resource, System, Domain, User, Group. catalog-info.yaml structure: apiVersion, kind, metadata, spec. Entity lifecycle: experimental → production → deprecated. Relationships: dependsOn, providesApis, partOf, ownedBy. Your first entity: catalog-info.yaml for anihpj/jobpost-api annotated line by line.

F.4 The anihpj/jobpost Application — Your Backstage Playground — Django job board (web + API + PostgreSQL + Redis). Complete file structure: anihpj/ project + k8s/ manifests (14 YAML files) + catalog/ directory (6 entity YAML files) + backstage/ configuration. Dockerfile: multi-stage build, distroless, non-root user. How each tier maps to Backstage entities. Full entity hierarchy diagram.

F.5 Backstage vs Other Developer Portals — Making the Right Choice — Backstage vs Port vs Cortex vs OpsLevel. Comparison matrix: open source (Apache 2.0 vs proprietary), self-hosted vs SaaS, plugin ecosystem (200+ vs limited), built-in TechDocs + Templates (unique to Backstage), CNCF status (Incubating — only Backstage). When to choose each platform.

If you're new to Backstage: Read through F.1–F.5 before diving into Part 1. These foundations will give you the mental models needed to understand the Catalog, Templates, TechDocs, and plugin ecosystem. The entire guide uses the anihpj/jobpost Django application as its running example with complete catalog-info.yaml files.

🔵 Category 1: Backstage Development Workflow (24%)Q1-Q48

🟢 Novice-Friendly: Building & Running Backstage as a Developer

This category is about BUILDING and RUNNING Backstage as a developer. Think of Backstage like a workshop — you need to know how to start the tools (yarn dev), organize your workspace (monorepo structure), compile your code (TypeScript), manage your supplies (Yarn/NPM), and package your output (Docker images). For anihpj/jobpost, Backstage is the developer portal where you'll model your Django services as catalog entities.

Q1-Q48 48 Questions 🟢 Beginner

1.1 What is Backstage? — Understanding the IDPQ1-Q10

Dev Workflow Q1 of 200
What is the PRIMARY problem that Backstage solves for engineering organizations?
A) It replaces Kubernetes as a container orchestration platform for microservices
B) It provides a cloud-hosted CI/CD pipeline for deploying applications to production
C) It solves the "tool sprawl" problem — developers have 10+ disconnected tools (GitHub, Jira, Grafana, PagerDuty) with no unified interface, causing cognitive overload and slow onboarding
D) It is a database management system that stores service configurations and secrets
✅ Correct Answer: C

Backstage's primary purpose is to solve the "tool sprawl" problem. In modern engineering orgs, developers need 10+ different tools daily — GitHub (code), Jira (tickets), Jenkins/GitHub Actions (CI/CD), Grafana (metrics), PagerDuty (alerts), Confluence (docs), and more. Each tool has its own URL, login, and UI. Backstage unifies ALL of these behind a single Developer Portal — one URL, one login, everything connected.

📝 Detailed Explanation
The "Tool Sprawl" Problem — Before vs After Backstage
  🔴 BEFORE BACKSTAGE — 10+ Disconnected Tools
  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐
  │ GitHub   │ │ Jira     │ │ Jenkins  │ │ Grafana  │ │ Confluence│
  │ (code)   │ │ (tickets)│ │ (CI/CD)  │ │(metrics) │ │ (docs)   │
  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘
  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐
  │ Docker   │ │ Slack    │ │PagerDuty │ │ AWS      │
  │ (images) │ │ (chat)   │ │(alerts)  │ │(infra)   │
  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘
  ❌ 10+ tabs, 10+ logins, ZERO integration, cognitive overload

  🟢 AFTER BACKSTAGE — ONE Unified Developer Portal
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              🏠 backstage.anihpj.com                  │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌─────────┐ │
  │  │🔍Catalog │ │📝Templates│ │📖TechDocs│ │🔌Plugins│ │
  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └─────────┘ │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  ✅ ONE URL, ONE login, EVERYTHING connected

For anihpj/jobpost: Before Backstage, the dev team had to open GitHub for code, Jira for tickets, Grafana for dashboards, PagerDuty for alerts, and Confluence for docs — all separate. After Backstage, they open backstage.anihpj.com and see EVERYTHING for jobpost-api on ONE page. Spotify created Backstage internally because its 1,000+ engineers couldn't find each other's services. They donated it to CNCF in 2020 — it's now an Incubating project used by Netflix, American Airlines, Zalando, and 500+ other companies.

Dev Workflow Q2 of 200
What is an Internal Developer Portal (IDP)?
A) A unified interface that connects all your infrastructure, tooling, and documentation into one cohesive developer experience — a "single pane of glass" for your engineering organization
B) A cloud-based IDE (Integrated Development Environment) where developers write and compile code inside a browser
C) A security firewall that controls which developers can access production Kubernetes clusters
D) A centralized logging platform that aggregates application logs from all microservices
✅ Correct Answer: A

An Internal Developer Portal (IDP) is not a single tool — it's a unified platform that sits ABOVE all your existing tools. Think of it like a shopping mall directory: the stores (GitHub, Jira, Grafana, PagerDuty) already exist, but without a directory, you're wandering around trying to find them. Backstage IS that directory — it doesn't replace your tools, it connects them.

📝 Detailed Explanation
IDP Concept — Backstage Sits ABOVE Your Tools
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │              🏠 INTERNAL DEVELOPER PORTAL (Backstage)        │
  │              backstage.anihpj.com                            │
  │                                                             │
  │   ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐ ┌─────────┐         │
  │   │🔍Catalog│ │📝Template│ │📖TechDocs│ │🔌Plugins│         │
  │   └────┬────┘ └────┬────┘ └────┬────┘ └────┬────┘         │
  │        │           │          │           │                │
  └────────┼───────────┼──────────┼───────────┼────────────────┘
           │           │          │           │
           ▼           ▼          ▼           ▼
     ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌──────────┐
     │ GitHub │ │ ArgoCD │ │ Grafana│ │PagerDuty │
     │ (code) │ │(deploy)│ │(metric)│ │ (alerts) │
     └────────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘ └──────────┘
  
  ✅ IDP connects existing tools — does NOT replace them
  ✅ One URL, one login, unified search across ALL tools

For anihpj/jobpost: The IDP at backstage.anihpj.com shows the jobpost-api Component page. From ONE page, a developer sees: GitHub repo link, CI/CD pipeline status from GitHub Actions, Grafana dashboard showing request latency, PagerDuty on-call schedule, Sentry error counts, and TechDocs with API documentation. All connected, no tab switching.

Dev Workflow Q3 of 200
Who created Backstage and when was it donated to the CNCF?
A) Google created Backstage in 2022 as part of their Anthos platform
B) Spotify created Backstage internally to manage 1,000+ engineers and 10,000+ components, then donated it to CNCF in 2020
C) Microsoft created Backstage as an extension of Azure DevOps in 2019
D) Amazon created Backstage to replace the AWS Management Console in 2021
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage was born inside Spotify around 2016-2017. By 2020, Spotify's engineering org had grown to 1,000+ engineers managing 10,000+ software components. Finding a service's owner, documentation, or deployment status meant asking around in Slack or digging through wikis — a nightmare at scale. Spotify built Backstage internally to solve this, then open-sourced it and donated it to the CNCF (Cloud Native Computing Foundation) in September 2020.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Backstage Timeline — From Spotify Internal Tool to CNCF
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Backstage Origin & Journey                                  │
  │                                                              │
  │  2016 ── Spotify begins internal development                 │
  │         • "Where's the docs for service X?"                  │
  │         • "Who owns this component?"                         │
  │         • 1,000+ engineers, no unified portal                │
  │                                                              │
  │  2020 ── Open-sourced & donated to CNCF (September)          │
  │         • Apache 2.0 license                                 │
  │         • Spotify continues as primary maintainer            │
  │                                                              │
  │  2022 ── CNCF Incubating status                              │
  │         • 500+ adopters (Netflix, Zalando, Expedia...)       │
  │         • 200+ community plugins                             │
  │                                                              │
  │  2024 ── CBA (Certified Backstage Associate) exam launches   │
  │         • Entry-level CNCF certification                     │
  │         • This guide prepares you for it! 🎯                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Backstage portal running at backstage.anihpj.com is powered by the same open-source CNCF project that Spotify created internally and still uses to manage 10,000+ components. Spotify continues as a primary maintainer alongside 500+ contributing companies. Key takeaway: Backstage is NOT a commercial product — it's an open-source CNCF project with a community-driven ecosystem.

Dev Workflow Q4 of 200
What is Backstage's current status within the CNCF (Cloud Native Computing Foundation)?
A) Sandbox — the earliest entry-level stage for new CNCF projects
B) Graduated — the highest maturity level (like Kubernetes, Prometheus, and Envoy)
C) Incubating — a growing project with proven adoption, moving toward Graduated status
D) Archived — a project that is no longer actively maintained
✅ Correct Answer: C

Backstage is currently a CNCF Incubating project. The CNCF has three maturity levels: Sandbox (early-stage, experimental) → Incubating (growing, proven adoption, increasing community) → Graduated (mature, widely adopted, strong governance). Backstage reached Incubating status in 2022, demonstrating significant adoption with 500+ organizations using it in production.

  │  • Experimental       • Security audits    • Widely adopted │
  │  • 2+ maintainers     • 500+ adopters      • Strong gov.    │

  │    - KCL              │  - Backstage ✅     │  - Kubernetes  │
  │    - KubeVirt         │  - Argo            │  - Prometheus  │
  │    - Backstage (2020) │  - Cilium          │  - Envoy       │

  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Backstage's Incubating status means your developer portal at backstage.anihpj.com runs on a CNCF-vetted platform — it has passed security audits, is used in production by 500+ organizations, and has an active community. It's not an experimental side project. The CBA exam tests your understanding of Backstage's place in the CNCF ecosystem, so remember: Incubating means proven in production, maturing toward Graduated.

Dev Workflow Q5 of 200
Under what open-source license is Backstage released?
A) Apache 2.0 — a permissive license that allows commercial use, modification, and distribution with patent protection
B) GPL v3 — a copyleft license requiring all derivative works to also be open-sourced under the same license
C) MIT — a very short permissive license with no patent grant
D) Business Source License (BSL) — free for non-production use, requires a paid license for production
✅ Correct Answer: A

Backstage is released under the Apache 2.0 license. This is significant because Apache 2.0 is a permissive license that explicitly grants patent rights — meaning you can use, modify, and distribute Backstage freely, even in commercial products, without fear of patent litigation. This is the same license used by Kubernetes, Prometheus, and most CNCF projects.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Open Source Licenses — What They Mean for You
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LICENSE COMPARISON                                          │
  │                                                              │
  │  📜 Apache 2.0 ← Backstage uses this ✅                      │
  │     • Permissive — use freely in commercial products         │
  │     • Patent grant — protects you from patent lawsuits       │
  │     • Must include NOTICE file with attribution              │
  │     • Used by: Kubernetes, Backstage, Prometheus, Argo       │
  │                                                              │
  │  📜 MIT                                                      │
  │     • Very short, permissive                                 │
  │     • NO explicit patent grant (unlike Apache 2.0)           │
  │     • Used by: React, Node.js, .NET Core                     │
  │                                                              │
  │  📜 GPL v3                                                   │
  │     • Copyleft — derivative works MUST also be open-source   │
  │     • Restrictive for commercial embedding                   │
  │     • Used by: Linux kernel, Ansible, Git                    │
  │                                                              │
  │  📜 BSL (Business Source License)                            │
  │     • Free for non-production, paid for production           │
  │     • NOT open-source by OSI definition                      │
  │     • Used by: CockroachDB, Terraform (pre-2023)             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Because Backstage is Apache 2.0 licensed, you can freely deploy it at backstage.anihpj.com for your entire engineering organization — no license fees, no usage limits, no vendor lock-in. You can modify the React frontend, build custom plugins, and even resell your Backstage setup as a service. The Apache 2.0 license is one reason why Backstage has been adopted by 500+ companies.

Dev Workflow Q6 of 200
What are the FOUR core pillars of Backstage?
A) Dashboard, Logging, Monitoring, and Alerting — like a traditional observability platform
B) Software Catalog, Software Templates, TechDocs, and Plugins — each addressing a different developer need
C) Authentication, Authorization, Encryption, and Auditing — security-focused components
D) Build, Test, Deploy, and Monitor — a CI/CD pipeline platform
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage is built around four core pillars: Software Catalog (model every service, API, and resource as entities), Software Templates (golden paths to create new services with one click), TechDocs (documentation that lives WITH your code, rendered in the portal), and Plugins (connect any tool — GitHub, Grafana, PagerDuty, Kubernetes — to your developer portal).

For anihpj/jobpost: The Catalog models jobpost-api, jobpost-web, postgres-db, and redis-cache as entities. Templates let developers create a "New Django Service" with one click — repo, CI/CD, K8s manifests, and catalog entry all generated. TechDocs renders API docs from the repo's docs/ folder using MkDocs. Plugins connect GitHub Actions (CI/CD status), Grafana (latency dashboards), and PagerDuty (on-call schedules) — all visible from the jobpost-api entity page.

Dev Workflow Q7 of 200
How does Backstage reduce "cognitive load" on developers?
A) By providing a single unified interface where developers find every service, its docs, CI/CD status, and metrics — eliminating the need to switch between 10+ separate tools and their different UIs
B) By automatically writing boilerplate code so developers don't need to think about configuration
C) By replacing all external tools (GitHub, Jira, Grafana) with Backstage's own built-in alternatives
D) By using machine learning to predict which service a developer will need next
✅ Correct Answer: A

Cognitive load is the mental effort required to juggle multiple tools, contexts, and logins. Before Backstage, answering "Is jobpost-api healthy?" meant opening GitHub (code), Jenkins (CI/CD), Grafana (metrics), PagerDuty (alerts), and Confluence (docs) — 5+ tabs, 5+ different UIs, 5+ mental context switches. Backstage eliminates this by surfacing everything on ONE entity page with ONE consistent UI.

  Tab 4: PagerDuty  → Check active alerts        [UI: incident list]
  Tab 5: Confluence → Find runbook               [UI: wiki page]
  
  ⏱️  15 min | 🧠 5 mental models | 🔐 5 logins

  ONE page: backstage.anihpj.com/catalog/default/component/jobpost-api

For anihpj/jobpost: The jobpost-api entity page shows GitHub commits, CI/CD status, Grafana latency, PagerDuty alerts, and API documentation on ONE page. New team members go from "I don't know where anything is" to "I can see everything" in under 60 seconds. This is why onboarding drops from 2 weeks to 2 hours.

Dev Workflow Q8 of 200
What is "tool sprawl" and how does Backstage address it?
A) Tool sprawl is when too many developers work on the same codebase — Backstage solves this with code ownership features
B) Tool sprawl is the proliferation of 10-15+ disconnected developer tools (GitHub, Jira, Jenkins, Grafana, PagerDuty, Confluence, Slack...) — each with its own URL, login, and UI. Backstage addresses it by becoming a unified portal that connects all these tools behind ONE interface
C) Tool sprawl is when Kubernetes clusters grow too large — Backstage solves this by replacing kubectl with a GUI
D) Tool sprawl is when too many plugins are installed in Backstage — it's solved by disabling unused plugins
✅ Correct Answer: B

Tool sprawl is the #1 problem Backstage was created to solve. In a typical engineering org, developers interact with GitHub (code), Jira (tickets), Jenkins/GitHub Actions (CI/CD), Grafana (monitoring), PagerDuty (alerts), Confluence (docs), Slack (communication), Docker Hub (images), and AWS Console (infra). That's 10+ separate tools — each a different website, different login, different UI paradigm. Backstage doesn't REPLACE these tools — it CONNECTS them via plugins, so developers access everything from ONE portal.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Tool Sprawl — How Backstage Connects Without Replacing
  🔴 TOOL SPRAWL — 10+ Disconnected Islands
  ─────────────────────────────────────────
  ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐
  │ GitHub │ │  Jira  │ │Jenkins │ │Grafana │ │Conf.   │
  │ code   │ │tickets │ │ CI/CD  │ │metrics │ │ docs   │
  └────────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘
  ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐ ┌────────┐
  │ Docker │ │ Slack  │ │PagerDt │ │  AWS   │
  │ images │ │ chat   │ │ alerts │ │ infra  │
  └────────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘ └────────┘
  ❌ Each tool = separate URL, login, UI, mental model

  🟢 BACKSTAGE SOLUTION — Unified via Plugins
  ──────────────────────────────────────────
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │         🏠 backstage.anihpj.com               │
  │                                              │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐     │
  │  │🔍Catalog │ │📝Template│ │📖TechDocs│     │
  │  └──────────┘ └──────────┘ └──────────┘     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │        🔌 PLUGINS (connect tools)    │    │
  │  │  GitHub  Jira  Jenkins  Grafana      │    │
  │  │  PagerDuty  Slack  Docker  AWS       │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────┘
  ✅ ONE URL, ONE login — plugins connect, not replace

For anihpj/jobpost: Before Backstage, the team had 10+ separate tools. After Backstage, backstage.anihpj.com shows GitHub repos, Jira tickets, CI/CD pipelines, Grafana dashboards, PagerDuty alerts, and Confluence docs — all connected via plugins. The tools haven't changed, but accessing them now takes ONE click instead of 10.

Dev Workflow Q9 of 200
What is the "single pane of glass" concept in Backstage?
A) A monitoring dashboard showing real-time CPU and memory usage across all clusters
B) The app-config.yaml file that consolidates all Backstage settings in one place

✅ Correct Answer: C

The "single pane of glass" is the core design philosophy of Backstage. Instead of looking through 10 different windows (tools) to understand your engineering org, Backstage gives you ONE window where everything is visible and connected. It doesn't replace your tools — it gives you ONE place to see them all.

1.2 Backstage vs Competitors — IDP LandscapeQ11-Q16

Dev Workflow Q11 of 200
How does Backstage compare to Port as an Internal Developer Portal?
A) Port is more customizable than Backstage because it's also open source under Apache 2.0
B) Backstage is fully open source (Apache 2.0, CNCF Incubating) with 200+ community plugins, while Port is a proprietary commercial product. Both offer self-hosting, but only Backstage has built-in TechDocs and Software Templates
C) Backstage and Port are identical — Port is just a hosted version of Backstage managed by Spotify
D) Port has a larger plugin ecosystem than Backstage and is the only CNCF-incubated IDP
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage (Spotify, CNCF Incubating, Apache 2.0) and Port (commercial, proprietary) are the two most popular IDPs. Key differences: Backstage is open source with 200+ community plugins, built-in TechDocs, and Software Templates. Port is a commercial SaaS product (with a self-hosted option) that focuses on service catalog and scorecards but lacks TechDocs and Templates. Port is easier to set up initially but less customizable at the deepest level.

  │  │ 📖 TechDocs Built-in  │          │ ❌ No TechDocs     │  │
  │  │ 📝 Software Templates │          │ ⚡ Rapid SaaS Setup │  │
  │  │ 🖥️ Self-Hosted First  │          │ ☁️ SaaS + Self-Host│  │

  │  └────────────────────────┘          └────────────────────┘  │
  │                                                              │
  │  Choose Backstage for:         Choose Port for:              │
  │  • Unlimited customization     • Rapid setup (hours)         │
  │  • Zero licensing costs        • Managed infrastructure     │
  │  • Built-in docs + templates   • Smaller teams              │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Backstage was chosen because the team needs deep customization (custom Django service templates, custom Grafana dashboards, custom plugins for internal tools) and wants zero licensing costs. Port would be a good alternative if the team prioritized rapid SaaS setup over deep customization.

Dev Workflow Q12 of 200
How does Backstage compare to Cortex as an Internal Developer Portal?
A) Cortex is a SaaS-only service catalog focused on service quality scorecards and team ownership. Backstage is broader — an open-source IDP with catalog, templates, TechDocs, and a plugin ecosystem. Cortex lacks Software Templates and TechDocs entirely
B) Cortex is the open-source version of Backstage maintained by the CNCF
C) Cortex is better than Backstage because it has more plugins and supports self-hosting
D) Cortex and Backstage are functionally identical — just different brand names
✅ Correct Answer: A

Cortex is a SaaS-only platform focused on service quality scorecards — measuring engineering maturity, tracking SLAs, and managing team ownership. Unlike Backstage, Cortex does not offer Software Templates (golden paths to create new services), TechDocs (docs that live with code), or a plugin ecosystem. Cortex is best for teams that want a managed SaaS experience focused on service quality metrics, while Backstage is for orgs that need a fully customizable, open-source developer portal.

Dev Workflow Q14 of 200
What is Backstage's UNIQUE advantage over ALL its competitors (Port, Cortex, OpsLevel)?

(3) Software Templates — the Scaffolder engine that creates new services with one click, encoding best practices into golden paths. Port, Cortex, and OpsLevel each miss at least two of these three.

Commercial IDPs like Port and OpsLevel excel at rapid time-to-value for smaller teams. You sign up, connect your tools, and have a working portal in hours — no infrastructure to manage, no upgrades to handle, no plugin development needed. The trade-off: you lose the unlimited customization, TechDocs, Software Templates, and vendor-neutral Apache 2.0 licensing that Backstage offers. Choose commercial when ease-of-setup beats customization-needs.

Dev Workflow Q16 of 200
Can you self-host Backstage? What about Port, Cortex, and OpsLevel?

B) Only Cortex and OpsLevel support self-hosting for enterprise customers

1.3 Backstage Architecture Deep DiveQ17-Q30

Dev Workflow Q17 of 200
What programming language and framework is Backstage's FRONTEND built with?
A) Python with Django templates for server-side rendering
B) TypeScript with React and Material-UI (MUI) v5 — a typed, component-based framework for building interactive UIs
C) JavaScript with Vue.js and Vuetify for the component library
D) Go with HTMX for lightweight server-rendered pages
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage's frontend uses TypeScript + React + Material-UI v5. React provides the component-based UI architecture, TypeScript adds type safety at compile time, and Material-UI (MUI) delivers Google's Material Design components — buttons, cards, tables, navigation — giving Backstage its polished, professional look. This stack was chosen by Spotify for its scalability, large ecosystem, and proven track record in complex web applications.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Backstage Frontend Tech Stack
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BACKSTAGE FRONTEND STACK                                    │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  ⚛️ REACT — Component-based UI architecture           │    │
  │  │  • Reusable components (EntityPage, CatalogTable...)  │    │
  │  │  • Virtual DOM for efficient rendering               │    │
  │  │  • Hooks for state management (useState, useEffect)  │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  🔷 TYPESCRIPT — Type-safe JavaScript                 │    │
  │  │  • Catches bugs at compile time (not runtime)        │    │
  │  │  • Interfaces for entities, plugins, and APIs        │    │
  │  │  • tsconfig.json with "strict": true                 │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │

  │  │  • Buttons, Cards, Tables, Dialogs, Navigation       │    │

  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When the platform team customizes the Backstage portal at backstage.anihpj.com, they write React components in TypeScript and use MUI's theming to match anihpj's brand colors. Every plugin installed adds new React components that render on entity pages.

Dev Workflow Q18 of 200
What programming language and framework is Backstage's BACKEND built with?
A) Python with Django REST Framework for building REST APIs
B) Java with Spring Boot for enterprise-grade microservices
C) Ruby on Rails for rapid API development and convention-over-configuration
D) TypeScript with Node.js and Express.js — the backend runs on the same language as the frontend, enabling code sharing and a unified development experience
✅ Correct Answer: D

Backstage's backend runs on Node.js + Express.js + TypeScript. This is intentional — using the SAME language (TypeScript) for both frontend and backend means developers can work across the full stack without context switching. Express.js provides the HTTP framework for REST APIs, and TypeScript ensures type safety across the entire codebase. The backend handles catalog processing, scaffolder execution, authentication, and plugin backends.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Backstage Backend — Node.js + Express + TypeScript
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BACKSTAGE BACKEND (Node.js + Express)                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  📡 HTTP Server (Express.js)                                 │
  │  ├── GET  /api/catalog/entities      → Catalog backend       │
  │  ├── POST /api/scaffolder/v2/tasks   → Scaffolder backend    │
  │  ├── GET  /api/techdocs/*            → TechDocs backend      │
  │  ├── POST /api/auth/*                → Auth backend          │
  │  └── GET  /api/search/query          → Search backend        │
  │                                                              │
  │  🗄️ Database Layer (Knex.js)                                 │
  │  ├── PostgreSQL (production)                                  │
  │  └── SQLite (development)                                     │
  │                                                              │
  │  🔌 Plugin Backends                                           │
  │  ├── Catalog Backend      → Entity processing                │
  │  ├── Scaffolder Backend   → Template execution               │
  │  ├── TechDocs Backend     → MkDocs build & publish           │
  │  ├── Auth Backend         → OAuth/OIDC/SAML providers        │
  │  ├── Search Backend       → Elasticsearch / PostgreSQL       │
  │  └── Proxy Backend        → Secured API forwarding           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The backend at backstage.anihpj.com handles catalog entity processing for jobpost-api and postgres-db, executes Django service templates via the Scaffolder, builds TechDocs from the docs/ folders, and proxies requests to internal tools like Grafana.

Dev Workflow Q19 of 200
What database abstraction layer does Backstage use?
A) Knex.js — a SQL query builder for TypeScript/JavaScript that supports PostgreSQL and SQLite. It handles migrations, schema building, and queries across multiple database engines
B) Django ORM — Python's object-relational mapper that maps Python classes to database tables
C) MongoDB native driver — Backstage uses a NoSQL document database for flexible schemas
D) Prisma ORM — a type-safe ORM for TypeScript that generates database clients
✅ Correct Answer: A

Backstage uses Knex.js as its database abstraction layer. Knex is a SQL query builder (not a full ORM) that provides a unified API for PostgreSQL and SQLite. Each plugin manages its own database schema through Knex migrations — when Backstage starts, it runs all pending migrations automatically. This allows plugins to add tables without conflicting with each other.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Knex.js in Backstage — Database Layer
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  KNEX.JS — Backstage's Database Abstraction                  │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  Backstage Plugins (each with own tables)             │    │
  │  │  Catalog → entities, locations, relations            │    │
  │  │  Scaffolder → tasks, task_events                     │    │
  │  │  Auth → user_credentials, sessions                   │    │
  │  │  Search → search_indices                             │    │
  │  └──────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                         │                                    │
  │                         ▼                                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │             KNEX.JS (SQL Query Builder)               │    │
  │  │  • knex migrate:latest → runs all pending migrations  │    │
  │  │  • knex('entities').select('*') → type-safe queries   │    │
  │  │  • Same API for PostgreSQL AND SQLite                 │    │
  │  └──────────────────────┬───────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                         │                                    │
  │           ┌─────────────┴─────────────┐                      │
  │           ▼                           ▼                      │
  │  ┌──────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────┐            │
  │  │ PostgreSQL (prod)│    │ SQLite (dev)         │            │
  │  │ Full-featured    │    │ File-based, no setup │            │
  │  │ Production-grade │    │ Great for local dev  │            │
  │  └──────────────────┘    └──────────────────────┘            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Backstage instance uses PostgreSQL on the kubeadm cluster (StatefulSet with PVC). Knex handles all database operations for catalog entities (jobpost-api, postgres-db), scaffolder tasks (when creating new Django services), and user sessions. During local development, SQLite is used for simplicity.

Dev Workflow Q20 of 200
What database does Backstage use in production vs development?
A) MongoDB in production, SQLite in development — Backstage is NoSQL-first
B) PostgreSQL in production, SQLite in development. PostgreSQL provides full ACID compliance, concurrent access, and production reliability. SQLite is file-based with zero setup — perfect for local dev
C) MySQL in production, SQLite in development — Backstage requires MySQL 8.0+
D) The same PostgreSQL database for both — there's no lightweight development option
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage defaults to SQLite for development (stored as a local file in the project directory — zero setup, just run yarn dev) and PostgreSQL for production (configured in app-config.production.yaml with connection string). PostgreSQL provides ACID transactions, concurrent connections, replication, and production-grade reliability. SQLite is perfectly fine for single-user local development but cannot handle concurrent connections in production.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Database — Development vs Production
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DEVELOPMENT (yarn dev)                                      │
  │  ─────────────────                                           │
  │  Database: SQLite (file-based, zero config)                  │
  │  Location: ./my-backstage-app/backstage.db                   │
  │  Setup:    Automatic — no installation needed                │
  │  Pros:     Instant start, no Docker, no credentials          │
  │  Cons:     Single-user, no replication, not for prod         │
  │                                                              │
  │  PRODUCTION (yarn build + yarn start)                        │
  │  ────────────────────────────────────                        │
  │  Database: PostgreSQL (relational, ACID-compliant)           │
  │  Connection: postgresql://user:pass@host:5432/backstage      │
  │  Config:    app-config.production.yaml                       │
  │  Pros:     Multi-user, replication, backups, production      │
  │  Cons:     Requires PostgreSQL installation and setup        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: In production on the kubeadm cluster, Backstage connects to a PostgreSQL StatefulSet in the same Kubernetes namespace. In local development, developers use SQLite for instant setup when running yarn dev on their laptops.

app-config.yaml is the single source of configuration truth for Backstage. Dev Workflow Q22 of 200
How does Backstage handle environment-specific configuration?
A) app-config.yaml provides base config, app-config.production.yaml (or .development.yaml) layers environment-specific overrides on top, and environment variables (APP_CONFIG_ prefix) take the highest precedence
B) You create a completely separate app-config.yaml for each environment with no inheritance between them
C) All configuration is hardcoded in TypeScript files — there is no environment-specific config
D) Backstage uses a web UI admin panel to change configuration at runtime per environment
✅ Correct Answer: A

Backstage uses a layered configuration system: (1) app-config.yaml — base configuration shared across all environments. (2) app-config.production.yaml or app-config.development.yaml — environment-specific overrides merged on top. (3) Environment variables with APP_CONFIG_ prefix — highest precedence, perfect for secrets (GitHub tokens, database passwords). This layering means you don't repeat yourself — base config is shared, overrides are minimal.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Configuration Layering — From Lowest to Highest Priority
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CONFIGURATION PRIORITY (Highest wins)                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  🥇 ENVIRONMENT VARIABLES (Highest Priority)                 │
  │     APP_CONFIG_backend_database_connection                   │
  │     = postgresql://prod-host/backstage                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  🥈 app-config.production.yaml                               │
  │     backend:                                                 │
  │       database:                                              │
  │         client: pg                                           │
  │                                                              │
  │  🥉 app-config.yaml (Base — always loaded)                   │
  │     app:                                                     │
  │       title: Anihpj Portal                                   │
  │       baseUrl: http://localhost:3000                         │
  │                                                              │
  │  app-config.local.yaml (gitignored, local overrides only)    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The base app-config.yaml defines the app title and structure. app-config.production.yaml overrides the database to PostgreSQL and the baseUrl to backstage.anihpj.com. Environment variables inject the GitHub token and DB password — these are NEVER committed to Git.

Dev Workflow Q23 of 200
What is the monorepo structure of a Backstage project?
A) One single folder containing all frontend and backend code mixed together with no separation
B) Separate Git repositories for frontend, backend, and each plugin — managed as independent microservices
C) A traditional three-tier structure with frontend/, backend/, and database/ directories
D) A monorepo with packages/app (frontend/React), packages/backend (backend/Express), and plugins/ (custom plugins). Managed by Yarn workspaces or Lerna, enabling shared TypeScript types and dependencies across all packages
✅ Correct Answer: D

Backstage uses a monorepo structure managed by Yarn workspaces: packages/app/ contains the React frontend (App.tsx, components, routing), packages/backend/ contains the Express backend (index.ts, plugin backends, database), and plugins/ is where custom plugins live. This structure enables code sharing — frontend and backend can share TypeScript types, utilities, and configuration.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Backstage Monorepo Structure
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  my-backstage-app/                    ← Root                 │
  │  ├── app-config.yaml                  ← Configuration        │
  │  ├── catalog-info.yaml                ← Self-registration    │
  │  ├── package.json                     ← Workspaces root      │
  │  │                                                          │
  │  ├── packages/                        ← Monorepo packages    │
  │  │   ├── app/                         ← FRONTEND (React)     │
  │  │   │   └── src/                     │                      │
  │  │   │       ├── App.tsx              ← Main component       │
  │  │   │       ├── plugins.ts           ← Plugin registration  │
  │  │   │       └── apis.ts             ← API clients          │
  │  │   │                                                      │
  │  │   └── backend/                     ← BACKEND (Express)    │
  │  │       └── src/                     │                      │
  │  │           ├── index.ts             ← Entry point          │
  │  │           └── plugins/             ← Backend plugins      │
  │  │                                                          │
  │  └── plugins/                         ← CUSTOM PLUGINS       │
  │      └── my-custom-plugin/            ← Your plugin          │
  │          └── src/                                            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team creates custom plugins in plugins/anihpj-theme/ (branding) and plugins/anihpj-templates/ (Django service templates). These share TypeScript types with the main app and backend through the monorepo structure.

Dev Workflow Q24 of 200
What is the Plugin API and what are its two halves?
A) The Plugin API is a REST interface that plugins use to communicate — it has GET and POST halves
B) The Plugin API has two halves: createPlugin() for frontend plugins (React components mounted in the UI) and createBackendPlugin() for backend plugins (Express routers handling API requests). Plugins can have BOTH halves — a UI and a backend service
C) The Plugin API consists of install() and uninstall() methods for adding plugins to Backstage
D) The Plugin API is a single function that handles both frontend rendering and backend logic in one file
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage plugins have two halves: Frontend plugins (created with createPlugin()) are React components that render in the UI — they provide entity page tabs, sidebar items, and cards. Backend plugins (created with createBackendPlugin()) are Express.js routers that handle API requests, database operations, and external service integration. A plugin like the Grafana plugin has BOTH: a frontend that embeds dashboards and a backend that proxies requests to the Grafana API.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Plugin API — Two Halves, One Plugin
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PLUGIN ANATOMY — Frontend + Backend Halves                  │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐    ┌──────────────────────┐        │
  │  │ FRONTEND PLUGIN      │    │ BACKEND PLUGIN       │        │
  │  │ createPlugin()       │    │ createBackendPlugin()│        │
  │  │                      │    │                      │        │
  │  │ • React components   │    │ • Express routers    │        │
  │  │ • Entity page tabs   │    │ • REST API endpoints │        │
  │  │ • Sidebar items      │    │ • Database access    │        │
  │  │ • Cards & widgets    │    │ • External API calls │        │
  │  │ • Routes             │    │ • Background tasks   │        │
  │  └──────────┬───────────┘    └──────────┬───────────┘        │
  │             │                           │                     │
  │             └───────────┬───────────────┘                     │
  │                         │                                     │
  │                         ▼                                     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  A plugin can have:                                  │    │
  │  │  • Only frontend (e.g., Tech Radar visualization)    │    │
  │  │  • Only backend (e.g., catalog ingestion processor)  │    │
  │  │  • BOTH frontend + backend (e.g., Grafana plugin)    │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Grafana plugin has a frontend half (renders dashboard embeds on entity pages) and a backend half (proxies requests to Grafana's API securely). The catalog plugin has both halves too — frontend renders the catalog UI, backend processes entity ingestion.

Dev Workflow Q25 of 200
How does Backstage's frontend routing work?
A) React Router-based — each plugin registers routes via createRoutableExtension(). The app shell mounts extensions at specific routes (/catalog, /create, /docs). This enables lazy loading — plugin code loads ONLY when the user navigates to that route
B) Server-side routing — the backend determines which page to render based on the URL and sends pre-rendered HTML
C) File-based routing like Next.js — each file in the pages/ directory automatically becomes a route
D) Hash-based routing with # fragments — all navigation uses URL hashes like /#/catalog
✅ Correct Answer: A

Backstage uses React Router with a plugin extension system. Each plugin defines routes using createRoutableExtension() — this creates a mountable React component that the app shell places at a specific route. The key benefit is lazy loading: plugin code is only downloaded when the user actually navigates to that route, keeping initial page load fast. The sidebar navigation items link to these routes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Frontend Routing — Plugin Extensions at Routes
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BACKSTAGE ROUTING SYSTEM                                    │
  │                                                              │
  │  App.tsx (Shell)                                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  /                    → Home Page (Root)              │    │
  │  │  /catalog             → Catalog Plugin (lazy loaded)  │    │
  │  │  /catalog/:namespace/ │                                │    │
  │  │    :kind/:name        → Entity Page (lazy loaded)     │    │
  │  │  /create              → Scaffolder (lazy loaded)      │    │
  │  │  /create/actions      → Template List (lazy loaded)   │    │
  │  │  /docs                → TechDocs Home (lazy loaded)   │    │
  │  │  /docs/:namespace/    │                                │    │
  │  │    :kind/:name        → Entity Docs (lazy loaded)     │    │
  │  │  /settings            → Settings (lazy loaded)        │    │
  │  │  /my-custom-plugin    → Custom Plugin (lazy loaded)   │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                              │
  │  ⚡ Lazy Loading: Plugin code downloads ONLY when needed      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When a developer navigates to /catalog/default/component/jobpost-api, only the catalog plugin code is loaded — not the scaffolder or TechDocs plugins. This keeps the portal fast even with dozens of plugins installed.

Dev Workflow Q27 of 200
How does Backstage's backend handle plugin registration?
A) Plugins are manually listed in a JSON file and loaded alphabetically at startup
B) Each plugin runs as a separate Docker container and registers via a service mesh
C) Plugins register through a central registry API at plugin.backstage.io
D) Plugins are loaded via createBackend() with feature detection. Each plugin exports a function that receives env (logger, config, database, router) and sets up its routes, database migrations, and scheduled tasks. The framework handles startup/shutdown lifecycle
✅ Correct Answer: D

Backstage's backend uses createBackend() for plugin discovery. Each backend plugin exports a function that receives an environment object (env) containing: logger, config reader, database client, HTTP router, and more. The plugin registers its routes, migrations, and scheduled tasks through this environment. The framework handles startup (calling each plugin's registration) and graceful shutdown automatically.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Backend Plugin Registration Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BACKEND STARTUP — Plugin Registration                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  createBackend()                                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  1. Discover all plugins in packages/backend/src/     │    │
  │  │     plugins/ directory                                │    │
  │  │                                                      │    │
  │  │  2. Call each plugin's register(env) function         │    │
  │  │     env = {                                           │    │
  │  │       logger:   Winston logger instance               │    │
  │  │       config:   Config reader (app-config.yaml)       │    │
  │  │       database: PluginDatabaseManager (Knex)          │    │
  │  │       router:   Express router for HTTP routes        │    │
  │  │       scheduler: Task scheduler for cron jobs         │    │
  │  │     }                                                │    │
  │  │                                                      │    │
  │  │  3. Run all pending Knex migrations                   │    │
  │  │                                                      │    │
  │  │  4. Start HTTP server on port 7007                    │    │
  │  │                                                      │    │
  │  │  5. On shutdown: gracefully stop all plugins          │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When the Backstage backend starts, it loads the catalog backend (entity processing), scaffolder backend (template execution), auth backend (GitHub OAuth), and TechDocs backend (MkDocs build). Each plugin receives its database namespace and registers its routes at /api/<plugin-id>/.

Dev Workflow Q28 of 200
What is the Backstage Scaffolder?
A) A visual drag-and-drop editor for creating Kubernetes YAML manifests
B) A CI/CD pipeline that builds and deploys Backstage itself to Kubernetes
C) A template execution engine that fetches a skeleton, runs a series of actions, and publishes the result — enabling one-click creation of new services with best practices baked in
D) A database migration tool that scaffolds new database tables for Backstage plugins
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Scaffolder is Backstage's "golden path" engine. It takes a Software Template (YAML definition), collects user inputs (service name, port, team...), executes a series of actions (fetch skeleton, substitute parameters, create GitHub repo, register in catalog, set up CI/CD), and outputs a fully functional new service. It eliminates the manual, error-prone bootstrapping that developers hate.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Scaffolder — From Template to Production
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SCAFFOLDER FLOW                                             │
  │                                                              │
  │  ① INPUT: Developer fills form in Backstage UI               │
  │     "Service Name: my-service, Port: 8000, DB: yes"          │
  │                                                              │
  │  ② FETCH: Scaffolder clones template skeleton from Git       │
  │     fetch:template → url: https://github.com/anihpj/template │
  │                                                              │
  │  ③ PROCESS: Substitute parameters into template files        │
  │     {{name}} → my-service, {{port}} → 8000                   │
  │                                                              │
  │  ④ PUBLISH: Create new GitHub repo and push code             │
  │     publish:github → anihpj/my-service                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  ⑤ REGISTER: Add new entity to Backstage Catalog             │
  │     catalog:register → catalog-info.yaml                     │
  │                                                              │
  │  ⑥ OUTPUT: Links to new repo, catalog page, CI/CD pipeline   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The "Create Django Service" template uses the Scaffolder to generate a new Django project with correct settings.py, Dockerfile, k8s manifests, and catalog-info.yaml — all in one click. What used to take a developer 4+ hours now takes 2 minutes.

Dev Workflow Q29 of 200
How does Backstage Search work?
A) It uses the browser's built-in Ctrl+F to search the current page content
B) It's a pluggable search platform — each plugin can register collators that feed search indices (Elasticsearch or PostgreSQL). Results appear in the global search bar, returning entities, docs, and plugin-specific content in one unified result list
C) It queries each connected tool's API directly at search time — GitHub, Jira, and Grafana APIs are called in real-time
D) It uses Google Custom Search to index the entire Backstage portal as a public website
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage Search uses a pluggable architecture: each plugin registers collators that extract searchable content (catalog entities, TechDocs pages, scaffolder templates...). This content is indexed into either Elasticsearch (production) or PostgreSQL (development). The global search bar queries this index and returns unified results — a developer searching "jobpost" might see the jobpost-api entity, its API docs, and related templates all in one place.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Backstage Search Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SEARCH ARCHITECTURE                                         │
  │                                                              │
  │  Plugins (Content Sources)       Search Backend              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ Catalog → entities   │──▶    │                      │     │
  │  │ TechDocs → docs      │──▶    │ Elasticsearch (prod) │     │
  │  │ Scaffolder → tmpl    │──▶    │ or PostgreSQL (dev)  │     │
  │  └──────────────────────┘       └──────────┬───────────┘     │
  │                                            │                  │
  │                                            ▼                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  🔍 Global Search Bar (Cmd+K / Ctrl+K)               │    │
  │  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐│    │
  │  │  │ jobpost-api                            [results] ││    │
  │  │  │ ▸ Component: jobpost-api       (Catalog)        ││    │
  │  │  │ ▸ API Documentation            (TechDocs)       ││    │
  │  │  │ ▸ Create Django Service        (Template)       ││    │
  │  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘│    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When a developer presses Cmd+K and types "api", the search returns the jobpost-api entity, its OpenAPI spec in TechDocs, and the "Create Django Service" template — all from one search. The catalog plugin and TechDocs plugin each contribute their content to the search index.

Dev Workflow Q30 of 200
What is the Catalog Processing Loop in Backstage?
A) A continuous cycle: Fetch entity YAML from location → Parse YAML → Validate schema → Enrich with annotations → Stitch (resolve cross-entity references like dependsOn) → Store in database. Runs on a schedule and on-demand
B) A single HTTP request that loads all catalog entities from the database when the page loads
C) A manual process where an admin approves each entity before it appears in the catalog
D) A Kubernetes controller that watches for new pods and automatically creates catalog entities for them
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Catalog Processing Loop is the engine that keeps the Software Catalog up-to-date. It continuously cycles through all configured locations (GitHub repos, static URLs...), fetches catalog-info.yaml files, parses the YAML, validates entity schemas, enriches entities with annotations, stitches relationships (resolving "dependsOn: resource:postgres-db" to the actual postgres-db entity), and stores the final entity in the database. It runs every ~100 seconds by default.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Catalog Processing Loop — 6 Stages
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CATALOG PROCESSING LOOP (runs every ~100 seconds)           │
  │                                                              │
  │  ① FETCH     ② PARSE    ③ VALIDATE  ④ ENRICH   ⑤ STITCH    │
  │  ────────   ────────   ──────────  ────────   ────────     │
  │  GitHub      YAML →     Schema       Add        Resolve     │
  │  URL →       Entity     validation   annotations cross-refs  │
  │  raw YAML    object     checks       backstage   dependsOn   │
  │                                    .io/view-url  ownedBy     │
  │       │         │           │          │           │         │
  │       └─────────┴───────────┴──────────┴───────────┘         │
  │                         │                                    │
  │                         ▼                                    │
  │                  ⑥ STORE → PostgreSQL database               │
  │                                                              │
  │  🔄 Runs on schedule + on-demand (manual refresh in UI)      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When a developer adds catalog-info.yaml to a new GitHub repo, GitHub Discovery finds it on the next scan cycle, the processing loop validates and stitches it (linking jobpost-api → dependsOn → postgres-db), and the entity appears in the catalog at backstage.anihpj.com within a few minutes.

1.4 Installing & Running BackstageQ31-Q40

Dev Workflow Q31 of 200
What command creates a new Backstage application?
A) npm install backstage — installs Backstage as an npm dependency in an existing project
B) npx @backstage/create-app@latest — scaffolds a complete Backstage monorepo with frontend, backend, and plugin directories pre-configured
C) docker pull backstage/backstage — pulls the official Backstage Docker image
D) git clone backstage/backstage — clones the entire Backstage open-source repository
✅ Correct Answer: B

npx @backstage/create-app@latest is the ONE command that scaffolds everything. It creates a complete monorepo with packages/app/ (React frontend), packages/backend/ (Express backend), plugins/ directory, app-config.yaml, catalog-info.yaml, tsconfig.json, and package.json — all pre-configured. After running it, you just cd into the directory and run yarn dev to start.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Creating a Backstage App — What Happens
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  npx @backstage/create-app@latest                            │
  │  ───────────────────────────────                             │
  │                                                              │
  │  ① ASKS: "Enter a name for the app [my-backstage-app]:"     │
  │                                                              │
  │  ② SCAFFOLDS complete monorepo structure:                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  my-backstage-app/                                    │    │
  │  │  ├── packages/app/        ← React frontend           │    │
  │  │  ├── packages/backend/    ← Express backend          │    │
  │  │  ├── plugins/             ← Custom plugins dir       │    │
  │  │  ├── app-config.yaml      ← Configuration            │    │
  │  │  ├── catalog-info.yaml    ← Self-registration        │    │
  │  │  ├── tsconfig.json        ← TypeScript config        │    │
  │  │  └── package.json         ← Workspaces root          │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                              │
  │  ③ INSTALLS dependencies: yarn install                       │
  │  ④ READY: cd my-backstage-app && yarn dev                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The platform team ran npx @backstage/create-app@latest to scaffold the anihpj developer portal, then customized the theme, added the GitHub Discovery provider, and configured PostgreSQL — all starting from this one command.

Dev Workflow Q32 of 200
What command runs Backstage in development mode, and what does it start?
A) yarn dev — starts the React frontend on port 3000 with hot module reload AND the Express backend on port 7007. Changes to source code appear instantly in the browser without manual rebuild
B) yarn start — starts only the production-optimized frontend on port 80
C) npm run serve — serves the built static files on port 3000 without a backend
D) node backend/src/index.ts — starts only the backend server without the frontend
✅ Correct Answer: A

yarn dev is the primary development command. It starts TWO processes: the React frontend on localhost:3000 (with webpack-dev-server and hot module reload) and the Express backend on localhost:7007. When you edit a TypeScript file, the change appears in the browser in under a second — no manual rebuild needed. This is how you develop Backstage day-to-day.
