For anihpj/jobpost: Local development at localhost:3000 uses yarn dev with SQLite (zero config). The platform team edits React components, adds plugins, and tests catalog-info.yaml files — all with instant hot reload.

Dev Workflow Q33 of 200
What is the default project structure after running create-app?
A) A single flat directory with all TypeScript files and configuration mixed together
B) A microservices architecture with separate repositories for frontend, backend, and each plugin
C) A monorepo with packages/app (frontend/React), packages/backend (backend/Express), plugins/ (custom plugins), app-config.yaml, catalog-info.yaml, and package.json at the root — all managed by Yarn workspaces
D) A Docker-based structure where each component runs in its own container from day one
✅ Correct Answer: C

The scaffolded project is a Yarn workspaces monorepo. packages/app/ contains the React frontend with App.tsx, plugins.ts, and apis.ts. packages/backend/ contains the Express backend with index.ts and plugin backends. plugins/ is an empty directory ready for custom plugins. app-config.yaml is the central configuration, and catalog-info.yaml registers Backstage itself in the catalog.

📝 Detailed Explanation
create-app Output — Complete File Tree
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  my-backstage-app/                    ← Root directory       │
  │  ├── app-config.yaml                  ← Main configuration   │
  │  ├── catalog-info.yaml                ← Self-registration    │
  │  ├── package.json                     ← Yarn workspaces root │
  │  ├── tsconfig.json                    ← TypeScript config    │
  │  ├── lerna.json                       ← Monorepo management  │
  │  │                                                          │
  │  ├── packages/                        ← Yarn workspaces      │
  │  │   ├── app/                         ← FRONTEND             │
  │  │   │   └── src/                                            │
  │  │   │       ├── App.tsx              ← Main React component │
  │  │   │       ├── plugins.ts           ← Plugin registration  │
  │  │   │       ├── apis.ts              ← API clients          │
  │  │   │       └── components/          ← Shared UI            │
  │  │   │                                                      │
  │  │   └── backend/                     ← BACKEND              │
  │  │       └── src/                                            │
  │  │           ├── index.ts             ← Entry point          │
  │  │           └── plugins/             ← Plugin backends      │
  │  │                                                          │
  │  └── plugins/                         ← YOUR custom plugins  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team added custom plugins in plugins/anihpj-theme/ and backend modules in packages/backend/src/plugins/, all following this standard structure.

Dev Workflow Q34 of 200
What are the key directories in a Backstage project and what do they contain?
A) /frontend for React, /backend for Express, /config for YAML — three separate top-level folders
B) /src for all code, /dist for compiled output, /node_modules for dependencies — standard Node.js structure
C) /containers for Docker, /kubernetes for K8s manifests, /terraform for infrastructure
D) packages/app/src (App.tsx, plugins.ts, apis.ts for frontend), packages/backend/src (index.ts, plugins/ for backend), plugins/ (custom plugins), app-config.yaml (configuration), catalog-info.yaml (self-registration)
✅ Correct Answer: D

The three most important directories are: packages/app/src/ (where you customize the React UI — App.tsx is the main component, plugins.ts registers all plugins, apis.ts sets up API clients), packages/backend/src/ (where you configure the Express server — index.ts is the entry point, plugins/ directory holds backend plugin registrations), and plugins/ (where you build custom plugins that aren't from the community).

📝 Detailed Explanation

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DIRECTORIES YOU'LL WORK IN DAILY                            │

  │  ─────────────────                                            │
  │  App.tsx        → Add routes, wrap with providers            │
  │  plugins.ts     → Register new plugins from community        │
  │  apis.ts        → Configure API clients and auth             │
  │  components/    → Build custom React components              │
  │                                                              │
  │  packages/backend/src/      ← ✏️ Edit backend daily          │
  │  ───────────────────                                          │
  │  index.ts       → Add backend plugins, configure database    │
  │  plugins/       → Register plugin backend modules            │
  │                                                              │
  │  plugins/                   ← ✏️ Build custom plugins        │
  │  ────────                                                      │
  │  my-plugin/     → Your custom plugin (frontend + backend)    │
  │                                                              │
  │  app-config.yaml            ← 🔧 Configure integrations      │
  │  catalog-info.yaml          ← 📋 Register Backstage itself   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team most frequently edits packages/app/src/plugins.ts to add community plugins (GitHub, Grafana, PagerDuty) and plugins/anihpj-theme/ for custom branding.

Dev Workflow Q35 of 200
What port does Backstage frontend and backend run on by default?
A) Frontend on port 80 (HTTP), Backend on port 443 (HTTPS) — standard web ports
B) Frontend on port 3000, Backend on port 7007 — these are the default ports set by create-app and used during yarn dev
C) Both frontend and backend share port 8080 — Backstage uses a reverse proxy
D) Frontend on port 5000, Backend on port 5001 — Flask-style conventions
✅ Correct Answer: B

In development (yarn dev), Backstage runs the React frontend on localhost:3000 and the Express backend on localhost:7007. The frontend proxies API requests to the backend at /api/* — so when your browser calls localhost:3000/api/catalog/entities, it's forwarded to localhost:7007/api/catalog/entities. This proxy is configured automatically by create-app.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Port Configuration — Frontend → Backend Proxy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DEFAULT PORTS                                               │
  │                                                              │
  │  Browser                    Frontend          Backend        │
  │  ───────                    ────────          ───────        │
  │  http://localhost:3000  →   :3000 (React)    :7007 (Express) │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  HOW API CALLS WORK:                                 │    │
  │  │                                                      │    │
  │  │  Browser calls:                                      │    │
  │  │  GET localhost:3000/api/catalog/entities             │    │
  │  │         │                                            │    │
  │  │         ▼ (proxy configured in app-config.yaml)      │    │
  │  │  Forwarded to:                                       │    │
  │  │  GET localhost:7007/api/catalog/entities             │    │
  │  │                                                      │    │
  │  │  This proxy avoids CORS issues in development        │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                              │
  │  Can be changed via app-config.yaml:                         │
  │  app.listen.port: 3000    (frontend)                         │
  │  backend.listen.port: 7007 (backend)                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: In production on the kubeadm cluster, Backstage runs behind an Ingress at backstage.anihpj.com on standard HTTPS port 443. The port 3000/7007 split is only for local development.

Dev Workflow Q36 of 200
How do you build Backstage for production?
A) yarn build compiles TypeScript and bundles the frontend into optimized static assets, then yarn start runs the production build. Or use Docker: yarn build-image creates a production container image
B) Run yarn dev — it's the same for development and production
C) Push the source code to GitHub and Backstage automatically builds on Vercel
D) There's no build step — Backstage runs directly from TypeScript source in production via ts-node
✅ Correct Answer: A

For production, you use yarn build (compiles TypeScript → JavaScript, bundles with webpack, minifies, tree-shakes) followed by yarn start (runs the optimized production server). For Kubernetes deployment, yarn build-image creates a Docker image with the built frontend and backend. The key difference from yarn dev: no hot reload, no source maps, optimized bundles, and production database (PostgreSQL).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Production Build Pipeline
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PRODUCTION BUILD                                            │
  │                                                              │
  │  yarn build                                                  │
  │  ──────────                                                  │
  │  ① yarn tsc        → TypeScript → JavaScript                 │
  │  ② webpack         → Bundle JS + CSS + assets                │
  │  ③ Minification    → Reduce file size                        │
  │  ④ Output: dist/   → Production-ready static files           │
  │                                                              │
  │  yarn start                                                  │
  │  ──────────                                                  │
  │  • Serves dist/ from Express backend                         │
  │  • NODE_ENV=production                                       │
  │  • Uses app-config.production.yaml                           │
  │  • Connects to PostgreSQL (NOT SQLite)                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  Docker Alternative:                                         │
  │  ──────────────────                                          │
  │  yarn build-image → Creates Docker container                 │
  │  docker run backstage → Production in a container            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The production Backstage at backstage.anihpj.com is built with yarn build, containerized with yarn build-image, and deployed to the kubeadm cluster as a Kubernetes Deployment with a PostgreSQL StatefulSet.

Dev Workflow Q37 of 200
What is the difference between yarn dev and yarn start?
A) They are identical — both start the application in development mode
B) yarn dev starts the frontend only, yarn start starts both frontend and backend
C) yarn dev = development mode with hot module reload, source maps, and SQLite database. yarn start = production mode serving optimized static files from the build output, with PostgreSQL database and no hot reload
D) yarn dev uses PostgreSQL, yarn start uses SQLite
✅ Correct Answer: C

yarn dev is for development: hot reload (changes appear instantly), source maps (debug TypeScript in browser), SQLite database (zero setup), and verbose logging. yarn start is for production: serves pre-built optimized bundles from dist/, uses PostgreSQL, no hot reload, minimal logging, and assumes yarn build was already run. You never use yarn start during development.

📝 Detailed Explanation
yarn dev vs yarn start — Side-by-Side
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  yarn dev (DEVELOPMENT)         yarn start (PRODUCTION)      │
  │  ────────────────────────       ─────────────────────────    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────┐       ┌──────────────────────┐     │
  │  │ ⚡ Hot Reload        │       │ ❌ No Hot Reload     │     │
  │  │ 🔍 Source Maps       │       │ ❌ No Source Maps    │     │
  │  │ 🗄️  SQLite (auto)    │       │ 🗄️  PostgreSQL       │     │
  │  │ 📝 Verbose Logs      │       │ 📝 Minimal Logs      │     │
  │  │ 🐛 Debug Friendly    │       │ 🚀 Optimized Bundles │     │
  │  │ 🔧 ts-node + nodemon │       │ 📦 Built dist/ files │     │
  │  └──────────────────────┘       └──────────────────────┘     │
  │                                                              │
  │  Prerequisite: none              Prerequisite: yarn build    │
  │  Database: SQLite (auto)         Database: PostgreSQL        │
  │  Ports: 3000 + 7007              Ports: 3000 + 7007          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Developers use yarn dev on their laptops with SQLite for instant setup. The production deployment at backstage.anihpj.com uses yarn build + yarn start with PostgreSQL on the kubeadm cluster.

Dev Workflow Q38 of 200
How do you configure the database for Backstage?
A) Backstage auto-detects the database — no configuration is needed
B) In app-config.yaml under backend.database.connection. For development, SQLite is the default (no config needed). For production, specify a PostgreSQL connection string: postgresql://user:pass@host:5432/backstage_db
C) Database configuration is hardcoded in packages/backend/src/index.ts and cannot be changed
D) Backstage only supports SQLite — PostgreSQL requires a commercial license
✅ Correct Answer: B

Database configuration lives in app-config.yaml under backend.database.connection. In development, you can omit this entirely — Backstage defaults to SQLite stored as a local file. For production, set the connection to PostgreSQL: backend.database.client: pg and backend.database.connection: postgresql://.... You can also set it via environment variable APP_CONFIG_backend_database_connection for secrets.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Database Configuration — Dev vs Prod
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DATABASE CONFIGURATION                                      │
  │                                                              │
  │  Development (app-config.yaml — defaults):                   │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────                       │
  │  backend:                                                    │
  │    database:                                                 │
  │      client: better-sqlite3    ← Auto, no config needed     │
  │  → Creates backstage.db in project root                      │
  │                                                              │
  │  Production (app-config.production.yaml):                    │
  │  ────────────────────────────────────────                    │
  │  backend:                                                    │
  │    database:                                                 │
  │      client: pg                 ← PostgreSQL                 │
  │      connection:                                            │
  │        host: postgres.anihpj.svc                            │
  │        port: 5432                                           │
  │        user: backstage                                      │
  │        password: ${DB_PASSWORD}  ← From env var              │
  │                                                              │
  │  Or via environment variable (highest priority):             │
  │  APP_CONFIG_backend_database_connection=                     │
  │    postgresql://user:pass@host:5432/backstage                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Production uses PostgreSQL StatefulSet on kubeadm. The connection string is injected via Kubernetes Secret as an environment variable. Local dev uses SQLite with zero configuration.

Dev Workflow Q39 of 200
What is app-config.local.yaml and when is it used?
A) A gitignored local override file for developer-specific settings like personal GitHub tokens. It's merged on top of app-config.yaml during local development and NEVER committed to version control
B) The primary configuration file that replaces app-config.yaml entirely in production
C) A file that stores only localization/translation settings for different languages
D) A generated file created automatically by yarn build — never edited manually
✅ Correct Answer: A

app-config.local.yaml is a developer's personal configuration file. It's listed in .gitignore by default because it contains secrets and personal settings that should NEVER be committed (personal GitHub tokens, local database paths, dev-specific feature flags). It's merged on top of app-config.yaml only when running locally — production deployments ignore it.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Config Priority — app-config.local.yaml Role
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CONFIG LAYERING (Development)                               │
  │                                                              │
  │  🥉 app-config.yaml          ← Base config (committed)      │
  │       app:                                                   │
  │         title: Backstage                                     │
  │                                                              │
  │  🥈 app-config.local.yaml    ← Local overrides (GITIGNORED!) │
  │       integrations:                                          │
  │         github:                                              │
  │           - host: github.com                                 │
  │             token: ghp_myPersonalToken  ← NEVER COMMIT!     │
  │                                                              │
  │  🥇 Environment Variables     ← Highest priority             │
  │       APP_CONFIG_backend_database_connection=...             │
  │                                                              │
  │  Result: All three merged at runtime in local dev            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Each developer has their own app-config.local.yaml with their personal GitHub token for testing catalog discovery. This file is in .gitignore — it never reaches the shared repository or production.

Dev Workflow Q40 of 200
How do you customize the Backstage theme?
A) Edit a CSS file at packages/app/public/theme.css — Backstage uses plain CSS for theming
B) Use the Theme Editor in the Backstage UI at /settings/theme — a drag-and-drop interface
C) Modify packages/app/src/apis.ts or App.tsx to create a custom theme using Material-UI's createTheme() function. Override colors, typography, logo, and spacing to match your brand
D) Backstage themes are fixed — you cannot customize them beyond the default dark theme
✅ Correct Answer: C

Backstage theming uses Material-UI's createTheme(). In packages/app/src/apis.ts, you call createApp() with a custom theme that overrides: primary/secondary colors, typography fonts, dark/light mode defaults, logo (full and icon variants), and component defaults. Since Backstage is built on MUI, ALL plugins automatically inherit your theme — you theme once, everything updates.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Backstage Theming — Customize via createTheme()
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  THEMING IN packages/app/src/apis.ts                         │
  │                                                              │
  │  const app = createApp({                                     │
  │    apis: [...],                                              │
  │    themes: [{                                                │
  │      id: 'anihpj-theme',                                     │
  │      title: 'Anihpj Dark',                                   │
  │      variant: 'dark',                                        │
  │      provider: ({ children }) => (                           │
  │                                                          │
  │          {children}                                          │
  │                                              │
  │      ),                                                      │
  │    }],                                                       │
  │  })                                                          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The platform team created an "Anihpj Dark" theme with brand colors (blue primary, green secondary) and the company logo. Every plugin — GitHub, Grafana, PagerDuty — automatically renders with anihpj's branding because they all use MUI's theme system.

1.5 Backstage CLI & Development WorkflowQ41-Q48

Dev Workflow Q41 of 200
How do you create a new Backstage plugin?
A) Manually create a folder in plugins/ and write all files from scratch
B) Run yarn backstage-cli plugin:create — it scaffolds the plugin with correct structure, package.json, tsconfig, and optionally frontend and/or backend code
C) Download a plugin template from the Backstage Plugin Marketplace website
D) Use npx create-react-plugin — Backstage plugins are standard React packages
✅ Correct Answer: B

yarn backstage-cli plugin:create is the scaffolding command. It asks for a plugin ID and whether you want frontend, backend, or both. It generates: src/plugin.ts (plugin instance), src/index.ts (public API), package.json with Backstage dependencies, tsconfig.json, and optionally src/components/ (frontend) or src/router.ts (backend).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Plugin Creation — Scaffolding Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  yarn backstage-cli plugin:create                            │
  │  ────────────────────────────────                            │
  │                                                              │
  │  ① Asks: "Enter an ID for the plugin [required]"             │
  │     → my-custom-plugin                                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  ② Asks: "What kind of plugin do you want?"                  │
  │     → frontend (React components)                            │
  │     → backend  (Express routes)                              │
  │     → both     (frontend + backend)                          │
  │                                                              │
  │  ③ Generates:                                                │
  │  plugins/my-custom-plugin/                                   │
  │  ├── src/                                                    │
  │  │   ├── index.ts          ← Public API exports              │
  │  │   ├── plugin.ts         ← Plugin instance                 │
  │  │   ├── components/       ← React components (frontend)    │
  │  │   └── router.ts         ← Express routes (backend)       │
  │  ├── package.json          ← Dependencies                   │
  │  └── tsconfig.json         ← TypeScript config               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team used this command to create anihpj-theme (frontend plugin for custom branding) and anihpj-templates (backend plugin for custom scaffolder actions).

Dev Workflow Q42 of 200
What is the purpose of the Backstage CLI (backstage-cli)?
A) A command-line tool for scaffolding, building, linting, testing, and packaging Backstage plugins and apps — it's the Swiss Army knife for Backstage development
B) A deployment tool that pushes Backstage to cloud providers (AWS, GCP, Azure)
C) A monitoring dashboard CLI that shows real-time Backstage metrics
D) A database migration tool specifically for PostgreSQL schema changes
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Backstage CLI (backstage-cli) is the primary development tool. Its main commands: plugin:create (scaffold new plugin), package build (build a plugin), package lint (run ESLint), package test (run Jest tests), package start (start dev server), versions:bump (upgrade Backstage packages), and config:check (validate app-config). Every Backstage developer uses it daily.

Dev Workflow Q43 of 200
How do you add an existing community plugin to your Backstage instance?
A) Download a ZIP file from backstage.io/plugins and extract it into the plugins/ directory
B) Use the Backstage Plugin Marketplace UI at /plugins to click "Install" on any plugin
C) Clone the plugin's GitHub repository directly into your packages/ directory
D) For frontend: yarn add --cwd packages/app @backstage/plugin-<name> then register in packages/app/src/plugins.ts. For backend: yarn add --cwd packages/backend @backstage/backend-plugin-<name> then add to packages/backend/src/index.ts
✅ Correct Answer: D

Adding a community plugin is a two-step process: Install the npm package into the correct workspace (packages/app for frontend, packages/backend for backend), then Register it — frontend plugins go in packages/app/src/plugins.ts, backend plugins in packages/backend/src/index.ts. Some plugins (like Grafana) need BOTH frontend and backend components.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Adding a Community Plugin — Step by Step
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  EXAMPLE: Adding the Grafana Plugin                          │
  │                                                              │
  │  Step 1: Install frontend package                            │
  │  ─────────────────────────────                               │
  │  yarn add --cwd packages/app @backstage/plugin-grafana       │
  │                                                              │
  │  Step 2: Register frontend in plugins.ts                     │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────                       │
  │  // packages/app/src/plugins.ts                              │
  │  export { grafanaPlugin } from '@backstage/plugin-grafana';  │
  │                                                              │
  │  Step 3: Install backend package                             │
  │  ─────────────────────────────                               │
  │  yarn add --cwd packages/backend                             │
  │    @backstage/plugin-grafana-backend                         │
  │                                                              │
  │  Step 4: Register backend in index.ts                        │
  │  ───────────────────────────────────                         │
  │  // packages/backend/src/index.ts                            │
  │  backend.add(import('@backstage/plugin-grafana-backend'));   │
  │                                                              │
  │  Step 5: Configure in app-config.yaml                        │
  │  ──────────────────────────────────                          │
  │  proxy:                                                     │
  │    endpoints:                                               │
  │      '/grafana':                                            │
  │        target: https://grafana.anihpj.com                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team added GitHub, Grafana, PagerDuty, and Kubernetes plugins using this exact process. Each plugin required both frontend and backend packages, plus configuration in app-config.yaml.

Dev Workflow Q44 of 200
How do you run tests in Backstage?
A) Backstage doesn't include a testing framework — you must install Jest separately
B) Use npm test — Backstage uses Mocha and Chai for all testing
C) Run yarn test or yarn backstage-cli package test. Backstage uses Jest with React Testing Library for frontend components and Jest with supertest for backend API testing
D) Tests run automatically in the browser at localhost:3000/__tests__
✅ Correct Answer: C

Backstage uses Jest as its testing framework. Frontend components are tested with React Testing Library (render components, simulate clicks, assert output). Backend APIs are tested with supertest (make HTTP requests to Express routes, assert responses). Run all tests with yarn test or test a specific package with yarn backstage-cli package test --help.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Backstage Testing — Frontend + Backend
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TESTING IN BACKSTAGE                                        │
  │                                                              │
  │  Frontend Tests (React Testing Library + Jest)               │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────                   │
  │  test('renders entity page', async () => {                   │
  │    render();               │
  │    expect(screen.getByText('jobpost-api')).toBeVisible();    │
  │  });                                                         │
  │                                                              │
  │  Backend Tests (Jest + supertest)                            │
  │  ────────────────────────────────                            │
  │  test('GET /api/catalog/entities', async () => {             │
  │    const response = await request(app)                       │
  │      .get('/api/catalog/entities')                           │
  │      .expect(200);                                          │
  │    expect(response.body).toHaveLength(3);                    │
  │  });                                                         │
  │                                                              │
  │  Commands:                                                   │
  │  yarn test                    → Run all tests                │
  │  yarn test --watch            → Watch mode (re-run on change)│
  │  yarn test --coverage         → Generate coverage report     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The CI/CD pipeline (GitHub Actions) runs yarn test on every pull request. Custom plugins like anihpj-theme include their own Jest tests to ensure branding renders correctly.

Dev Workflow Q45 of 200
How do you lint Backstage code?
A) Backstage has no built-in linting — you must configure ESLint manually
B) Run yarn lint or yarn backstage-cli package lint. Backstage uses ESLint with its shared config @backstage/cli/config/eslint, which enforces Backstage coding standards
C) Linting runs automatically in the browser — errors appear as red underlines in the Backstage UI
D) Use Prettier only — Backstage prioritizes formatting over linting rules
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage uses ESLint with the @backstage/cli/config/eslint shared configuration. This config enforces Backstage-specific rules: TypeScript strict mode, React hooks rules, import ordering, and naming conventions. Run yarn lint to check all packages, or yarn lint --fix to auto-fix issues.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Linting in Backstage — ESLint + Backstage Config
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LINTING COMMANDS                                            │

  │  yarn backstage-cli package lint → Lint specific package     │
  │                                                              │
  │  ESLint Config (inherits @backstage/cli/config/eslint):      │
  │  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────    │
  │  • @typescript-eslint rules — TypeScript best practices      │
  │  • react-hooks rules — useEffect dependencies, etc.         │
  │  • import/order — Consistent import ordering                │
  │  • no-console — Warn on console.log in production code      │
  │                                                              │
  │  CI/CD Integration:                                          │
  │  GitHub Actions: yarn lint → must pass before merge          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The CI/CD pipeline runs yarn lint on every PR. If linting fails, the PR cannot be merged — enforcing consistent code quality across the entire platform team.

Dev Workflow Q46 of 200
What is the TypeScript configuration in Backstage?
A) tsconfig.json at the project root with "strict": true. Sub-projects (packages/app, packages/backend, plugins/) extend it. Path aliases (@backstage/*, @internal/*) resolve to the correct packages in the monorepo
B) TypeScript is optional — Backstage can run with plain JavaScript if you remove tsconfig.json
C) Each plugin has its own completely independent TypeScript config with no shared settings
D) Backstage uses Babel for TypeScript compilation, not tsc — tsconfig.json is just for IDE support
✅ Correct Answer: A

The root tsconfig.json defines the base TypeScript configuration with "strict": true (enables all strict type-checking options). Each package (packages/app/tsconfig.json, packages/backend/tsconfig.json) extends the root config. Path aliases like @backstage/core-plugin-api and @internal/plugin-name resolve correctly across the monorepo.

📝 Detailed Explanation
TypeScript Configuration — Monorepo Setup
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TSCONFIG HIERARCHY                                          │
  │                                                              │
  │  tsconfig.json (ROOT)                                        │
  │  ─────────────────                                           │
  │  {                                                           │
  │    "compilerOptions": {                                      │
  │      "strict": true,          ← Maximum type safety          │
  │      "target": "ES2021",      ← Modern JavaScript output     │
  │      "module": "commonjs",    ← Node.js module system        │
  │      "jsx": "react-jsx",     ← React JSX support            │
  │      "paths": {                                              │
  │        "@backstage/*": [...], ← Monorepo path aliases        │
  │        "@internal/*": [...]                                  │
  │      }                                                       │
  │    }                                                         │
  │  }                                                           │
  │                                                              │
  │  ├── packages/app/tsconfig.json     ← Extends root           │
  │  ├── packages/backend/tsconfig.json ← Extends root           │
  │  └── plugins/*/tsconfig.json        ← Each extends root      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The strict TypeScript config ensures that custom plugins follow the same type safety standards as core Backstage. Any type error in anihpj-theme or anihpj-templates is caught at compile time.

Dev Workflow Q47 of 200
How do you create a new entity type in the catalog?
A) Add a new YAML file with kind: MyNewType — Backstage auto-discovers new entity kinds
B) Use the Backstage UI at /catalog/create to define a custom entity kind through a form
C) Edit the Backstage source code to add the new kind to the core entity model
D) Create a catalog backend module with createBackendModule(), define the entity kind, add a processor for YAML → entity transformation, and register it in the backend
✅ Correct Answer: D

Custom entity kinds require a backend module that extends the catalog. You use createBackendModule() with a catalogProcessingExtensionPoint to register a new entity processor. The processor reads YAML files of your custom kind, transforms them into entity objects, and the catalog ingests them just like built-in kinds (Component, API, Resource, etc.).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Custom Entity Kind — Catalog Module
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CREATING A CUSTOM ENTITY KIND                               │
  │                                                              │
  │  Step 1: Create catalog module                               │
  │  ───────────────────────────                                 │
  │  const myModule = createBackendModule({                      │
  │    pluginId: 'catalog',                                      │
  │    moduleId: 'my-entity-processor',                          │
  │    register(env) {                                           │
  │      env.registerInit({                                      │
  │        deps: { catalog: catalogProcessingExtensionPoint },   │
  │        async init({ catalog }) {                             │
  │          catalog.addProcessor(new MyEntityProcessor());      │
  │        },                                                    │
  │      });                                                     │
  │    },                                                        │
  │  });                                                         │
  │                                                              │
  │  Step 2: Implement processor → read YAML → create entity     │
  │  Step 3: Register in packages/backend/src/index.ts           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: While the built-in entity kinds (Component, API, Resource, System) cover the current needs, the team could create a custom "DeploymentEnvironment" kind in the future to model staging/production environments as first-class entities.

Dev Workflow Q48 of 200
How do you upgrade Backstage to a new version?
A) Run yarn upgrade backstage — a single command upgrades everything automatically
B) Use the Backstage Upgrade Helper, run yarn backstage-cli versions:bump to update @backstage/* packages, check breaking changes in the changelog, test in dev, then build and deploy
C) Delete the project and run create-app again with the new version — there's no upgrade path
D) Backstage auto-updates itself like a web browser — no manual upgrade needed
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage upgrades are a manual but well-documented process. The Backstage Upgrade Helper (a CLI tool) shows you exactly which packages changed and any breaking changes. You run yarn backstage-cli versions:bump to update package versions, review the changelog for breaking changes, update your code if needed, test with yarn dev, run yarn tsc to verify types, then yarn build and deploy.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Backstage Upgrade Process
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  UPGRADE WORKFLOW                                            │
  │                                                              │
  │  ① yarn backstage-cli versions:bump                          │
  │     → Updates @backstage/* packages to latest                │
  │                                                              │
  │  ② Check changelog for breaking changes                      │
  │     → backstage.io/docs/releases/                            │
  │                                                              │
  │  ③ Apply migration steps (if any)                            │
  │     → Update app-config.yaml structure                       │
  │     → Update plugin API calls                                │
  │                                                              │
  │  ④ yarn install → Install updated packages                   │
  │  ⑤ yarn tsc      → Verify no type errors                     │
  │  ⑥ yarn dev      → Test locally                              │
  │  ⑦ yarn build    → Build for production                      │
  │  ⑧ Deploy        → Update K8s deployment                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The platform team upgrades Backstage monthly, testing in a staging environment (kind cluster, ksw → 2) before deploying to production (kubeadm, ksw → 1).

2.1 Framework — Release Strategy, K8s Deploy & OperationsQ49-Q56

Infrastructure Q49 of 200
What is the Backstage release channel strategy?
A) Backstage has an LTS (Long-Term Support) release every 6 months with 2 years of security backports — like Node.js
B) Backstage follows a major.minor.patch semver with quarterly major releases and 1 year of support for each
C) Frequent releases (roughly weekly), no LTS — you stay current with the latest version. Breaking changes are documented in the changelog, and the Backstage Upgrade Helper assists with migrations
D) Backstage auto-updates itself via the @backstage/core-plugin-api — no manual upgrades needed
✅ Correct Answer: C

Backstage uses a frequent release cadence (approximately weekly) rather than fixed major releases. There is no LTS version — the expectation is that adopters stay current. Breaking changes are clearly documented in each release's changelog, and the Backstage Upgrade Helper (a CLI tool) shows you exactly what changed between your version and the target version.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Backstage Release Strategy — Stay Current, No LTS
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BACKSTAGE RELEASE STRATEGY                                  │
  │                                                              │
  │  📦 Release Cadence: ~Weekly (not fixed)                     │
  │  🏷️  Versioning: 1.x.y (following semver loosely)            │
  │  🚫 No LTS: No "long term support" releases                  │
  │                                                              │
  │  UPGRADE PATH:                                               │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐               │
  │  │ v1.14.0  │ →  │ v1.15.0  │ →  │ v1.16.0  │  ...         │
  │  └──────────┘    └──────────┘    └──────────┘               │
  │                                                              │
  │  TOOLS FOR UPGRADING:                                        │
  │  🔧 Upgrade Helper: backstage.github.io/upgrade-helper       │
  │     → Shows diff between your version and latest            │
  │     → Lists breaking changes with migration steps            │
  │  📋 yarn backstage-cli versions:bump                         │
  │     → Updates @backstage/* packages to latest               │
  │  📝 CHANGELOG.md in every package                            │
  │     → Documents what changed and why                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team upgrades monthly (not weekly) to balance stability with staying current. They use the Upgrade Helper to assess the impact of each jump, test on the staging kind cluster (ksw → 2), and only then promote to the kubeadm production cluster (ksw → 1).

How do you contribute to the Backstage open-source project?
A) Send an email to the CNCF TOC (Technical Oversight Committee) with your patch attached
B) Go to github.com/backstage/backstage, pick a "good first issue", sign the CLA (Contributor License Agreement), follow CONTRIBUTING.md, and submit a pull request. CNCF community meetings are held every other week
C) Upload your plugin to the Backstage Plugin Marketplace website — a review committee evaluates and merges it monthly
D) Only Spotify employees can contribute to the core Backstage repo — external contributions go to community plugins only
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage is an open-source CNCF project that welcomes community contributions. The standard flow: fork the backstage/backstage repo on GitHub, find a "good first issue" (tagged for newcomers), sign the CLA (Contributor License Agreement), follow the guidelines in CONTRIBUTING.md, make your changes, and submit a pull request. Community meetings happen every other week and are open to everyone.

  │     → Required for all contributors                          │
  │     → Grants Spotify/CNCF rights to distribute your code     │

  │                                                              │

  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Infrastructure Q51 of 200
How do you deploy Backstage to Kubernetes?
A) Use the official Helm chart (backstage/backstage), or build a Docker image with `yarn build-image` → push to registry → apply Kubernetes manifests (Deployment, Service, Ingress, ConfigMap, Secret)
B) Backstage is a SaaS product — you log into backstage.io and your portal is automatically hosted for you
C) Run `yarn deploy-k8s` — Backstage has a built-in command that provisions an entire Kubernetes cluster and deploys automatically
D) Deploy Backstage as a Kubernetes Operator using the official Backstage Operator from OperatorHub
✅ Correct Answer: A

Backstage is self-hosted — you deploy it yourself. The recommended approach for Kubernetes: (1) Build the Docker image with yarn build-image (which compiles the TypeScript backend and packages everything), (2) Push to a container registry, (3) Use the Helm chart from backstage/backstage or write your own K8s manifests — Deployment + Service + Ingress + ConfigMap + Secret.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Backstage on Kubernetes — Deployment Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BACKSTAGE ON KUBERNETES                                     │
  │                                                              │
  │  Internet → Ingress (HTTPS) → Service → Deployment           │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────┐         │
  │  │  KUBERNETES CLUSTER (kubeadm, ksw → 1)          │         │
  │  │                                                  │         │
  │  │  ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────────────────────┐   │         │
  │  │  │ Ingress  │ ──▶ │ Service: backstage        │   │         │
  │  │  │ TLS cert │     │ port: 7007                │   │         │
  │  │  └──────────┘     └───────────┬──────────────┘   │         │
  │  │                               │                   │         │
  │  │                      ┌────────▼──────────┐       │         │
  │  │                      │ Deployment         │       │         │
  │  │                      │ backstage-backend  │       │         │
  │  │                      │ replicas: 2        │       │         │
  │  │                      │ image: backstage   │       │         │
  │  │                      │ envFrom: secret    │       │         │
  │  │                      │          configMap │       │         │
  │  │                      └────────┬──────────┘       │         │
  │  │                               │                   │         │
  │  │                      ┌────────▼──────────┐       │         │
  │  │                      │ StatefulSet        │       │         │
  │  │                      │ PostgreSQL 15      │       │         │
  │  │                      │ PVC: 20Gi          │       │         │
  │  │                      └───────────────────┘       │         │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────┘         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Backstage is deployed on the kubeadm cluster (ksw → 1) using a custom Helm chart. The image is built via GitHub Actions CI/CD and pushed to a private registry. PostgreSQL runs as a StatefulSet in the same namespace.

Infrastructure Q52 of 200
What does Backstage need in a production Kubernetes deployment?
A) Just a Deployment and Service — Backstage is stateless and needs no database
B) A cloud-managed SQL database (AWS RDS / GCP Cloud SQL) is mandatory — SQLite won't work in Kubernetes
C) Only an Ingress controller — Backstage runs as a static frontend served by NGINX
D) PostgreSQL database (StatefulSet or external), PersistentVolume for DB storage, Ingress for HTTPS, Secrets for tokens/passwords, and ConfigMap for app-config.yaml
✅ Correct Answer: D

Production Backstage requires: PostgreSQL (not SQLite in production), a PersistentVolume for database storage (so data survives pod restarts), an Ingress with TLS for HTTPS access, Secrets for sensitive values (GitHub token, DB password), and a ConfigMap for app-config.yaml (non-sensitive configuration).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Production Requirements — What You MUST Have
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PRODUCTION DEPLOYMENT CHECKLIST                             │
  │                                                              │
  │  ✅ PostgreSQL Database (NOT SQLite)                         │
  │     • SQLite is for dev ONLY — not suitable for production   │
  │     • Can be StatefulSet in-cluster or external (e.g., RDS)  │
  │                                                              │
  │  ✅ PersistentVolume (for Postgres data)                     │
  │     • Pod restarts must not lose catalog data                │
  │     • 20Gi minimum recommended                               │
  │                                                              │

  │     • HTTPS at backstage.anihpj.com                          │

  │                                                              │
  │  ✅ Secrets (K8s Secret resource)                            │

  │                                                              │
  │  ✅ ConfigMap (for app-config.yaml)                          │
  │     • Non-sensitive config: baseUrl, title, catalog schedule │
  │     • Mounted as a file in the container                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The kubeadm cluster runs PostgreSQL as a StatefulSet with a 20Gi PVC. Secrets are injected via Kubernetes Secret resources (NOT committed to Git). The Ingress terminates TLS at backstage.anihpj.com using cert-manager.

Infrastructure Q53 of 200
How does Backstage handle database migrations?
A) Backstage uses Knex.js migrations — they run automatically on backend startup via `knex migrate:latest`. Migration files live in each plugin's `migrations/` directory
B) You must manually run `yarn migrate` before starting Backstage — migrations never run automatically
C) Backstage uses Prisma ORM with auto-migration — schema changes are detected and applied in real-time
D) Database migrations don't exist in Backstage — the schema is defined in app-config.yaml and auto-created
✅ Correct Answer: A

Backstage uses Knex.js as its database query builder and migration framework. When the backend starts, it automatically runs knex migrate:latest — this checks the migrations/ directory in each plugin and applies any migration files that haven't been run yet (tracked in a knex_migrations table). This means database schema changes are applied seamlessly on deployment.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Knex.js Migrations — Auto-Applied on Startup
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DATABASE MIGRATIONS IN BACKSTAGE                            │
  │                                                              │
  │  Backend Startup Flow:                                       │
  │  ① Backend starts → initializes database connection          │
  │  ② Scans ALL plugins for migrations/ directory               │
  │  ③ Checks knex_migrations table for applied migrations       │
  │  ④ Runs any NEW migrations (knex migrate:latest)             │
  │  ⑤ Migrations run in timestamp order                         │
  │  ⑥ Backend continues startup → ready for traffic             │
  │                                                              │
  │  Example Migration File:                                     │
  │  plugins/catalog-backend/migrations/20231201_create_entities │
  │  ├── exports.up = function(knex) { ... }   ← Apply change    │
  │  └── exports.down = function(knex) { ... } ← Rollback        │
  │                                                              │
  │  Tracked in: knex_migrations table                            │
  │  ┌────────────────┬──────────────────────┐                   │
  │  │ name           │ migrated_at          │                   │
  │  ├────────────────┼──────────────────────┤                   │
  │  │ 20231201_...   │ 2024-01-15 10:30:00  │                   │
  │  └────────────────┴──────────────────────┘                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When the platform team upgrades Backstage and new migrations are included, they simply deploy the new Docker image — Knex automatically applies the migration on the first pod that starts. No manual migration scripts needed.

Infrastructure Q54 of 200
How do you configure Backstage for high availability (HA)?
A) Run multiple backend replicas behind a load balancer, share a PostgreSQL database, use Redis for externalized sessions, keep the frontend stateless, and configure Kubernetes liveness/readiness probes
B) Backstage is single-instance by design — you cannot run multiple replicas because the catalog uses in-memory caching that can't be shared
C) Use AWS ECS Fargate with auto-scaling — Backstage natively supports horizontal scaling with no additional configuration
D) Deploy Backstage as a serverless function on AWS Lambda — it scales automatically to zero when not in use
✅ Correct Answer: A

Backstage can be run in HA mode with the right configuration: Multiple backend replicas behind a load balancer (Kubernetes Service or external LB), a shared PostgreSQL database (all replicas connect to the same DB — this is critical), Redis for session storage (so sessions survive pod restarts and are shared across replicas), a stateless frontend (static React bundle served by NGINX or the backend), and health probes for Kubernetes to restart unhealthy pods.

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │                    └────┬─────┘                              │
  │                         │                                    │
  │          ┌──────────────┼──────────────┐                     │
  │          │              │              │                     │
  │     ┌────▼────┐   ┌────▼────┐   ┌────▼────┐                 │
  │     │ Pod A   │   │ Pod B   │   │ Pod C   │  (replicas: 3)  │
  │     │ Backend │   │ Backend │   │ Backend │                 │

  │     │PostgreSQL│   │  Redis  │  (shared, externalized)       │

  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The production deployment on kubeadm runs 2 backend replicas (controlled by replicas: 2 in the Deployment). PostgreSQL is a StatefulSet in the same namespace. Redis for sessions runs as a separate Deployment. Kubernetes probes at /healthcheck ensure only healthy pods receive traffic.

Infrastructure Q55 of 200
How do you monitor Backstage in production?
A) Backstage has a built-in monitoring dashboard at /monitoring — no external tools needed
B) Backstage sends telemetry to Spotify's internal monitoring platform — external users must request access
C) Use `/healthcheck` and `/.well-known` endpoints for health/liveness, Prometheus metrics via `@backstage/plugin-app-backend`, structured logging with winston, and distributed tracing via OpenTelemetry
D) Production monitoring is not supported — Backstage is a development tool and isn't designed for production observability
✅ Correct Answer: C

Backstage supports production monitoring through several mechanisms: /healthcheck endpoint (returns HTTP 200 if healthy — used by K8s liveness/readiness probes), /.well-known endpoints for service discovery, Prometheus metrics via the @backstage/plugin-app-backend plugin (exposes HTTP request counts, latencies, error rates), structured logging via winston (JSON-formatted logs), and OpenTelemetry integration for distributed tracing.

For anihpj/jobpost: The kubeadm cluster runs Prometheus + Grafana. Backstage's /metrics endpoint is scraped by Prometheus every 30s. Grafana dashboards show request latency, error rates, and catalog entity counts. Kubernetes probes hit /healthcheck to auto-restart unhealthy pods.

Infrastructure Q56 of 200
How do you back up Backstage data?
A) The PostgreSQL database is the critical piece — back it up with `pg_dump`. Catalog entities are re-ingestible from source repos. app-config.yaml should be in Git. A database dump is all you need for disaster recovery
B) Use `yarn backup` — Backstage has a built-in backup command that exports everything to a ZIP file
C) Backstage data is entirely ephemeral — there's nothing to back up because all data is regenerated on startup
D) Back up the entire plugins/ directory — all Backstage data is stored as files in the plugin directories
✅ Correct Answer: A

The PostgreSQL database is the ONLY stateful data in Backstage that needs backing up. It stores catalog entities, user data, scaffolder tasks, and session state. Backup via pg_dump backstage_db > backup.sql. Catalog entities are re-ingestible — if you lose the database, the catalog can be rebuilt from source repos (GitHub/GitLab) by re-scanning. app-config.yaml should always be in Git. Plugin code and the Backstage app itself are also in Git.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Backup Strategy — What to Back Up (and What NOT to)
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BACKSTAGE BACKUP STRATEGY                                   │
  │                                                              │
  │  🔴 MUST BACK UP — PostgreSQL Database                       │
  │  pg_dump -h localhost -U backstage backstage_db > backup.sql │
  │  • Schedule: daily cronjob or K8s CronJob                    │
  │  • Store: off-cluster (S3, GCS, or separate volume)          │
  │  • Retention: 30 days minimum                                │
  │                                                              │
  │  🟡 RE-INGESTIBLE (can be rebuilt)                           │
  │  • Catalog entities → re-scan GitHub/GitLab repos            │
  │  • Search index → regenerated from catalog on restart        │
  │  • TechDocs → rebuilt from source repos                      │
  │                                                              │
  │  🟢 ALREADY IN GIT (no backup needed)                        │
  │  • app-config.yaml → in your Backstage repo                  │
  │  • Plugin code → in your Backstage repo                      │
  │  • catalog-info.yaml files → in each service repo            │
  │  • Software Templates → in your Backstage repo               │
  │                                                              │
  │  DISASTER RECOVERY PLAN:                                     │
  │  ① Restore PostgreSQL from pg_dump                           │
  │  ② Re-deploy Backstage (same config from Git)                │
  │  ③ Catalog re-ingests entities from source repos             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: A Kubernetes CronJob runs pg_dump nightly on the PostgreSQL StatefulSet in the kubeadm cluster. Backups are pushed to an S3-compatible object store. The catalog-info.yaml files in each GitHub repo (anihpj/jobpost, anihpj/infra, etc.) serve as the source of truth for catalog entities.

Infrastructure Q57 of 200
What environment variables does Backstage require in production?
A) Only NODE_ENV=production — Backstage auto-detects everything else from the Kubernetes environment
B) POSTGRES_USER, POSTGRES_PASSWORD, and POSTGRES_DB — the standard PostgreSQL environment variables
C) NODE_ENV=production, DATABASE_URL or POSTGRES_HOST/PORT/USER/PASSWORD, GITHUB_TOKEN (or equivalent for your Git provider), APP_CONFIG_app_baseUrl (or set in app-config), and auth provider secrets
D) No environment variables are needed — everything is configured in app-config.yaml and committed to Git
✅ Correct Answer: C

Backstage in production needs: NODE_ENV=production (enables optimizations, disables dev features), database connection details (DATABASE_URL or individual POSTGRES_HOST/PORT/USER/PASSWORD), integration tokens (GITHUB_TOKEN for catalog discovery, GITLAB_TOKEN, etc.), APP_CONFIG_app_baseUrl (public URL of your Backstage instance), and auth provider secrets (OAuth client secrets, SAML certs).

  │  APP_CONFIG_app_baseUrl=https://backstage.anihpj.com         │
  │                                                              │
  │  🟡 REQUIRED FOR INTEGRATIONS                                │

For anihpj/jobpost: The production Backstage on kubeadm uses a Kubernetes Secret that injects DATABASE_URL, GITHUB_TOKEN, and AUTH_GITHUB_CLIENT_SECRET as environment variables into the Deployment pod. The APP_CONFIG_app_baseUrl is set via ConfigMap.

Infrastructure Q58 of 200
How does Backstage handle secrets (tokens, passwords, API keys)?
A) Secrets go in app-config.yaml under a `secrets:` section — the file is encrypted at rest by Backstage
B) Secrets should NEVER be in app-config.yaml. Use environment variables (process.env.GITHUB_TOKEN). In Kubernetes, mount Secrets as env vars. Never commit tokens to Git
C) Backstage has a built-in Vault integration that automatically encrypts all secrets in app-config.yaml
D) Secrets are stored in the PostgreSQL database in an encrypted column — they're set via the Backstage UI at /admin/secrets
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage follows the 12-Factor App principle: configuration (especially secrets) should be stored in environment variables, not in config files. Never commit tokens, passwords, or API keys to Git. In app-config.yaml, reference secrets via ${GITHUB_TOKEN} — the value is resolved from the environment at runtime. In Kubernetes, use Secret resources and inject them as environment variables via envFrom in the Deployment spec.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Secrets Handling — The Right Way
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SECRETS BEST PRACTICES                                      │
  │                                                              │
  │  ❌ WRONG: Secrets in app-config.yaml                        │
  │  integrations:                                               │
  │    github:                                                   │
  │      - host: github.com                                      │
  │        token: ghp_abc123  ← NEVER DO THIS!                   │
  │                                                              │
  │  ✅ RIGHT: Reference environment variables                   │
  │  integrations:                                               │
  │    github:                                                   │
  │      - host: github.com                                      │
  │        token: ${GITHUB_TOKEN}  ← Resolved from env           │
  │                                                              │
  │  KUBERNETES IMPLEMENTATION:                                  │
  │  apiVersion: v1                                              │
  │  kind: Secret                                                │
  │  metadata:                                                   │
  │    name: backstage-secrets                                   │
  │  stringData:                                                 │
  │    GITHUB_TOKEN: ghp_xxxx                                    │
  │    DATABASE_URL: postgres://...                               │
  │  ---                                                         │
  │  # In Deployment:                                            │
  │  envFrom:                                                    │
  │    - secretRef:                                              │
  │        name: backstage-secrets                               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: All secrets (GitHub token, DB password, OAuth secrets) are stored in a Kubernetes Secret named backstage-secrets on the kubeadm cluster. The Secret is created via a separate, access-controlled process — it's never in the Git repo. The Deployment references it via envFrom.

Infrastructure Q59 of 200
What is the Backstage Scaffolder security model?
A) Anyone who can access the Backstage UI can execute any scaffolder template — there are no restrictions
B) Scaffolder templates run arbitrary shell commands provided by the user in the template parameters
C) Templates can be restricted by RBAC (who can execute). Actions are allow-listed (only registered actions can run). Parameters are validated against a schema. All actions run server-side — no arbitrary code execution is exposed to the UI
D) The Scaffolder is disabled by default in production — you must set `scaffolder.enabled: true` and provide a security review for each template
✅ Correct Answer: C

The Scaffolder has a defense-in-depth security model: (1) RBAC — only authorized users/groups can execute specific templates (via the permissions framework), (2) Action allow-listing — only registered scaffolder actions can be invoked (no arbitrary code execution), (3) Parameter validation — all template parameters are validated against JSON Schema before execution, (4) Server-side execution — all actions run on the backend, never in the browser.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Scaffolder Security — Defense in Depth
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SCAFFOLDER SECURITY LAYERS                                  │
  │                                                              │
  │  LAYER 1: AUTHENTICATION                                     │
  │  → User must be logged in to access /create                  │
  │                                                              │
  │  LAYER 2: RBAC (Permissions Framework)                       │
  │  → Only users with `scaffolder.template.execute` permission   │
  │    can run templates                                         │
  │  → Can restrict per-template: "only team-leads can create    │
  │    production services"                                      │
  │                                                              │
  │  LAYER 3: PARAMETER VALIDATION                               │
  │  → All inputs validated against JSON Schema                  │
  │  → No SQL injection, no path traversal                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  LAYER 4: ACTION ALLOW-LISTING                               │
  │  → Only registered actions can execute                       │
  │  → Custom actions must be explicitly added to backend        │
  │  → No arbitrary code execution from the UI                   │
  │                                                              │
  │  LAYER 5: SERVER-SIDE EXECUTION                              │
  │  → All actions run on the backend, never in browser          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The "Create Django Service" template is restricted to the anihpj-team group via RBAC. Only registered actions like fetch:template, publish:github, and catalog:register can execute — no arbitrary shell access. All parameter inputs (service name, port, database name) are validated before execution.

Infrastructure Q60 of 200
How do you set up CI/CD for Backstage itself?
A) Use GitHub Actions (or GitLab CI): `yarn install` → `yarn tsc` → `yarn test` → `yarn lint` → `yarn build` → `docker build` → `docker push` → deploy to K8s. Can use ArgoCD for the GitOps deployment step

D) Use the Backstage SaaS platform at backstage.io — it handles all CI/CD automatically
✅ Correct Answer: A

Backstage is software you deploy yourself, so you need your own CI/CD pipeline. A typical GitHub Actions workflow: (1) yarn install — install dependencies, (2) yarn tsc — TypeScript type-check, (3) yarn test — run Jest tests, (4) yarn lint — ESLint check, (5) yarn build — compile the production bundle, (6) docker build — create container image, (7) docker push — push to registry, (8) deploy to K8s (kubectl apply or ArgoCD sync).

  │  │ ② yarn tsc --noEmit         ← TypeScript check        │    │

  │  │                                                           │

  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │ DEPLOY (choose one approach):                         │    │
  │  │  Option A: kubectl set image deployment/backstage     │    │
  │  │  Option B: ArgoCD sync (GitOps)                      │    │
  │  │     → Update K8s manifest in Git repo                 │    │
  │  │     → ArgoCD detects change and syncs                 │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: GitHub Actions runs the CI pipeline on every PR. Merged PRs trigger the full build → docker push → ArgoCD sync to the kubeadm cluster. The entire pipeline from merge to production deployment takes ~12 minutes.

Infrastructure Q61 of 200
What is the structure of app-config.yaml?

Infrastructure Q62 of 200
How do you configure the Catalog in app-config.yaml?
A) The Catalog needs no configuration — it auto-discovers all entities in your network
B) Under `catalog.providers`, configure entity providers (github, gitlab, bitbucket) with organization, schedule (scan frequency), and filters. Under `catalog.rules`, set allow/deny rules for entity kinds. Under `catalog.locations`, add static YAML URLs
C) Catalog configuration is done in the Backstage UI at /catalog/settings — not in app-config.yaml
D) The catalog is configured via TypeScript in `packages/backend/src/catalog.ts` — app-config.yaml only stores database connection details
✅ Correct Answer: B

Catalog configuration in app-config.yaml lives under the catalog: key with three main sub-sections: providers — where to find entities (GitHub org, GitLab group, Bitbucket workspace) and how often to scan (schedule using cron syntax), rules — which entity kinds to allow or deny (e.g., only allow Component, API, Resource), and locations — static URLs pointing to catalog-info.yaml files.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Catalog Configuration — app-config.yaml
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CATALOG CONFIGURATION                                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  catalog:                                                    │
  │    # Entity providers — WHERE to find entities               │
  │    providers:                                                │
  │      github:                                                 │
  │        organization: anihpj         ← GitHub org             │
  │        schedule:                    ← Scan frequency         │
  │          frequency: { minutes: 30 } ← Every 30 min           │
  │          timeout:   { minutes: 3 }                           │
  │        filters:                                              │
  │          repository: 'jobpost-.*'   ← Only matching repos    │
  │                                                              │
  │    # Rules — WHAT entity kinds to allow                      │
  │    rules:                                                    │
  │      - allow: [Component, API, Resource, System, Domain]     │
  │                                                              │
  │    # Static locations — direct YAML URLs                     │
  │    locations:                                                │
  │      - type: url                                             │
  │        target: https://github.com/anihpj/jobpost/blob/main/  │
  │                catalog/catalog-info.yaml                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The catalog is configured to scan the anihpj GitHub organization every 30 minutes, looking for catalog-info.yaml files in each repo. Entity kinds are restricted to Component, API, Resource, System, and Domain. The jobpost API spec is registered as a static location.

How do you configure Authentication in app-config.yaml?
A) Authentication is hardcoded — Backstage only supports GitHub OAuth and cannot be changed
B) Under `auth.providers`, configure one or more providers: github, gitlab, google, okta, microsoft, oauth2 (generic), oidc, or saml. Each needs clientId, clientSecret, and optional callbacks. Use `auth.environment` to switch between dev/prod config

D) Authentication is configured in the Backstage database after first startup — the setup wizard guides you through it
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage supports many authentication providers configured under auth.providers in app-config.yaml. Each provider (github, gitlab, google, okta, microsoft, oauth2, oidc, saml) needs clientId and clientSecret. The auth.environment field (development or production) determines which config subset is used. You can also configure sign-in resolvers to map external identities to Backstage users and groups.

  │          clientId: ${GITHUB_CLIENT_ID}                       │

  │                                                              │
  │  SUPPORTED PROVIDERS:                                        │
  │  🔹 github    🔹 gitlab    🔹 google    🔹 microsoft         │

For anihpj/jobpost: The production deployment uses GitHub OAuth (since all developers already have GitHub accounts). Client ID and secret are injected via Kubernetes Secrets, never hardcoded. The sign-in resolver maps GitHub usernames to Backstage users, and GitHub teams to Backstage groups.
