2.3 Configuration — Proxy, Environment Overrides & Plugin ConfigQ65-Q72

Infrastructure Q65 of 200
How do you configure the Proxy plugin in app-config.yaml?
A) Under `proxy.endpoints`, define a map of path → target. Backstage proxies requests from `/api/proxy/` to the target URL. This securely exposes internal APIs through Backstage without CORS issues
B) The proxy is configured via environment variables: `PROXY_GRAFANA_URL`, `PROXY_PAGERDUTY_URL`, etc.
C) Proxy configuration is done in the Backstage UI at /admin/proxy — you add endpoints through a web form
D) Backstage doesn't have a proxy — all plugin API calls go directly from the browser to the target service
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Backstage proxy plugin acts as a reverse proxy — the browser calls /api/proxy/grafana, and the backend forwards the request to the actual Grafana server. This solves CORS issues (browsers block cross-origin requests) and hides internal URLs from the client. Configured in proxy.endpoints as a map of path → target URL.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Proxy Plugin — How It Works
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PROXY PLUGIN CONFIGURATION                                  │
  │  proxy:                                                      │
  │    endpoints:                                                │
  │      '/grafana':                                             │
  │        target: https://grafana.anihpj.com                    │
  │        headers:                                              │
  │          Authorization: Bearer ${GRAFANA_TOKEN}               │
  │      '/pagerduty':                                           │
  │        target: https://api.pagerduty.com                     │
  │        headers:                                              │
  │          Authorization: Token token=${PAGERDUTY_TOKEN}        │
  │  HOW IT WORKS:                                               │
  │  Browser → GET /api/proxy/grafana/...                        │
  │  Backend → Forwards + adds auth → https://grafana.anihpj.com  │
  │  ✅ No CORS issues — browser only talks to Backstage          │
  │  ✅ Internal URLs hidden from client                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The proxy plugin securely exposes Grafana dashboards, PagerDuty incidents, and Sentry error data — all internal tools that would otherwise be inaccessible from the browser due to CORS. Auth tokens are injected server-side.

Infrastructure Q66 of 200
How do environment variables override app-config.yaml values?
A) Environment variables cannot override app-config.yaml — the YAML file always takes precedence
B) Use the `APP_CONFIG_` prefix. Dot-separated YAML paths become underscore-separated: `app.baseUrl` → `APP_CONFIG_app_baseUrl`. Arrays use double underscores. Env vars have HIGHEST priority
C) Use the `BACKSTAGE_` prefix: `BACKSTAGE_app_baseUrl`, `BACKSTAGE_database_url`, etc.
D) Environment variables override only if you add `env: true` to each key in app-config.yaml
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage uses the APP_CONFIG_ prefix for environment variable overrides — a convention borrowed from Node.js frameworks but with Backstage-specific rules. The dot-notation YAML path is converted to uppercase underscore-separated: app.baseUrl → APP_CONFIG_app_baseUrl, backend.cors.origin → APP_CONFIG_backend_cors_origin. For array indices, use a single underscore before the number and double underscores between array number and property: catalog.rules[0].allow → APP_CONFIG_catalog_rules_0__allow. Environment variables have ABSOLUTE HIGHEST PRIORITY in the config resolution chain: APP_CONFIG_* env vars > app-config.local.yaml (gitignored) > app-config..yaml (e.g., production) > app-config.yaml (base, committed to Git). This is critical for Kubernetes deployments: you set secrets and environment-specific values via K8s Secrets/ConfigMaps injected as environment variables, and they override everything in the YAML files. This prevents accidentally deploying staging configs to production.

📝 Detailed Explanation
APP_CONFIG_ Naming Rules & Priority
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  NAMING RULES: YAML PATH → ENV VAR                          ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  app.title              → APP_CONFIG_app_title              ║
  ║  app.baseUrl            → APP_CONFIG_app_baseUrl            ║
  ║  backend.cors.origin    → APP_CONFIG_backend_cors_origin    ║
  ║  database.connection    → APP_CONFIG_database_connection    ║
  ║  catalog.rules[0].allow → APP_CONFIG_catalog_rules_0__allow ║
  ║  (arrays: _N__property  — single underscore before index,   ║
  ║   double underscore between index and property name)        ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  CONFIG RESOLUTION PRIORITY (highest to lowest):            ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  ① APP_CONFIG_* env vars          ← K8s Secrets/ConfigMaps  ║
  ║  ② app-config.local.yaml          ← gitignored, local dev   ║
  ║  ③ app-config.{env}.yaml          ← production / staging    ║
  ║  ④ app-config.yaml                ← base config, in Git     ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

For anihpj/jobpost: In the 6-cluster Kubernetes lab, the production kubeadm cluster injects APP_CONFIG_backend_database_connection (PostgreSQL connection string with password) and APP_CONFIG_integrations_github_token via Kubernetes Secrets mounted as environment variables. These override the placeholder values in app-config.production.yaml. The staging kind cluster injects different values for the staging database. Developers' local minikube uses app-config.local.yaml with localhost database credentials — no env vars needed. This layered approach ensures secrets never appear in Git and each environment gets its correct configuration.

Infrastructure Q67 of 200
What is the configuration schema in Backstage and how does it work?
A) There is no schema — app-config.yaml accepts any arbitrary YAML keys
B) Configuration is validated by TypeScript type definitions at compile time — not at runtime
C) Each plugin defines a config schema using `@backstage/config-loader` JSON Schema. Backstage validates app-config.yaml at startup. Missing required config causes a clear error — Backstage won't start with invalid config
D) Config schema validation is an optional plugin you must install separately
✅ Correct Answer: C

Backstage has a built-in configuration schema system that acts like a contract between plugins and app-config.yaml. Every plugin declares exactly what configuration keys it expects, their data types (string, number, boolean, object, array), default values, and which keys are required. At startup, Backstage's config-loader reads ALL plugin schemas, validates app-config.yaml against them, and either starts normally (✅) or fails immediately with a clear, actionable error message (❌).

This is fundamentally different from most frameworks where invalid config silently causes runtime bugs. Backstage's approach means you can't deploy with broken configuration — the pod won't enter the Ready state until the config is valid. This is a fail-fast design philosophy borrowed from Kubernetes itself (where invalid YAML is rejected by the API server before being persisted).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Config Schema Validation — How It Works at Startup
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BACKSTAGE STARTUP — CONFIG VALIDATION                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  STEP 1: Plugin defines schema (config.d.ts or schema.json)  │
  │  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────        │
  │  const catalogSchema = {                                     │
  │    type: 'object',                                           │
  │    required: ['providers'],  ← MUST be present               │
  │    properties: {                                             │
  │      providers: {                                            │
  │        type: 'object',                                       │
  │        properties: {                                         │
  │          github: {                                           │
  │            type: 'object',                                   │
  │            required: ['organization'],                       │
  │            properties: {                                     │
  │              organization: { type: 'string' },               │
  │              schedule: {                                     │
  │                type: 'object',                               │
  │                default: { frequency: { minutes: 30 } }       │
  │              }                                               │
  │            }                                                 │
  │          }                                                   │
  │        }                                                     │
  │      }                                                       │
  │    }                                                         │
  │  };                                                          │
  │                                                              │
  │  STEP 2: Startup loads all schemas → validates config        │
  │  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────        │
  │  app-config.yaml ──► @backstage/config-loader                │
  │                          │                                   │
  │          ┌───────────────┼───────────────┐                   │
  │          ▼               ▼               ▼                   │
  │    catalog.schema   auth.schema    scaffolder.schema         │
  │          │               │               │                   │
  │          └───────────────┼───────────────┘                   │
  │                          ▼                                   │
  │                   VALIDATION RESULT                          │
  │                                                              │
  │  ✅ VALID CONFIG → Backstage starts normally                  │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐       │
  │  │ All required keys present, types correct           │       │
  │  │ Defaults applied where values omitted              │       │
  │  │ Backend binds to port 7007, catalog starts         │       │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
  │                                                              │
  │  ❌ INVALID CONFIG → Backstage REFUSES to start              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐       │
  │  │ ERROR: "Missing required config value at           │       │
  │  │        'catalog.providers'. This key is required   │       │
  │  │        by the catalog plugin. Add it to your       │       │
  │  │        app-config.yaml to fix this error."         │       │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
  │  → Kubernetes pod stays in CrashLoopBackOff                  │
  │  → Readiness probe fails at /healthcheck                     │
  │  → Traffic never reaches this pod                            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When the platform team deploys a new Backstage version, config schema validation catches missing or renamed config keys at startup — BEFORE the pod is marked Ready. If a breaking change renames catalog.providers to catalog.ingestion, Backstage refuses to start with a clear error, preventing a broken deployment from receiving production traffic on the kubeadm cluster.

Infrastructure Q68 of 200
How does Backstage handle configuration for different environments?
A) app-config.yaml (base) → app-config..yaml (env override) → app-config.local.yaml (gitignored) → APP_CONFIG_* env vars (highest). Layers merge, later overrides earlier
B) You maintain separate app-config files per environment and swap them manually before deployment
C) Backstage uses a single app-config.yaml with inline conditionals that switch based on NODE_ENV
D) Environment configuration is stored in the PostgreSQL database and loaded at runtime
✅ Correct Answer: A

Backstage uses a layered configuration system — think of it like transparent sheets stacked on top of each other. Each layer can override values from the layers below. This design means you can have ONE base config shared across all environments, then layer environment-specific overrides on top, and finally inject secrets at the highest layer without ever committing them to Git.

The four layers, from lowest priority to highest: (1) app-config.yaml — committed to Git, defines the shared base, (2) app-config.<env>.yaml — environment-specific overrides (production, staging, development), (3) app-config.local.yaml — developer-local overrides, gitignored, never shared, (4) APP_CONFIG_* environment variables — the HIGHEST priority layer, used exclusively for secrets and deployment-specific values.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Configuration Layering — 4 Levels of Override Priority
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CONFIG LAYERING (Transparent Sheets Model)                  │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  LAYER 4: APP_CONFIG_* ENV VARS   🟢 HIGHEST        │    │
  │  │  APP_CONFIG_backend_database_connection=postgres://..│    │
  │  │  GITHUB_TOKEN=ghp_xxxx                               │    │
  │  │  AUTH_GITHUB_CLIENT_SECRET=xxxx                       │    │
  │  │  → Overrides EVERYTHING below                         │    │
  │  │  → ONLY place for secrets                             │    │
  │  │  → In K8s: injected from Secret → envFrom             │    │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                          ▲ OVERRIDES                         │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  LAYER 3: app-config.local.yaml   🟡 GITIGNORED     │    │
  │  │  catalog:                                             │    │
  │  │    providers:                                         │    │
  │  │      github:                                          │    │
  │  │        organization: dev-org   ← Dev's personal org   │    │
  │  │  → Developer-local overrides                          │    │
  │  │  → In .gitignore — NEVER committed                    │    │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                          ▲ OVERRIDES                         │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  LAYER 2: app-config.production.yaml                 │    │
  │  │  app:                                                 │    │
  │  │    baseUrl: https://backstage.anihpj.com              │    │
  │  │  backend:                                             │    │
  │  │    database:                                          │    │
  │  │      client: pg    ← Override SQLite to PostgreSQL    │    │
  │  │  → Environment-specific overrides                     │    │
  │  │  → Committed to Git (no secrets!)                     │    │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                          ▲ OVERRIDES                         │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  LAYER 1: app-config.yaml   🔵 BASE (in Git)        │    │
  │  │  app:                                                 │    │
  │  │    title: Anihpj Portal                               │    │
  │  │  backend:                                             │    │
  │  │    database:                                          │    │
  │  │      client: better-sqlite3  ← Default for all envs   │    │
  │  │  → Shared base configuration                          │    │
  │  │  → Committed to Git                                   │    │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                              │
  │  RESULT (after merging all 4 layers):                        │
  │  app.title = "Anihpj Portal"          (from Layer 1)         │
  │  app.baseUrl = "https://backstage..."  (from Layer 2)        │
  │  backend.database.client = "pg"        (from Layer 2)        │
  │  backend.database.connection = "postgres://..."(Layer 4)     │
  │  github.token = "ghp_xxxx"             (from Layer 4)        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team keeps app-config.yaml in Git as the shared base. app-config.production.yaml overrides the database to PostgreSQL and the baseUrl to backstage.anihpj.com. Each developer has their own gitignored app-config.local.yaml with their personal GitHub token. The Kubernetes Secret injects APP_CONFIG_backend_database_connection with the actual PostgreSQL URL at the highest priority level.

Infrastructure Q69 of 200
Where do you configure the Backstage organization name and logo?
A) In the PostgreSQL database, using the /admin/settings UI page after first login
B) In `packages/app/public/index.html` — edit the <title> tag and <link rel="icon"> directly
C) In app-config.yaml: `app.title` (org name) and `app.branding` (logo). Logo files go in `packages/app/public/`. Full theme via `createApp()` with custom MUI theme in `packages/app/src/apis.ts`
D) Via the Backstage CLI: `yarn backstage-cli config:branding`
✅ Correct Answer: C

Branding in Backstage happens in three places: (1) app-config.yaml — sets the portal title via app.title and the base URL via app.baseUrl, (2) Static assets in packages/app/public/ — logo SVGs, favicon, and any custom images, (3) MUI Theme customization in packages/app/src/apis.ts — this is where the REAL theming power lives: colors, typography, spacing, dark/light mode, and custom component overrides via createApp().

The beauty of MUI theming is that once you define a custom theme, every plugin automatically inherits it. You don't need to style the GitHub plugin or Grafana plugin separately — they all render with your organization's brand colors, fonts, and spacing because they use MUI's ThemeProvider. This is a huge time-saver compared to platforms where each plugin needs independent styling.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Branding & Theming — Three Layers of Customization
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LAYER 1: app-config.yaml (title + baseUrl)                  │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────                       │
  │  app:                                                        │
  │    title: Anihpj Developer Portal    ← Browser tab title     │
  │    baseUrl: https://backstage.anihpj.com                     │
  │                                                              │
  │  LAYER 2: Static Assets (packages/app/public/)               │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────                   │
  │  ├── logo.svg          ← Portal header logo (left side)      │
  │  ├── logo-full.svg     ← Full logo (sidebar, login page)    │
  │  ├── icon.svg          ← Favicon (browser tab icon)          │
  │  └── banner.png        ← Optional: custom images             │
  │                                                              │
  │  LAYER 3: MUI Theme (packages/app/src/apis.ts)               │
  │  ───────────────────────────────────────────                 │
  │  import { createApp } from '@backstage/app-defaults';        │
  │  import { anihpjTheme } from './themes/anihpjTheme';         │
  │                                                              │
  │  const app = createApp({                                     │
  │    themes: [{                                                │
  │      id: 'anihpj-dark',                                      │
  │      title: 'Anihpj Dark',                                   │
  │      variant: 'dark',                                        │
  │      provider: ({ children }) => (                           │
  │        <ThemeProvider theme={anihpjTheme}>                  │
  │          {children}                                           │
  │        </ThemeProvider>                                     │
  │      ),                                                      │
  │    }],                                                       │
  │  });                                                         │
  │                                                              │
  │  THEME INHERITANCE (automatic!):                             │
  │  ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐ ┌──────────┐       │
  │  │ GitHub   │ │ Grafana  │ │PagerDuty │ │Kubernetes│       │
  │  │ plugin   │ │ plugin   │ │ plugin   │ │ plugin   │       │
  │  └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘ └────┬─────┘       │
  │       └─────────────┼─────────────┼─────────────┘           │
  │                     ▼             ▼                          │
  │           ALL use anihpjTheme (MUI ThemeProvider)            │
  │           → Same colors, fonts, spacing everywhere           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The portal is branded "Anihpj Developer Portal" with a custom dark theme. The anihpjTheme in apis.ts sets the primary color to anihpj blue (#1a56db) and secondary to anihpj green (#10b981). Every installed plugin — GitHub, Grafana, PagerDuty, Kubernetes — renders with anihpj branding automatically through MUI's ThemeProvider.

Infrastructure Q70 of 200
How do you configure CORS (Cross-Origin Resource Sharing) in Backstage?
A) CORS is enabled by default for all origins — no configuration needed
B) In app-config.yaml under `backend.cors.origin`, specify allowed origins. Dev: `http://localhost:3000`. Prod: your Backstage domain. Multiple origins can be listed as an array
C) CORS is configured via the NGINX Ingress annotation — not in Backstage itself
D) CORS is not supported in Backstage — all requests must use the proxy plugin
✅ Correct Answer: B

CORS (Cross-Origin Resource Sharing) is a browser security mechanism that blocks web pages from making requests to a different domain than the one that served the page. In Backstage, this matters because the React frontend runs in the browser and makes API calls to the Node.js backend. If these are on different origins (different ports in dev, or different domains), the browser blocks the requests unless the backend explicitly allows them via CORS headers.

Configuration is in backend.cors.origin in app-config.yaml. In development, the frontend dev server runs on localhost:3000 while the backend runs on localhost:7007 — these are different origins (different ports), so CORS must allow port 3000. In production, both frontend and backend are typically served from the same domain (via Ingress), so CORS is simpler — just allow the production domain.

📝 Detailed Explanation
CORS in Backstage — Dev vs Production
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  WHAT IS CORS?                                               │
  │                                                              │
  │  Browser (frontend JS) ──GET /api/catalog──► Backend API     │
  │  Origin: localhost:3000             Expected: localhost:7007  │
  │                                                              │
  │  If origins DIFFER → Browser sends preflight OPTIONS request │
  │  Backend MUST respond with:                                  │
  │    Access-Control-Allow-Origin: http://localhost:3000        │
  │  Otherwise → Browser BLOCKS the request (CORS error)         │
  │                                                              │
  │  ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────     │
  │                                                              │
  │  🔧 DEVELOPMENT (different ports = different origins)        │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐       │
  │  │  Browser                          Backend         │       │
  │  │  localhost:3000 ──✗ CORS ✗──► localhost:7007     │       │
  │  │  (React frontend)                (Express API)    │       │
  │  │                                                   │       │
  │  │  FIX: backend.cors.origin: http://localhost:3000   │       │
  │  │  → Backend adds CORS header: "Allow port 3000"     │       │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
  │                                                              │
  │  🟢 PRODUCTION (same domain via Ingress)                     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐       │
  │  │  Browser                          Ingress          │       │
  │  │  backstage.anihpj.com ──────────► backstage.anihpj │      │
  │  │  (React frontend, :443)           → Backend :7007  │       │
  │  │                                                   │       │
  │  │  Same origin = NO CORS issue!                     │       │
  │  │  backend.cors.origin: https://backstage.anihpj.com │       │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
  │                                                              │
  │  app-config.yaml:                                            │
  │  backend:                                                    │
  │    cors:                                                     │
  │      origin: https://backstage.anihpj.com                    │
  │      methods: [GET, POST, PUT, DELETE, PATCH]                │
  │      allowedHeaders: [Authorization, Content-Type]           │
  │      credentials: true    ← Allow cookies/auth tokens        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: In production, CORS origin is set to https://backstage.anihpj.com since the Ingress at the kubeadm cluster serves both frontend and backend from the same domain. In development, developers configure http://localhost:3000 because the React dev server (port 3000) and Express backend (port 7007) are on different ports — without this CORS setting, the browser would block all API calls.

Infrastructure Q71 of 200
How do you configure TechDocs in Backstage?
A) TechDocs works automatically — no configuration needed in app-config.yaml
B) TechDocs is configured via the Backstage UI at /docs/settings
C) TechDocs only needs `techdocs.generator` — either 'local' or 'docker'
D) `techdocs.builder` (how docs are built), `techdocs.publisher` (where built HTML is stored: local, GCS, S3, Azure), and `techdocs.generator` (MkDocs is default). All three must be configured
✅ Correct Answer: D

TechDocs turns your repository's Markdown files into a beautiful documentation site embedded directly in Backstage. It's a three-stage pipeline: (1) Builder — takes docs/*.md + mkdocs.yml from your repo and runs MkDocs to generate static HTML, (2) Publisher — stores the generated HTML in object storage (local filesystem, AWS S3, Google GCS, or Azure Blob), (3) Generator — the documentation engine (default is MkDocs with the mkdocs-techdocs-core plugin, which adds Backstage-specific extensions like Mermaid diagrams and admonitions).

Each of these three stages is independently configurable in app-config.yaml. For production, a common pattern is: build with Docker (isolated, no dependency conflicts), publish to S3 (durable, scalable), and serve from the Backstage backend.

📝 Detailed Explanation
TechDocs — Complete Build & Serve Pipeline
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TECHDOCS PIPELINE (3 Stages)                                │
  │                                                              │
  │  STAGE 1: SOURCE (Developer's Git Repo)                      │
  │  ───────────────────────────────────                         │
  │  jobpost-api/                                                │
  │  ├── docs/                                                   │
  │  │   ├── index.md          ← Documentation homepage          │
  │  │   ├── api-reference.md  ← API endpoints                   │
  │  │   ├── architecture.md   ← System design                   │
  │  │   └── runbooks/         ← Incident response guides        │
  │  │       └── database-outage.md                              │
  │  └── mkdocs.yml            ← MkDocs configuration            │
  │                                                              │
  │  STAGE 2: BUILDER (MkDocs + mkdocs-techdocs-core)            │
  │  ─────────────────────────────────────────────               │
  │  techdocs:                                                   │
  │    builder: 'local'        ← Build on Backstage server       │
  │    generator:                                                │
  │      runIn: 'docker'       ← Run MkDocs in Docker container  │
  │      dockerImage: 'spotify/techdocs:v1.2.0'                  │
  │                                                              │
  │  docs/*.md + mkdocs.yml ──► MkDocs ──► Static HTML + CSS     │
  │                                                              │
  │  STAGE 3: PUBLISHER (Object Storage)                         │
  │  ───────────────────────────────────                         │
  │  techdocs:                                                   │
  │    publisher:                                                │
  │      type: 'awsS3'          ← Store in S3 bucket             │
  │      awsS3:                                                  │
  │        bucketName: 'anihpj-techdocs'                         │
  │        credentials:                                          │
  │          accessKeyId: ${AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID}                   │
  │          secretAccessKey: ${AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY}           │
  │                                                              │
  │  STAGE 4: SERVE (Backstage UI)                               │
  │  ─────────────────────────────                               │
  │  Developer visits:                                            │
  │  backstage.anihpj.com/docs/default/component/jobpost-api     │
  │  → Backend fetches HTML from S3                              │
  │  → Renders in Backstage UI with navigation sidebar           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: TechDocs uses the 'local' builder (MkDocs in Docker) and 'awsS3' publisher. Each service's docs/ folder contains MkDocs-formatted Markdown. When a developer pushes docs changes to the jobpost-api repo, TechDocs detects the change (via the catalog refresh) and rebuilds the documentation, publishing the updated HTML to the S3 bucket automatically.

Infrastructure Q72 of 200
How do you configure the Kubernetes plugin in Backstage?
A) `kubernetes.clusterLocatorMethods` (how to find clusters: 'config', 'gke', 'eks', 'aks'). `kubernetes.serviceLocatorMethod` (how to map K8s resources to entities). A ServiceAccount with RBAC is required for read access
B) The Kubernetes plugin auto-discovers clusters — just install with `yarn add @backstage/plugin-kubernetes`
C) Kubernetes config is via kubectl — Backstage reads ~/.kube/config
D) Configured via the Backstage UI at /kubernetes/settings
✅ Correct Answer: A

The Kubernetes plugin is what makes Backstage a true Internal Developer Portal — it pulls LIVE data from your Kubernetes clusters and displays it directly on entity pages. Configuration has two critical parts: (1) clusterLocatorMethods — HOW to discover and connect to clusters (via kubeconfig, GKE API, EKS API, or AKS API), and (2) serviceLocatorMethod — HOW to map running K8s resources back to catalog entities so the right pods show on the right entity page.

A ServiceAccount with RBAC is required for each cluster. The minimum RBAC permissions are read-only on pods, services, deployments, replicasets, and configmaps. Backstage uses this ServiceAccount token (or a kubeconfig file) to authenticate to the cluster's API server and fetch resource data.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubernetes Plugin — Multi-Cluster Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  KUBERNETES PLUGIN CONFIGURATION                             │
  │                                                              │
  │  app-config.yaml:                                            │
  │  kubernetes:                                                 │
  │    clusterLocatorMethods:                                    │
  │      - type: 'config'           ← Reads from kubeconfig      │
  │        clusters:                                             │
  │          - name: 'kubeadm-prod'  ← Production cluster        │
  │            url: https://kubernetes.anihpj.com:6443           │
  │            authProvider: 'serviceAccount'                    │
  │            serviceAccountToken: ${K8S_PROD_TOKEN}            │
  │            skipTLSVerify: false                              │
  │          - name: 'kind-staging'  ← Staging cluster           │
  │            url: https://kind-staging.anihpj.com:6443         │
  │            serviceAccountToken: ${K8S_STAGING_TOKEN}         │
  │          - name: 'kind-dev'      ← Dev cluster               │
  │            url: https://kind-dev.anihpj.com:6443             │
  │            serviceAccountToken: ${K8S_DEV_TOKEN}             │
  │    serviceLocatorMethod:                                     │
  │      type: 'multiTenant'  ← Each entity sees its own K8s data│
  │                                                              │
  │  HOW IT WORKS AT RUNTIME:                                    │
  │  ┌──────────────┐                                            │
  │  │ Backstage    │  ──GET /api/kubernetes/services──►         │
  │  │ Backend      │       │ K8s API (kubeadm)                  │
  │  └──────────────┘       │ K8s API (kind-staging)             │
  │                         │ K8s API (kind-dev)                 │
  │                                                              │
  │  REQUIRED RBAC (ServiceAccount):                             │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐       │
  │  │ apiVersion: rbac.authorization.k8s.io/v1           │       │
  │  │ kind: ClusterRole                                   │       │
  │  │ metadata:                                           │       │
  │  │   name: backstage-reader                            │       │
  │  │ rules:                                              │       │
  │  │   - apiGroups: [""]                                │       │
  │  │     resources: [pods, services, configmaps]         │       │
  │  │     verbs: [get, list, watch]                       │       │
  │  │   - apiGroups: [apps]                               │       │
  │  │     resources: [deployments, replicasets]           │       │
  │  │     verbs: [get, list, watch]                       │       │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Kubernetes plugin connects to all 6 lab clusters (kubeadm → 1, kind clusters → 2-5, minikube → 6). Each cluster has a dedicated ServiceAccount with read-only RBAC. The backstage.io/kubernetes-id annotation on the jobpost-api entity page maps to the actual running pods in the kubeadm production cluster, showing live pod status, replica counts, and service endpoints.

2.4 Deploy — Docker Images, Strategies & RollbacksQ73-Q80

Infrastructure Q73 of 200
How do you build a Docker image for Backstage?
A) Backstage provides pre-built images on Docker Hub — just `docker pull backstageio/backstage:latest`
B) Run `docker build .` with the default Dockerfile from create-app — no special steps needed
C) Run `yarn build` (compiles TypeScript + bundles frontend) then `yarn build-image` (creates optimized Docker image). The image runs as non-root and includes only production dependencies
D) Use `npx @backstage/build-docker` — a separate tool that builds the image from the Backstage SaaS platform
✅ Correct Answer: C

The production build is a two-step process: (1) yarn build — compiles all TypeScript, bundles the React frontend, runs linting and tests, (2) yarn build-image — creates an optimized Docker image using the Dockerfile in your Backstage project. The image runs as non-root user, includes only production dependencies (no devDependencies), and is based on Node.js slim/alpine.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Build Pipeline — From Source to Container
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  yarn build                   ← Compile & bundle             │
  │  ├── yarn tsc                  ← TypeScript → JavaScript     │
  │  ├── yarn build:frontend       ← React → static bundle       │
  │  └── yarn build:backend        ← Backend TypeScript → JS     │
  │                                                              │
  │  yarn build-image             ← Create Docker image          │
  │  ├── Stage 1: Build (Node.js)                                │
  │  │   → yarn install --production (only prod deps)            │
  │  │   → Copy compiled output from yarn build                  │
  │  ├── Stage 2: Runtime (Node.js slim)                         │
  │  │   → Copy only production artifacts                        │
  │  │   → USER 1001:1001 (non-root)                             │
  │  │   → CMD ["node", "packages/backend"]                      │
  │  └── docker push registry.anihpj.com/backstage:v1.2.3        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The GitHub Actions CI pipeline runs `yarn build` → `yarn build-image` → `docker push`. The resulting image is tagged with the Git commit SHA and deployed to the kubeadm cluster.

Infrastructure Q74 of 200
What are the deployment strategies for Backstage?
A) Backstage can ONLY be deployed via Helm — it's the officially supported method
B) Helm chart (backstage/backstage), raw Kubernetes manifests (Deployment + Service + Ingress), Docker Compose (for small teams), or cloud-specific (AWS ECS, GCP Cloud Run). All are valid — Backstage is just a Node.js app
C) Backstage is deployed as a Kubernetes Operator — you install the operator and it manages Backstage automatically

Backstage is fundamentally a Node.js application — it can be deployed anywhere Node.js runs. Common strategies: Helm chart (packages all K8s resources), raw K8s manifests (kubectl apply), Docker Compose (for small teams or self-hosted), cloud services (AWS ECS, GCP Cloud Run, Azure Container Apps). No single method is "official" — choose what fits your infrastructure.

Deployment Options

Infrastructure Q75 of 200
How do you handle the Backstage database in production?
A) Use PostgreSQL (never SQLite in prod). Configure connection pooling (pg-pool or PgBouncer). Use Kubernetes StatefulSet with PVC for data persistence. Back up with pg_dump (cronjob). Migrations run automatically at startup
B) SQLite is fine for production — it's embedded and requires no external database server
C) Use MongoDB — Backstage supports MongoDB as an alternative to PostgreSQL via the `database.client: mongo` config
D) Backstage is stateless and needs no database — all data is stored in the catalog-info.yaml files in Git repos
✅ Correct Answer: A

Production Backstage requires PostgreSQL. Key considerations: Connection pooling (pg-pool or PgBouncer to handle multiple backend replicas), persistent storage (K8s PVC or cloud-managed disk), regular backups (pg_dump via cronjob — the DB is the only stateful component), Knex.js auto-migrations (run on startup, no manual intervention). Never use SQLite in production — it's single-writer and unsuitable for concurrent access.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Production Database Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐  ┌──────────┐                   │
  │  │ Backend  │  │ Backend  │  │ Backend  │  (3 replicas)     │
  │  │ Pod A    │  │ Pod B    │  │ Pod C    │                   │
  │  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘  └────┬─────┘                   │
  │       └──────────────┼──────────────┘                       │
  │                      │                                      │
  │              ┌───────▼───────┐                               │
  │              │ PgBouncer     │  ← Connection pooler          │
  │              │ (or pg-pool)  │                               │
  │              └───────┬───────┘                               │
  │                      │                                      │
  │              ┌───────▼───────┐                               │
  │              │ PostgreSQL 15 │  ← StatefulSet + PVC          │
  │              │ (primary)     │                               │
  │              └───────┬───────┘                               │
  │                      │                                      │
  │              ┌───────▼───────┐                               │
  │              │ pg_dump cron  │  ← Nightly backup             │
  │              │ → S3 bucket   │                               │
  │              └───────────────┘                               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: PostgreSQL 15 runs as a StatefulSet on the kubeadm cluster with a 20Gi PVC. PgBouncer handles connection pooling for 2 backend replicas. A K8s CronJob runs pg_dump nightly and pushes to S3.

Infrastructure Q76 of 200
How do you configure Backstage for containerized environments?
A) Set `containerized: true` in app-config.yaml — Backstage auto-adjusts all settings
B) No special configuration is needed — the same app-config.yaml works everywhere
C) Use a separate `app-config.container.yaml` — Backstage auto-detects Docker and loads it

Infrastructure Q77 of 200
How does Backstage handle static assets in production?
A) Static assets are served from a CDN — Backstage requires CloudFront or Cloudflare in production
B) The backend serves the compiled React frontend as static files. After `yarn build`, the frontend bundle is embedded in the backend package. A single Node.js process serves both API and UI — no separate web server needed
C) Static assets must be served by a separate NGINX container — Backstage backend cannot serve frontend files
D) Static assets are stored in PostgreSQL and served via the /api/assets endpoint
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage uses a monolithic serving model in production — a single Node.js process serves BOTH the React frontend and the Express API backend. During yarn build, the React frontend is compiled into static files (HTML, JS, CSS, images) and bundled into packages/backend/dist/static/. At runtime, the backend serves these static files alongside API routes. This is fundamentally different from typical microservice architectures where you'd have separate containers for frontend (NGINX) and backend (Node.js).

This design simplifies Kubernetes deployments to a single Deployment and Service — no separate frontend pod, no NGINX sidecar, no complex routing between containers. The backend checks if an incoming request matches an API route (/api/*) first; if not, it falls through to serving the static React bundle. This is the same pattern used by Next.js, Django with WhiteNoise, and other monolithic-but-modern frameworks.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Monolithic Serving — One Process, Two Roles
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SINGLE NODE.JS PROCESS (packages/backend)                   │
  │                                                              │
  │  INCOMING REQUEST ROUTING:                                   │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │  Request arrives at :7007                            │     │
  │  │         │                                            │     │
  │  │         ▼                                            │     │
  │  │  Is path /api/* ?                                    │     │
  │  │    │                                                 │     │
  │  │    ├── YES → Route to appropriate plugin backend     │     │
  │  │    │        /api/catalog/*    → Catalog backend       │     │
  │  │    │        /api/scaffolder/* → Scaffolder backend    │     │
  │  │    │        /api/auth/*       → Auth backend          │     │
  │  │    │                                                 │     │
  │  │    └── NO  → Serve static file from dist/static/     │     │
  │  │             /              → index.html (React app)   │     │
  │  │             /static/*.js   → JS bundles               │     │
  │  │             /static/*.css  → CSS stylesheets          │     │
  │  │             /logo.svg      → Static assets            │     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                                                              │
  │  ❌ OLD WAY (more complex):                                  │
  │  ┌──────────┐   ┌──────────┐                                 │
  │  │ NGINX    │───│ Node.js  │  Two containers, two services   │
  │  │ (static) │   │ (API)    │  Complex routing, more ops      │
  │  └──────────┘   └──────────┘                                 │
  │                                                              │
  │  ✅ BACKSTAGE WAY (simpler):                                  │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────┐                             │
  │  │  Node.js (static + API)     │  One container, one service │
  │  │  Serves BOTH from :7007     │  Simple K8s deployment      │
  │  └─────────────────────────────┘                             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The production Docker image contains the compiled React frontend bundled inside the backend package. The single container on the kubeadm cluster serves both backstage.anihpj.com (React UI) and all /api/* backend routes from one Node.js process on port 7007. No separate NGINX pod needed.

Infrastructure Q78 of 200
How do you configure health checks for Backstage?
A) Health checks are automatic — Kubernetes knows when Backstage is healthy without configuration
B) Backstage exposes no health endpoints — you must write a custom script that checks the process
C) Use the built-in `/healthcheck` endpoint (returns 200 when healthy). Configure K8s livenessProbe and readinessProbe to hit this endpoint. The backend also exposes `/.well-known` for service discovery
D) Health checks are configured via the Backstage UI at /admin/health — you set thresholds there
✅ Correct Answer: C

Backstage exposes a /healthcheck endpoint that returns HTTP 200 when the backend is healthy (database connected, all plugins initialized). In Kubernetes, configure livenessProbe (to restart crashed processes) and readinessProbe (to stop sending traffic until healthy) to hit this endpoint. The /.well-known endpoints provide service discovery metadata for load balancers and monitoring systems.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubernetes Health Probes
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  K8s DEPLOYMENT PROBES:                                      │
  │                                                              │
  │  livenessProbe:            ← "Is the process alive?"         │
  │    httpGet:                                                  │
  │      path: /healthcheck                                      │
  │      port: 7007                                              │
  │    initialDelaySeconds: 30   ← Wait for startup              │
  │    periodSeconds: 10          ← Check every 10s              │
  │    failureThreshold: 3        ← Restart after 3 failures     │
  │                                                              │
  │  readinessProbe:           ← "Can this pod receive traffic?" │
  │    httpGet:                                                  │
  │      path: /healthcheck                                      │
  │      port: 7007                                              │
  │    initialDelaySeconds: 10                                   │
  │    periodSeconds: 5                                          │
  │                                                              │
  │  What /healthcheck checks:                                   │
  │  ✅ Database connection alive                                │
  │  ✅ All plugins initialized                                  │
  │  ✅ No critical errors in memory                             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The kubeadm Deployment has liveness and readiness probes hitting /healthcheck. If the database connection drops, the readiness probe fails and the pod is removed from the Service — traffic goes only to healthy pods.

Infrastructure Q79 of 200
How do you roll back a Backstage deployment?
A) In Kubernetes: `kubectl rollout undo deployment/backstage`. With ArgoCD: revert the Git commit and sync. Database migrations must be reversible (Knex down migrations). Always test rollback in staging first
B) Run `yarn backstage-cli rollback` — it automatically reverts to the previous version
C) Restore the PostgreSQL database from backup — Backstage has no application-level rollback
D) Backstage auto-rolls back if the new version fails health checks 3 times
✅ Correct Answer: A

Rollback is a standard K8s operation: kubectl rollout undo deployment/backstage reverts to the previous ReplicaSet. With GitOps (ArgoCD), revert the manifest change in Git — ArgoCD detects and syncs. Database considerations: Knex migrations should have functional down functions. If migrations can't be reversed, restore from the pre-upgrade pg_dump backup.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Rollback Strategy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ROLLBACK APPROACHES:                                        │
  │                                                              │
  │  🚢 KUBERNETES:                                              │
  │  kubectl rollout undo deployment/backstage                   │
  │  → Reverts to previous ReplicaSet (previous image tag)       │
  │                                                              │
  │  🔄 GITOPS (ArgoCD):                                         │
  │  git revert                                      │
  │  git push                                                    │
  │  → ArgoCD detects change and syncs back                      │
  │                                                              │
  │  ⚠️ DATABASE MIGRATIONS:                                     │
  │  If migrations have 'down' functions:                        │
  │    → Automatically reversed on older code startup            │
  │  If migrations are irreversible:                             │
  │    → Restore from pre-upgrade pg_dump                        │
  │    → Plan: ALWAYS take DB backup before upgrade!             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team uses ArgoCD for GitOps rollbacks. Before every upgrade, a pg_dump is taken. If the upgrade fails in staging (kind cluster, ksw → 2), the issue is fixed before the production kubeadm cluster (ksw → 1) is touched.

Infrastructure Q80 of 200
How do you handle plugin dependencies in production builds?
A) Plugins are auto-installed at runtime — Backstage downloads them from npm on startup
B) All plugin dependencies are resolved at BUILD time. `yarn build` compiles everything into the backend bundle. Frontend plugins are bundled into the React app. Backend plugins are compiled into the Node.js backend. No runtime npm install
C) Plugins are loaded as dynamic `.so` files — they don't need to be compiled into the build
D) Each plugin runs as a separate microservice with its own Docker container
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage is NOT a plugin runtime that loads plugins dynamically. All plugins (frontend AND backend) are compile-time dependencies. During yarn build, frontend plugins are bundled into the React SPA, and backend plugins are compiled into the Node.js backend. The resulting Docker image contains everything — no runtime npm install, no dynamic loading. This makes the deployment a self-contained, immutable artifact.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Plugin Build Model — Compile-Time, Not Runtime
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  yarn build                                                  │
  │  ├── Frontend plugins (React components)                     │
  │  │   → Bundled into static JS/CSS via Webpack                │
  │  │   → Included in packages/backend/dist/static/             │
  │  ├── Backend plugins (Express routers + services)            │
  │  │   → Compiled from TypeScript → JavaScript                 │
  │  │   → Included in packages/backend/dist/                    │
  │  └── Result: One self-contained Node.js process              │
  │                                                              │
  │  ✅ NO runtime npm install                                   │
  │  ✅ NO dynamic plugin loading                                │
  │  ✅ Immutable Docker image                                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When the team adds a new plugin (like PagerDuty), they add it as a yarn dependency, import it in plugins.ts (frontend) and index.ts (backend), then rebuild. The new plugin is now part of the immutable Docker image — no runtime changes needed.

2.5 Operations — Troubleshooting, Logging & PerformanceQ81-Q92

Infrastructure Q81 of 200
How do you troubleshoot Backstage startup failures?
A) Check the Backstage UI at /admin/diagnostics — all startup errors are displayed there
B) Run `yarn diagnose` — a built-in diagnostic tool that identifies all startup issues
C) Check container logs (`kubectl logs` or `docker logs`). Common causes: missing/invalid app-config.yaml (schema validation), database connection refused, missing required env vars, port already in use, or plugin initialization errors. Structured winston logs show exact errors
D) Backstage writes startup errors to a file at /var/log/backstage/startup.log inside the container
✅ Correct Answer: C

Startup failures are diagnosed via container logs. Backstage uses winston structured logging — errors are JSON-formatted with timestamps, plugin names, and stack traces. Common causes: Config schema validation (missing required keys), database connection (wrong host/port/credentials), missing env vars (GITHUB_TOKEN, auth secrets), port conflicts, or plugin init failures.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Startup Failure Diagnosis
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl logs deployment/backstage --tail=100                │
  │                                                              │
  │  COMMON STARTUP ERRORS:                                      │
  │  ❌ "Missing required config at 'catalog.providers'"          │
  │     → app-config.yaml missing catalog section                │
  │  ❌ "connect ECONNREFUSED postgres-svc:5432"                  │
  │     → Database not reachable or wrong host                   │
  │  ❌ "GITHUB_TOKEN is not set"                                 │
  │     → Missing environment variable                           │
  │  ❌ "EADDRINUSE :::7007"                                      │
  │     → Port 7007 already in use                               │
  │  ❌ "Plugin 'catalog' failed to initialize"                   │
  │     → Check plugin-specific logs                             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When Backstage fails to start on kubeadm, the team runs `kubectl logs deployment/backstage -n backstage`. Winston JSON logs pinpoint whether it's a DB connection issue, missing config, or plugin failure.

Infrastructure Q82 of 200
How do you debug database connection issues in Backstage?
A) The Backstage UI shows a red banner when the database is disconnected — click it for details
B) Check logs for Knex.js errors ("connect ECONNREFUSED", "password authentication failed"). Verify DATABASE_URL or connection config. Test connectivity from within the container (kubectl exec). Check PostgreSQL pod status and pg_hba.conf
C) Run `yarn db:ping` — Backstage has a built-in database connectivity test command
D) Database issues are shown in the Kubernetes dashboard — Backstage is auto-instrumented
✅ Correct Answer: B

Database issues manifest as Knex.js connection errors in the logs. Steps: (1) Check logs for specific error (ECONNREFUSED = host/port wrong, authentication failed = credentials wrong, database doesn't exist = DB name wrong), (2) Verify DATABASE_URL or individual connection parameters, (3) kubectl exec into the Backstage pod and test with nc -zv postgres-svc 5432, (4) Check PostgreSQL pod status and logs.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Database Debugging Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl logs deployment/backstage | grep -i "database"      │
  │                                                              │
  │  ERROR PATTERNS:                                             │
  │  "connect ECONNREFUSED 10.96.1.5:5432"                       │
  │  → PostgreSQL service not reachable                          │
  │  → Fix: Check Service name, port, NetworkPolicy              │
  │                                                              │
  │  "password authentication failed for user 'backstage'"       │
  │  → Wrong password in DATABASE_URL                            │
  │  → Fix: Verify K8s Secret values                             │
  │                                                              │
  │  "database 'backstage_db' does not exist"                     │
  │  → Database not created                                      │
  │  → Fix: CREATE DATABASE backstage_db;                        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When the team encounters DB issues, they first verify the PostgreSQL StatefulSet is Running, then check the Secret's DATABASE_URL value matches the actual service name and credentials on the kubeadm cluster.

Infrastructure Q83 of 200
How do you troubleshoot catalog ingestion failures?
A) Check catalog logs (filter for "catalog" plugin). Verify GitHub token has permissions. Check catalog-info.yaml syntax. Look for entity processing errors (validation, stitching). Use `yarn backstage-cli config:check` to validate configuration
B) All catalog errors appear as red badges on entity pages in the Backstage UI
C) Run `yarn catalog:validate` — it scans all repos and reports exactly which catalog-info.yaml files are broken
D) Catalog ingestion failures silently skip entities — there's no way to detect them
✅ Correct Answer: A

Catalog ingestion failures are one of the most common Backstage issues because they involve multiple systems: GitHub API, YAML parsing, entity validation, and database storage. The debugging approach follows a pipeline mindset — trace the entity through each stage of the ingestion pipeline to find where it fails.

Key debug steps: (1) Check the logs filtered for "plugin":"catalog" — every ingestion stage logs success/failure, (2) Verify GitHub access — the token/App must have repo scope for private repos, (3) Validate YAML syntax — even a single misplaced indent breaks the entity, (4) Check entity schema — missing apiVersion, kind, or metadata.name causes validation rejection, (5) Test stitching — dependsOn or providesApis referencing non-existent entities causes partial ingestion failures.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Catalog Ingestion — Debug Each Pipeline Stage
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  INGESTION PIPELINE (6 stages — debug from left to right):   │
  │                                                              │
  │  GitHub Repo ──► Discovery ──► Parse ──► Validate ──► Stitch │
  │                                              │               │
  │                                              ▼               │
  │                                     ┌──────────────┐        │
  │                                     │  Database    │        │
  │                                     │  (PostgreSQL)│        │
  │                                     └──────┬───────┘        │
  │                                            ▼                 │
  │                                     ┌──────────────┐        │
  │                                     │  Catalog UI  │        │
  │                                     └──────────────┘        │
  │                                                              │
  │  STAGE-BY-STAGE DEBUGGING:                                   │
  │                                                              │
  │  ① DISCOVERY — "Did Backstage find the repo?"               │
  │  kubectl logs deploy/backstage | grep "reading"              │
  │  ❌ "GitHub token lacks permissions for repo anihpj/jobpost" │
  │  ✅ "Reading anihpj/jobpost/blob/main/catalog-info.yaml"     │
  │                                                              │
  │  ② PARSE — "Can Backstage read the YAML?"                    │
  │  kubectl logs deploy/backstage | grep "parse"                │
  │  ❌ "Failed to parse catalog-info.yaml: YAML syntax error"   │
  │  ✅ "Parsed entity: Component:default/jobpost-api"           │
  │                                                              │
  │  ③ VALIDATE — "Does the entity have required fields?"        │
  │  kubectl logs deploy/backstage | grep "validate"             │
  │  ❌ "Entity validation failed: missing 'metadata.name'"       │
  │  ✅ "Entity validation passed for jobpost-api"               │
  │                                                              │
  │  ④ STITCH — "Do cross-references resolve?"                   │
  │  kubectl logs deploy/backstage | grep "stitch"               │
  │  ❌ "Failed to resolve: resource:default/postgres-db"        │
  │  ✅ "Stitched relations for jobpost-api"                     │
  │                                                              │
  │  ⑤ STORE — "Was it saved to PostgreSQL?"                     │
  │  kubectl logs deploy/backstage | grep "refresh"              │
  │  ❌ "Database error: duplicate key"                          │
  │  ✅ "Catalog refresh complete: processed 42 entities"        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When a new service doesn't appear in the catalog, the team traces it through each stage: verify the catalog-info.yaml is on the main branch, check the GitHub App has repo access, look for YAML syntax errors, ensure all dependsOn references resolve, and confirm the catalog refresh schedule has had time to run (30-minute interval).

Infrastructure Q84 of 200
How do you debug authentication issues in Backstage?
A) Authentication errors appear as toast notifications in the Backstage UI with fix suggestions
B) Run `yarn auth:test` — Backstage includes a CLI tool to test each auth provider
C) Auth issues are only visible in the provider's dashboard (e.g., GitHub OAuth Apps page)

✅ Correct Answer: D

Infrastructure Q85 of 200
How do you view and manage Backstage logs?
A) Backstage uses winston structured logging — JSON-formatted logs to stdout/stderr. In K8s: `kubectl logs`. Can be aggregated via Fluentd/Logstash → Elasticsearch → Kibana, or Loki + Grafana. Log level is configurable via NODE_ENV or LOG_LEVEL env var
B) Logs are viewable in the Backstage UI at /admin/logs — a built-in log viewer
C) Logs are written to /var/log/backstage/ inside the container and must be accessed via `kubectl cp`
D) Backstage doesn't produce logs — all errors are surfaced as UI notifications only
✅ Correct Answer: A

Backstage uses winston for structured, JSON-formatted logging to stdout/stderr (12-Factor App practice). In Kubernetes: kubectl logs deployment/backstage. For production, aggregate logs via Fluentd/Logstash → Elasticsearch (view in Kibana) or Promtail → Loki → Grafana. Log level is controlled by the LOG_LEVEL environment variable (error, warn, info, debug).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Logging Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  Backstage (winston) → stdout (JSON)                         │

  │  Log aggregator (Fluentd/Promtail)                            │

  │                                                              │

  │  LOG_LEVEL=info   → Normal operations (default in prod)      │
