  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Production logs from the kubeadm cluster are shipped via Fluentd to Elasticsearch and viewed in Kibana. Developers use `kubectl logs -f` for real-time debugging. LOG_LEVEL=info in production, debug in staging.

Infrastructure Q86 of 200
How do you troubleshoot plugin loading failures?
A) Failed plugins show as greyed-out cards in the Backstage sidebar — click to see the error
B) Run `yarn plugin:verify` — checks all installed plugins for compatibility
C) Check logs for plugin init errors. Verify the plugin is in package.json and imported correctly. Frontend: check plugins.ts registration. Backend: check index.ts add() call. Run `yarn tsc` to catch type errors. Missing peer dependencies are a common cause
D) Plugins auto-disable on failure — Backstage continues running without them and logs a warning
✅ Correct Answer: C

Plugin loading failures are compile-time or startup-time issues. Debug by: (1) Check logs for plugin initialization errors, (2) Verify package.json — the plugin must be listed as a dependency, (3) Check registration — frontend plugins in plugins.ts, backend plugins via backend.add() in index.ts, (4) Run tsc — TypeScript catches import/type errors at build time, (5) Check peer dependencies — missing @backstage/core-plugin-api or @backstage/backend-common.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Plugin Loading Debugging
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DEBUG CHECKLIST:                                            │
  │                                                              │
  │  ① package.json — Is the plugin listed?                      │
  │     packages/app/package.json → @backstage/plugin-grafana    │
  │                                                              │
  │  ② Frontend registration — packages/app/src/plugins.ts:      │
  │     export { grafanaPlugin } from '@backstage/plugin-grafana';│
  │                                                              │
  │  ③ Backend registration — packages/backend/src/index.ts:     │
  │     backend.add(import('@backstage/plugin-grafana-backend')); │
  │                                                              │
  │  ④ yarn tsc — Do types check?                               │
  │     → Catches missing imports, wrong API usage               │
  │                                                              │
  │  ⑤ Peer dependencies — Are @backstage/core-plugin-api        │
  │     and @backstage/backend-common installed?                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When adding the PagerDuty plugin failed to show in the UI, the team found it wasn't exported from plugins.ts. Adding the export and rebuilding fixed it — a simple registration miss.

Infrastructure Q87 of 200
What are common Backstage memory and performance issues?
A) Backstage auto-scales memory — there are no known performance issues
B) Large catalogs (10,000+ entities) can cause high memory usage due to in-memory entity cache. Solutions: increase Node.js heap (--max-old-space-size), enable database-backed search (PostgreSQL/Elasticsearch), use entity filtering, and limit processing concurrency
C) Memory issues only occur with SQLite — PostgreSQL handles any catalog size without limits
D) Performance issues are always caused by plugins — the core Backstage engine has no scaling limits
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage keeps an in-memory entity cache for fast catalog queries. With 10,000+ entities, this can consume significant memory. Solutions: (1) Increase Node.js heap (NODE_OPTIONS=--max-old-space-size=4096), (2) Database-backed search (PostgreSQL search or Elasticsearch instead of in-memory Lunr), (3) Entity filtering (only ingest needed kinds), (4) Processing concurrency limits in catalog config.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Performance Optimization
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  MEMORY/PERF OPTIMIZATIONS:                                  │
  │                                                              │
  │  NODE_OPTIONS=--max-old-space-size=4096                      │
  │  → Increase heap from default ~1.5GB to 4GB                  │
  │                                                              │
  │  catalog:                                                    │
  │    processingInterval: { minutes: 5 }  ← Less frequent       │
  │    processingConcurrency: 3             ← Limit parallel     │
  │                                                              │
  │  search:                                                    │
  │    backend: pg  ← PostgreSQL-backed instead of in-memory     │
  │                                                              │
  │  K8s RESOURCE LIMITS:                                        │
  │  resources:                                                  │
  │    requests:                                                 │
  │      memory: "1Gi"                                           │
  │    limits:                                                   │
  │      memory: "4Gi"                                           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: With ~200 entities across 50 repos, the anihpj catalog is small enough that memory isn't a concern. But the team has K8s resource limits set (1Gi request, 4Gi limit) as a safety net.

Infrastructure Q88 of 200
How do you handle Backstage in multi-environment setups?
A) Run one Backstage instance that connects to all environments simultaneously
B) Use Backstage's built-in multi-tenancy feature — one deployment serves isolated views per environment
C) Deploy separate Backstage instances per environment (dev, staging, prod) with environment-specific app-config. Use Git branches or folders for per-environment configs. Each instance has its own database, auth, and catalog
D) Backstage doesn't support multiple environments — it's designed for a single production deployment only
✅ Correct Answer: C

Backstage follows a per-environment deployment model — each environment gets its own COMPLETE Backstage instance, not a shared instance. This is different from multi-tenant SaaS platforms where one deployment serves many teams. Each environment has: its own PostgreSQL database (separate catalog, user, and session data), its own app-config (different baseUrl, GitHub org, auth providers), its own GitHub App/Token (scanning different repos or branches), and its own monitoring and alerting.

This model is important because changes in staging should NEVER affect production. If you're testing a new catalog provider or auth config, you want it isolated. The per-environment configs are managed via app-config.<env>.yaml files, with environment variables injecting secrets at the highest layer.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Environment — Isolated Instances, Not Shared
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  THREE ENVIRONMENTS, THREE COMPLETE BACKSTAGE INSTANCES      │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  🧪 DEV ENVIRONMENT (kind-cl1, ksw → 3)               │    │
  │  │  URL: dev-backstage.anihpj.com                        │    │
  │  │  DB:  PostgreSQL (dev-data, small PVC)                 │    │
  │  │  Auth: GitHub OAuth (dev app)                         │    │
  │  │  Catalog: Scans dev-org GitHub repos                  │    │
  │  │  Purpose: Plugin development, config experimentation  │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  🟡 STAGING ENVIRONMENT (kind-cl2, ksw → 4)            │    │
  │  │  URL: staging-backstage.anihpj.com                     │    │
  │  │  DB:  PostgreSQL (staging-data, mirrors prod schema)    │    │
  │  │  Auth: GitHub OAuth (staging app)                     │    │
  │  │  Catalog: Scans staging-org repos (pre-prod mirror)   │    │
  │  │  Purpose: Pre-production testing, upgrade validation  │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐    │
  │  │  🟢 PRODUCTION ENVIRONMENT (kubeadm, ksw → 1)           │    │
  │  │  URL: backstage.anihpj.com                             │    │
  │  │  DB:  PostgreSQL (prod, 20Gi PVC, PgBouncer)           │    │
  │  │  Auth: GitHub OAuth (prod app, real users)             │    │
  │  │  Catalog: Scans anihpj org (ALL production repos)      │    │
  │  │  Purpose: Production developer portal (critical)       │    │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘    │
  │                                                              │
  │  KEY PRINCIPLE: Changes flow LEFT → RIGHT only               │
  │  DEV → STAGING → PRODUCTION (never right to left!)           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The dev Backstage runs on kind-cl1 (ksw → 3) with a small test catalog of 5-10 repos. Staging on kind-cl2 (ksw → 4) mirrors the production config exactly — same auth, same catalog providers, same plugin set — but with non-production data. Production on kubeadm (ksw → 1) serves the entire engineering team. Every upgrade goes through dev → staging → production, catching issues before they affect real developers.

Infrastructure Q89 of 200
How do you verify Backstage version and dependency health?
A) `yarn backstage-cli versions:check` shows current vs latest versions. `yarn npm audit` finds security vulnerabilities. `yarn outdated` lists packages behind latest. The `/.well-known/backstage` endpoint exposes version info at runtime
B) Version info is displayed in the Backstage UI footer — scroll to the bottom of any page
C) Run `yarn version` — Backstage reports a single unified version number for all packages
D) Dependency health is automatically reported to the CNCF — you receive email alerts for outdated packages
✅ Correct Answer: A

Backstage provides a comprehensive version and dependency health toolchain: (1) yarn backstage-cli versions:check — compares installed @backstage/* packages against the latest published versions. Shows which packages are outdated, the current version vs latest, and whether the upgrade is a patch/minor/major. Critical before upgrades. (2) yarn npm audit — scans the entire dependency tree for known security vulnerabilities (CVEs). Backstage takes security seriously — vulnerabilities should be fixed before deployment. (3) yarn outdated — lists ALL outdated packages (not just @backstage/*), useful for general dependency hygiene. (4) GET /.well-known/backstage — runtime HTTP endpoint that exposes the Backstage version and package list as JSON. Useful for monitoring and CI/CD pipelines to verify deployed versions. Run these checks in CI/CD on every PR and periodically in production to stay ahead of security issues and breaking changes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Version & Dependency Health — Complete Toolchain
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  yarn backstage-cli versions:check — Backstage Packages      ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  @backstage/core-plugin-api:    1.9.0  → 1.10.0 (minor)    ║
  ║  @backstage/plugin-catalog:     1.15.0 → 1.16.0 (minor)    ║
  ║  @backstage/plugin-scaffolder:  1.14.0 → 2.0.0  (MAJOR!)   ║
  ║  → Major upgrades require migration guide review            ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  yarn npm audit — Security Vulnerabilities                   ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  # MODERATE: semver < 7.5.2 → semver vulnerability CVE-...  ║
  ║  fix: yarn upgrade semver@^7.5.2                             ║
  ║  0 HIGH / 0 CRITICAL vulnerabilities (after fix)            ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  GET /.well-known/backstage — Runtime Version                ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  curl https://backstage.anihpj.com/.well-known/backstage     ║
  ║  → { "version": "1.20.0", "packages": [...] }                ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

For anihpj/jobpost: The CI/CD pipeline in GitHub Actions runs yarn backstage-cli versions:check and yarn npm audit on every pull request. If vulnerabilities are found with HIGH or CRITICAL severity, the PR is blocked from merging. A separate scheduled workflow (weekly) runs yarn outdated and opens an automated PR with safe dependency bumps. The production monitoring system (Prometheus + Grafana) periodically hits /.well-known/backstage to track deployed versions across the 6 clusters, ensuring the kubeadm production cluster hasn't drifted from the expected version.

Infrastructure Q90 of 200
How do you troubleshoot proxy/API forwarding issues?
A) Proxy errors show in the browser console only — check the Network tab for 502/504 errors
B) Check backend logs for proxy errors. Verify target URL is reachable from the Backstage pod. Test with curl from inside the container. Check proxy config in app-config.yaml (correct path → target mapping). Verify auth headers are correct
C) Run `yarn proxy:test --endpoint=grafana` — Backstage tests connectivity to each proxy target
D) Proxy issues are always DNS-related — check CoreDNS in Kubernetes first
✅ Correct Answer: B

Proxy issues manifest when plugins can't reach their backend services. Debug: (1) Check logs for proxy errors (ECONNREFUSED, 502, timeout), (2) Verify target reachability from the Backstage pod — kubectl exec -it deploy/backstage -- curl -v https://grafana.anihpj.com, (3) Check proxy config — ensure the path in proxy.endpoints matches the plugin's expected path, (4) Verify auth headers — incorrect or expired tokens cause 401/403 errors.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Proxy Debugging Flow
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  kubectl exec -it deploy/backstage -- sh                      │
  │  curl -v https://grafana.anihpj.com/api/health               │
  │                                                              │
  │  POSSIBLE ERRORS:                                            │
  │  ❌ "Could not resolve host: grafana.anihpj.com"              │
  │     → DNS issue or wrong target URL                          │
  │  ❌ "Connection refused" / "Connection timed out"             │
  │     → Target service not running or NetworkPolicy blocks     │
  │  ❌ "401 Unauthorized"                                        │
  │     → Auth header/token incorrect or expired                 │
  │  ❌ "404 Not Found"                                           │
  │     → Wrong path mapping in proxy.endpoints                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When Grafana dashboards stopped loading in Backstage, the team discovered the Grafana token in the proxy config had expired. Rotating the token in the K8s Secret fixed it immediately.

Infrastructure Q91 of 200
How do you manage Backstage configuration drift?
A) Configuration drift is automatically detected and corrected by Backstage's config reconciler
B) Use `yarn config:diff` to compare running config against the Git version
C) Backstage stores a config hash in PostgreSQL — any drift triggers an alert
D) Store all config in Git (app-config.yaml + environment overrides). Use GitOps (ArgoCD) to ensure deployed config matches Git. Run `yarn backstage-cli config:check` and `yarn backstage-cli config:print` to validate and inspect the resolved config at runtime
✅ Correct Answer: D

Configuration drift — when the running Backstage configuration diverges from what's in Git — is prevented through a GitOps-based approach: (1) Git as single source of truth — ALL configuration files (app-config.yaml, app-config.production.yaml, app-config.staging.yaml) are committed to the infrastructure Git repository. No manual edits to running ConfigMaps or Secrets are allowed. (2) GitOps automation (ArgoCD/Flux) — a GitOps controller continuously monitors the Git repository and automatically syncs the Kubernetes ConfigMaps and Secrets to match. If someone manually edits a ConfigMap via kubectl edit, ArgoCD detects the drift and reverts it within minutes. (3) Pre-deployment validation — yarn backstage-cli config:check validates the merged configuration against all plugin schemas, catching missing required keys or type errors before deployment. Run this in CI/CD on every config change. (4) Runtime inspection — yarn backstage-cli config:print prints the fully resolved configuration (after merging all layers and env vars), enabling manual verification that the correct values are active. This layered approach means configuration changes follow the standard Git PR → review → merge → auto-deploy workflow, with full audit trail and rollback capability.

📝 Detailed Explanation
GitOps Config Management — Drift Prevention
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  GITOPS FLOW: Git → ArgoCD → Kubernetes                      ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  Git Repo (anihpj/infra/backstage/):                         ║
  ║  ├── app-config.yaml              ← Base config              ║
  ║  ├── app-config.production.yaml   ← Prod overrides           ║
  ║  ├── app-config.staging.yaml      ← Staging overrides        ║
  ║  └── helm/values.yaml             ← Helm chart values        ║
  ║                                                              ║
  ║  ArgoCD (continuous sync):                                   ║
  ║  → Polls Git every 3 minutes                                 ║
  ║  → Detects: ConfigMap in cluster ≠ ConfigMap in Git          ║
  ║  → Action: Reverts cluster to match Git (auto-heal)          ║
  ║                                                              ║
  ║  CI/CD Pipeline (pre-merge validation):                      ║
  ║  → yarn backstage-cli config:check  ← Validate schema        ║
  ║  → yarn backstage-cli config:print  ← Inspect resolved       ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DRIFT PROTECTION: Any manual `kubectl edit configmap`       │
  │  → ArgoCD detects drift in <3 min → Auto-reverts to Git     │
  │  → Alert: "ConfigMap backstage-app-config drifted"          │
  │  → Lesson: Always change config via Git PR, never kubectl   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: All Backstage configuration lives in the anihpj/infra Git repository under backstage/. ArgoCD syncs these to the 6 Kubernetes clusters — kubeadm (production), kind clusters (staging, testing), and minikube (local dev). If anyone runs kubectl edit configmap backstage-app-config -n backstage in production, ArgoCD detects the drift within 3 minutes, reverts the change, and sends a Slack alert to #platform-eng: "Config drift detected and auto-corrected." The CI/CD pipeline runs config:check on every config PR, ensuring no invalid configuration reaches any cluster. This GitOps discipline means the entire Backstage configuration has a full Git history — who changed what, when, and why.

Infrastructure Q92 of 200
What operational metrics should you monitor for Backstage?
A) Only uptime matters — if Backstage is up, everything is fine
B) Backstage exposes no metrics — you must instrument it with custom code
C) Request latency (p50/p95/p99), error rate (4xx/5xx), catalog entity count, scaffolder task success rate, database connection pool usage, memory/heap usage, event loop lag. Prometheus metrics via @backstage/plugin-app-backend at /metrics
D) Only database metrics matter — the Backstage app itself has no performance characteristics to monitor
✅ Correct Answer: C

Key operational metrics: Request latency (p50/p95/p99 — are API calls fast?), Error rate (4xx client errors, 5xx server errors), Catalog entity count (are entities being ingested?), Scaffolder task success rate (are templates working?), DB connection pool (are connections exhausted?), Memory/heap usage (is the Node.js process healthy?), Event loop lag (is the process blocked?). Exposed via @backstage/plugin-app-backend at /metrics in Prometheus format.

  │                                                              │
  │  http_requests_total             (counter)                    │
  │  → 200 vs 4xx vs 5xx breakdown                               │
  │                                                              │

For anihpj/jobpost: Prometheus on the kubeadm cluster scrapes Backstage's /metrics every 30s. Grafana dashboards show request latency, error rates, and catalog health. Alerts fire if p95 latency exceeds 1s or error rate exceeds 5%.

🟠 Category 3: Backstage Catalog (22%)Q93-Q136

🟡 Intermediate: The Beating Heart of Backstage

This category is about the SOFTWARE CATALOG — the central registry where every service, API, resource, and team is modeled. You'll learn about entity kinds (Component, API, Resource, System, Domain, User, Group), YAML definitions (catalog-info.yaml), annotations, manual vs automatic ingestion, and troubleshooting entity visibility. For anihpj/jobpost, the catalog is where every Django service and its dependencies are registered.

Q93-Q136 44 Questions 🟡 Intermediate

3.1 Entities — Kinds, YAML Definitions & AnnotationsQ93-Q100

Catalog Q93 of 200
What are the standard entity Kinds in the Backstage Catalog?
A) Pod, Service, Deployment, Ingress — the same as Kubernetes resource kinds
B) Project, Repository, Pipeline, Environment — the Backstage-specific hierarchy
C) Component, API, Resource, System, Domain, User, Group — plus Template (for scaffolder templates). These 8 kinds form the Backstage entity hierarchy
D) Microservice, Database, Queue, Cache, Frontend, Backend — infrastructure-focused kinds
✅ Correct Answer: C

Backstage defines 7 core entity kinds plus Template (for the Scaffolder): Component (a piece of software — service, website, library), API (an interface exposed by a component), Resource (infrastructure needed by a component — database, cache, queue), System (a collection of components that work together), Domain (a business area grouping related systems), User (a person in the organization), Group (a team or org unit).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Entity Kind Hierarchy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ENTITY HIERARCHY:                                           │
  │                                                              │
  │  🌐 Domain: anihpj (business area)                           │
  │     │                                                        │
  │     └── 🔧 System: JobPost (collection of components)        │
  │            │                                                 │
  │            ├── 📦 Component: jobpost-api (Django REST API)   │
  │            │   ├── 🔗 dependsOn: postgres-db                 │
  │            │   ├── 🔗 dependsOn: redis-cache                 │
  │            │   └── 📡 API: jobpost-api (OpenAPI spec)        │
  │            │                                                 │
  │            ├── 📦 Component: jobpost-web (Django templates)  │
  │            │                                                 │
  │            ├── 🗄️  Resource: postgres-db (PostgreSQL 15)     │
  │            └── 🗄️  Resource: redis-cache (Redis 7)           │
  │                                                              │
  │  👤 User: owner (individual)                                  │
  │  👥 Group: anihpj-team (team)                                 │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The catalog models jobpost-api and jobpost-web as Components, postgres-db and redis-cache as Resources, the JobPost system as a System, anihpj as a Domain, and the anihpj-team as a Group (owner of all components).

Catalog Q94 of 200
What is the structure of a catalog-info.yaml entity definition?
A) `apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1`, `kind: Component` (one of the 7+1 kinds), `metadata:` (name, namespace, annotations, labels, tags), `spec:` (type, lifecycle, owner, system, dependsOn, providesApis). Every entity follows this exact schema
B) A free-form YAML file — Backstage auto-detects the schema based on the file contents
C) `version: 1`, `type: service`, `name: my-service`, `owner: team-a` — a simple flat structure
D) catalog-info.yaml uses JSON, not YAML — it must be valid JSON with `{ "apiVersion": "backstage.io/v1alpha1" }`
✅ Correct Answer: A

Every entity YAML file follows a strict schema: apiVersion (currently backstage.io/v1alpha1), kind (Component, API, Resource, etc.), metadata (name — unique per kind/namespace, annotations for integrations, labels for filtering, tags for categorization), spec (type of component, lifecycle stage, owner group, system it belongs to, dependencies).

📝 Detailed Explanation
catalog-info.yaml — Complete Example
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: backstage.io/v1alpha1                           │
  │  kind: Component                                             │
  │  metadata:                                                   │
  │    name: jobpost-api                    ← Unique identifier  │
  │    namespace: default                                         │
  │    annotations:                                               │
  │      github.com/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost                  │
  │      backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.                         │
  │    labels:                                                   │
  │      language: python                                        │
  │    tags: [django, rest-api, production]                      │
  │  spec:                                                       │
  │    type: service                       ← service, website,   │
  │    lifecycle: production              ← library, etc.        │
  │    owner: anihpj-team                                        │
  │    system: jobpost                                           │
  │    dependsOn:                                                │
  │      - resource:default/postgres-db                          │
  │      - resource:default/redis-cache                          │
  │    providesApis:                                             │
  │      - jobpost-api                                           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Each service has its own catalog-info.yaml in its GitHub repo (e.g., anihpj/jobpost/blob/main/catalog/catalog-info.yaml). The GitHub Discovery provider finds and ingests these files automatically.

Catalog Q95 of 200
What is the purpose of annotations in catalog-info.yaml?
A) Annotations are just decorative comments — they don't affect Backstage behavior
B) Annotations are alternative names for entities — like Kubernetes labels
C) Annotations are only used for sorting entities alphabetically in the catalog UI
D) Annotations connect entities to external systems. Each plugin reads specific annotations: `github.com/project-slug` links to GitHub, `backstage.io/techdocs-ref` enables TechDocs, `grafana/dashboard-selector` links Grafana. They're the "glue" between Backstage and your tools
✅ Correct Answer: D

Annotations are the integration mechanism — they tell plugins how to connect entities to external systems. Examples: github.com/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost (links to GitHub repo), backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:. (where TechDocs Markdown lives), grafana/dashboard-selector: "service=jobpost-api" (which Grafana dashboard to show), pagerduty.com/integration-key: abc123 (PagerDuty service mapping).

For anihpj/jobpost: The jobpost-api entity has annotations for GitHub (repo link), TechDocs (API documentation), Grafana (latency dashboard), and PagerDuty (on-call schedule). Each annotation enables a different plugin on the entity page.

Catalog Q96 of 200
What is the difference between labels and annotations?
A) There is no difference — labels and annotations are interchangeable
B) Labels are for filtering/searching within Backstage (e.g., `language: python`). Annotations are for connecting to external systems (e.g., `github.com/project-slug`). Labels are indexed for search; annotations are read by plugins
C) Labels go in metadata, annotations go in spec — they're stored in different database tables
D) Labels are for production entities only; annotations are for development entities
✅ Correct Answer: B

Labels are key-value pairs used for internal catalog operations — searching, filtering, grouping entities. They're indexed in the search engine. Annotations are also key-value pairs but are consumed by plugins to connect entities to external systems. Think of labels as "internal metadata" and annotations as "integration pointers." Both live under metadata: in the entity YAML.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Labels vs Annotations
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LABELS (internal — indexed, searchable):                     │
  │  metadata:                                                   │
  │    labels:                                                   │
  │      language: python       ← Filter: show all Python services│
  │      tier: backend           ← Filter: show backend services │
  │      criticality: high       ← Filter: show critical services│
  │                                                              │
  │  ANNOTATIONS (external — consumed by plugins):               │
  │  metadata:                                                   │
  │    annotations:                                              │
  │      github.com/project-slug: anihpj/jobpost                 │
  │      backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.                         │
  │      grafana/dashboard-selector: "service=jobpost-api"       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team uses labels to categorize services by language (`python`, `javascript`) and tier (`backend`, `frontend`). Annotations link each service to its GitHub repo, Grafana dashboard, and PagerDuty integration.

Catalog Q97 of 200
What is the `spec.type` field for a Component entity?
A) It classifies the component: `service` (backend service), `website` (frontend), `library` (shared code), `documentation` (docs site). It's a free-form string but common values are standardized across the Backstage ecosystem
B) It must be one of three fixed values: 'microservice', 'monolith', or 'serverless'
C) It defines the programming language used — e.g., 'python', 'typescript', 'go'
D) It specifies the deployment type — 'kubernetes', 'docker', 'vm', or 'serverless'
✅ Correct Answer: A

spec.type is a classification label that tells Backstage (and your team) what KIND of software this component represents. While it's technically a free-form string, the Backstage ecosystem has converged on standard values that the catalog UI uses to display appropriate icons and grouping. Think of it like file extensions — .py tells you it's Python, .tsx tells you it's React TypeScript. Similarly, type: service tells you it's a backend API, and type: website tells you it's a frontend.

The type also determines which plugins are relevant. A component with type: service typically needs Kubernetes info, Grafana dashboards, and PagerDuty alerts. A component with type: library needs npm/pip package info and API docs. The catalog UI intelligently shows different tabs and widgets based on the component type.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Component Types — Standard Values & UI Impact
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │                                                              │

  │     Example: jobpost-api (Django REST Framework)             │
  │                                                              │

  │     UI: K8s pods, CDN status, Lighthouse scores              │
  │     Example: jobpost-web (Django templates + static files)   │
  │                                                              │
  │  📚 library         → Shared code package (npm, pip, maven)  │
  │     UI: Package registry info, API reference, dependents     │
  │     Example: anihpj-common (shared Django utilities)         │
  │                                                              │
  │  📖 documentation   → Documentation site or knowledge base   │
  │     UI: TechDocs, page analytics, search metrics             │
  │     Example: anihpj-docs (team wiki)                         │
  │                                                              │
  │  🛠️  tool           → Internal CLI, script, or dev utility   │

  │                                                              │

  │  │                                                   │       │
  │  │  Entity page icons change based on type:          │       │
  │  │  service → ⚙️ gear icon                           │       │
  │  │  website → 🌐 globe icon                          │       │
  │  │  library → 📦 package icon                        │       │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: jobpost-api has spec.type: service (Django REST API), jobpost-web has spec.type: website (frontend), and anihpj-common has spec.type: library (shared Django utilities). This classification helps the catalog UI show the right widgets — Kubernetes pods for services, Grafana dashboards for services, but package registry info for libraries.

Catalog Q98 of 200
What does the `spec.lifecycle` field represent?
A) The deployment frequency — how often the component is released to production
B) The age of the component — how long it has existed in the organization
C) The maturity stage of the component: `experimental` (still being developed), `production` (stable, serving traffic), `deprecated` (being phased out). It helps teams understand which services are safe to depend on
D) The CI/CD pipeline stage — 'build', 'test', 'deploy', or 'monitor'
✅ Correct Answer: C

spec.lifecycle is one of the most important metadata fields in the catalog because it tells every developer "can I safely depend on this service?" There are three standard lifecycle stages: (1) experimental — early development, APIs may break without notice, no SLAs, use at your own risk, (2) production — stable, monitored, has on-call rotation, meets SLAs, safe to be a dependency, (3) deprecated — being phased out or replaced, should include a migration path in the description, new services must NOT depend on it.

The catalog UI displays lifecycle as a colored badge prominently on every entity page: 🟠 orange for experimental, 🟢 green for production, 🔴 red for deprecated. This visual cue immediately tells developers what they're looking at before they even read the description. The lifecycle also integrates with scoring frameworks like Backstage Tech Insights to calculate service maturity scores.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Lifecycle Stages — The Service Maturity Journey
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  LIFECYCLE PROGRESSION (typical path):                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  🧪 EXPERIMENTAL ──────────► 🟢 PRODUCTION ──────────► 🔴 DEPRECATED │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │  🧪 EXPERIMENTAL                                    │     │
  │  │  Badge: ORANGE                                      │     │
  │  │  Meaning: "We're building this"                     │     │
  │  │  APIs: May change without notice                    │     │
  │  │  SLAs: None — don't rely on this in production      │     │
  │  │  Monitoring: Optional / minimal                     │     │
  │  │  Owner: Still required! (accountability even in dev)│     │
  │  │  Example: New ML recommendation engine (v0)         │     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                          │                                    │
  │                          ▼ (promoted to production)           │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │  🟢 PRODUCTION                                      │     │
  │  │  Badge: GREEN                                       │     │
  │  │  Meaning: "Safe to depend on this"                  │     │
  │  │  APIs: Stable, versioned, backward-compatible       │     │
  │  │  SLAs: 99.9% uptime, p95 latency < 200ms            │     │
  │  │  Monitoring: Prometheus + Grafana + PagerDuty       │     │
  │  │  On-call: Rotation defined, alert routing active    │     │
  │  │  Example: jobpost-api (serves real traffic)         │     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                          │                                    │
  │                          ▼ (being replaced)                   │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │  🔴 DEPRECATED                                      │     │
  │  │  Badge: RED                                         │     │
  │  │  Meaning: "Migrate off this — it's going away"      │     │
  │  │  Migration: Description MUST include migration path │     │
  │  │  New dependencies: NOT allowed to depend on this     │     │
  │  │  Existing consumers: Should be migrating            │     │
  │  │  Example: jobpost-api-v1 (replaced by v2)           │     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: jobpost-api is production — it serves real job posting traffic with 99.9% uptime SLA, Prometheus monitoring, and PagerDuty alerting. A new ML recommendation service under development would be experimental (orange badge, warning: APIs may change). The old v1 API being replaced by v2 would be deprecated with a migration guide in its TechDocs.

Catalog Q99 of 200
How does `dependsOn` work in the Backstage entity model?
A) `dependsOn` is purely cosmetic — it doesn't affect Backstage behavior
B) `dependsOn` defines a dependency relationship between entities. It creates visual links in the catalog graph and dependency diagrams. It's a `spec.dependsOn` field referencing other entities by `kind:namespace/name` format
C) `dependsOn` automatically installs dependencies when deploying — like npm install for entities
D) `dependsOn` is only valid for Component → Resource relationships, not Component → Component
✅ Correct Answer: B

dependsOn in spec creates explicit dependency relationships between entities. The format is kind:namespace/name (e.g., resource:default/postgres-db). These relationships are visible in: (1) the entity's dependency graph, (2) the catalog graph plugin (visual graph of all dependencies), (3) API documentation showing upstream/downstream consumers. It helps answer "what breaks if this component goes down?"

📝 Detailed Explanation
dependsOn Relationships
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  spec:                                                       │
  │    dependsOn:                                                │
  │      - resource:default/postgres-db    ← Needs PostgreSQL    │
  │      - resource:default/redis-cache    ← Needs Redis         │
  │      - component:default/auth-service  ← Calls auth service  │
  │                                                              │
  │  DEPENDENCY GRAPH (visible in catalog UI):                   │
  │  ┌─────────────┐                                             │
  │  │ postgres-db │◄────┐                                       │
  │  └─────────────┘     │                                       │
  │  ┌─────────────┐     │  ┌──────────────┐                     │
  │  │ redis-cache │◄────┼──│  jobpost-api  │                     │
  │  └─────────────┘     │  └──────┬───────┘                     │
  │  ┌─────────────┐     │         │                              │
  │  │ auth-service│◄────┘         │                              │
  │  └─────────────┘               ▼                              │
  │                        ┌──────────────┐                      │
  │                        │  jobpost-web  │                      │
  │                        └──────────────┘                      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: jobpost-api's catalog-info.yaml declares dependsOn for postgres-db and redis-cache. The catalog graph plugin shows that if postgres-db goes down, jobpost-api is directly affected.

What is the `spec.owner` field and why is it critical?

B) `owner` is optional — entities without owners work the same as those with owners
C) `owner` specifies which Kubernetes namespace the entity belongs to
D) `owner` assigns a Group entity as the owning team. It's REQUIRED for all entities. It powers the catalog's ownership view ("show me all components owned by my team"), PagerDuty on-call routing, and access control. Format: `group:namespace/name`

spec.owner is a required field that assigns a Group entity as the owning team. Format: group:default/anihpj-team. For anihpj/jobpost: All anihpj catalog entities (jobpost-api, jobpost-web, postgres-db, redis-cache) are owned by `group:default/anihpj-team`. This means the anihpj-team is accountable for all these services, and PagerDuty routes alerts to them.

3.2 Catalog Processing — Export, Processors, Rules & Multi-TenancyQ101-Q114

Catalog Q101 of 200
How do you export catalog data from Backstage?
A) Use the "Export CSV" button in the catalog UI at /catalog
B) Via the Backstage Catalog API: `GET /api/catalog/entities` returns all entities as JSON. Can filter by kind, namespace, owner, and lifecycle. Results are paginated. Also `GET /api/catalog/entities/by-name/:kind/:namespace/:name` for single entities
C) Run `yarn catalog:export --format=json > entities.json` — a CLI command that dumps all entities
D) Backstage automatically exports a daily CSV to the configured S3 bucket — check the techdocs bucket
✅ Correct Answer: B

The Catalog exposes a REST API at /api/catalog/entities that returns all entities in JSON format. You can filter using query parameters: ?filter=kind=Component, ?filter=metadata.namespace=default, ?filter=spec.owner=anihpj-team. Results are paginated — use ?limit=50&offset=0 for large catalogs. For individual entities, use the by-name endpoint: /api/catalog/entities/by-name/Component/default/jobpost-api.
