For anihpj/jobpost: The platform team uses GET /api/catalog/entities?filter=spec.owner=anihpj-team to audit all services owned by the team. This API is also used by CI/CD pipelines to verify that newly created services are properly registered in the catalog.

Catalog Q102 of 200
Can you have entities with the same name in different namespaces?
A) Yes — namespaces provide isolation. An entity is uniquely identified by `kind:namespace/name`. So `Component:default/jobpost-api` and `Component:staging/jobpost-api` are different entities
B) No — entity names must be globally unique across all namespaces
C) Only for Component kind — other kinds must be globally unique
D) Yes, but duplicates in different namespaces cause a warning in the catalog UI
✅ Correct Answer: A

Namespaces are the primary isolation mechanism in the catalog. An entity's full identity is kind:namespace/name — the namespace acts as a scope. This means you can have Component:default/jobpost-api (production) and Component:staging/jobpost-api (staging) — they are completely separate entities with their own metadata, relations, and lifecycle states. The default namespace is always present and is where most entities live.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Namespaces — Entity Isolation
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ENTITY IDENTITY = kind:namespace/name                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │  default namespace (production)                      │     │
  │  │  ├── Component:default/jobpost-api                   │     │
  │  │  ├── Component:default/jobpost-web                   │     │
  │  │  ├── Resource:default/postgres-db                    │     │
  │  │  └── Resource:default/redis-cache                    │     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │  staging namespace (pre-production)                  │     │
  │  │  ├── Component:staging/jobpost-api    ← SAME name    │     │
  │  │  ├── Component:staging/jobpost-web    ← but DIFFERENT│     │
  │  │  └── Resource:staging/postgres-db     ← namespace!   │     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                                                              │
  │  ✅ Same name in different namespace = DIFFERENT entity      │
  │  ✅ Different lifecycle, different relations, different owner │
  │  ✅ Namespaces are how you model multi-environment catalogs  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team uses namespaces to separate production entities (in default) from staging entities (in staging). The staging jobpost-api mirrors production but connects to staging databases — all isolated by namespace.

Catalog Q103 of 200
What are Catalog Processors and what is their role?
A) Catalog Processors are CPU-intensive background jobs that index entities for search — like Elasticsearch workers
B) Processors are UI components that render entity pages — they determine how each entity kind looks
C) Processors are plugins in the ingestion pipeline that transform raw data into entities. They read catalog-info.yaml (or other sources), validate schema, resolve placeholders, stitch relations, and write to the database. Each processor handles one stage of the pipeline
D) Processors are cron jobs that periodically check if catalog entities are still reachable
✅ Correct Answer: C

Catalog Processors are the engine of the ingestion pipeline — they form a processing chain where each processor transforms entity data one step at a time. The chain includes: Location Readers (fetch raw YAML from GitHub/URLs), Entity Parsers (parse YAML into entity objects), Validators (check required fields and types), Placeholder Resolvers (substitute $text, $json, $yaml), Relation Stitchers (resolve dependsOn/providesApis references), and Database Writers (persist to PostgreSQL).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Catalog Processor Chain — 6 Stages
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  PROCESSOR CHAIN (sequential pipeline):                      │
  │                                                              │
  │  GitHub Repo                                                 │
  │     │                                                        │
  │     ▼                                                        │
  │  ① LocationReader   ← Fetches raw YAML from GitHub/URL       │
  │     │                                                        │
  │     ▼                                                        │
  │  ② EntityParser     ← Parses YAML → Entity object            │
  │     │                                                        │
  │     ▼                                                        │
  │  ③ Validator        ← Checks apiVersion, kind, metadata.name │
  │     │                                                        │
  │     ▼                                                        │
  │  ④ PlaceholderResolver ← Substitutes $text, $json, $yaml     │
  │     │                                                        │
  │     ▼                                                        │
  │  ⑤ RelationStitcher ← Resolves dependsOn → postgres-db links │
  │     │                                                        │
  │     ▼                                                        │
  │  ⑥ DatabaseWriter   ← Persists to PostgreSQL                 │
  │     │                                                        │
  │     ▼                                                        │
  │  Catalog UI (searchable)                                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When GitHub Discovery finds a new catalog-info.yaml in the anihpj/jobpost repo, it flows through all 6 processors. If the LocationReader can't access the repo (bad token), the pipeline stops at stage ①. If the YAML is valid but references a non-existent postgres-db, the RelationStitcher at stage ⑤ logs a warning and the entity appears with a broken dependency.

Catalog Q104 of 200
What is the Catalog ingestion schedule?
A) Default refresh every 30 minutes (configurable via `catalog.providers.github.schedule.frequency`). You can set it to any cron-like interval. The processing loop runs continuously, checking for new/changed/deleted entities on each cycle
B) Real-time — entities are ingested instantly via GitHub webhooks pushed to Backstage
C) Once per day at midnight UTC — you cannot change the schedule
D) Manual only — you must click "Refresh Catalog" in the UI to ingest new entities
✅ Correct Answer: A

The catalog processes entities on a configurable schedule, defaulting to every 30 minutes. This is set in app-config.yaml under catalog.providers.github.schedule.frequency. The format supports { minutes: N }, { hours: N }, or standard cron expressions. Each cycle: Backstage scans configured providers (GitHub org, GitLab group), reads catalog-info.yaml files, compares against the database, and adds/updates/removes entities.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Catalog Refresh Cycle
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  REFRESH CYCLE (every ~30 minutes by default):               │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌─────────┐     ┌──────────┐     ┌──────────┐              │
  │  │ Wait 30m │ ──▶ │ Scan     │ ──▶ │ Compare  │              │
  │  │ (config) │     │ Providers│     │ vs DB    │              │
  │  └─────────┘     └──────────┘     └────┬─────┘              │
  │                                        │                     │
  │                    ┌───────────────────┼───────────────┐     │
  │                    ▼                   ▼               ▼     │
  │              ┌──────────┐      ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐ │
  │              │ ADD new  │      │ UPDATE    │    │ REMOVE   │ │
  │              │ entities │      │ changed   │    │ deleted  │ │
  │              └──────────┘      └──────────┘    └──────────┘ │
  │                                                              │
  │  CONFIGURATION:                                              │
  │  catalog:                                                    │
  │    providers:                                                │
  │      github:                                                 │
  │        organization: anihpj                                  │
  │        schedule:                                             │
  │          frequency: { minutes: 10 }  ← Every 10 min          │
  │          timeout:   { minutes: 3 }   ← Max scan time         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The catalog refreshes every 30 minutes, scanning the anihpj GitHub org for new or changed catalog-info.yaml files. When a developer adds a catalog-info.yaml to a new repo, it may take up to 30 minutes to appear in the catalog. For faster feedback, the team reduced the staging environment's interval to 5 minutes.

Catalog Q105 of 200
How does the Catalog handle duplicate entities?
A) Duplicates are silently skipped — the first entity ingested wins
B) Duplicates cause both entities to be rejected — neither appears in the catalog
C) Duplicates are merged — properties from both entities are combined into one
D) An entity is uniquely identified by `kind:namespace/name`. If two locations produce the same entity, the LAST one processed wins (upsert behavior). If you need different entities with the same name, use different namespaces
✅ Correct Answer: D

The catalog uses upsert behavior (update or insert). Each entity is uniquely identified by its kind:namespace/name triple. If two catalog-info.yaml files produce the same entity (same kind, namespace, and name), the last one processed wins — it overwrites the previous. This is by design: it allows the same entity to be registered from multiple locations, with the most recently processed source being authoritative.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Duplicate Entity Resolution
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  UPSERT BEHAVIOR:                                            │
  │                                                              │
  │  Location A: github.com/anihpj/jobpost/catalog-info.yaml     │
  │  → Component:default/jobpost-api (lifecycle: production)     │
  │                                                              │
  │  Location B: github.com/anihpj/jobpost-alt/catalog-info.yaml │
  │  → Component:default/jobpost-api (lifecycle: experimental)   │
  │                                                              │
  │  RESULT: Last processed wins → experimental (if B was later) │
  │                                                              │
  │  ⚠️  Same kind+namespace+name with different content =       │
  │     overwrite, not merge!                                    │
  │                                                              │
  │  ✅ SOLUTION for truly separate entities:                    │
  │     Use different namespaces:                                │
  │     Component:default/jobpost-api                            │
  │     Component:staging/jobpost-api  ← Separate entity!        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team ensures each production entity has a single catalog-info.yaml source. For staging versions of the same service, they use the staging namespace rather than hoping duplicates merge correctly — namespaces provide clean isolation.

Catalog Q106 of 200
What are Catalog Rules and how do they work?
A) Rules are JavaScript functions that run on each entity to validate business logic
B) Allow/deny rules in app-config.yaml under `catalog.rules`. They control which entity kinds can be ingested. Syntax: `- allow: [Component, API, Resource]` or `- deny: [Template]`. Processed in order — first matching rule applies
C) Rules are defined in a separate `catalog-rules.yaml` file in the Backstage root directory
D) Rules are configured via the Backstage UI at /catalog/admin/rules — anyone with admin access can edit them
✅ Correct Answer: B

Catalog rules are allow/deny filters in app-config.yaml that gate what enters the catalog. They're processed in order — the first matching rule wins. You can allow specific kinds (whitelist approach) or deny specific kinds (blacklist approach). If no rule matches, the entity is allowed by default. Rules also support field-level filtering: you can allow only Components with spec.type: service or deny entities from specific owners.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Catalog Rules — Gating Entity Ingestion
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  catalog:                                                    │
  │    rules:                                                    │
  │      - allow: [Component, API, Resource, System, Domain]     │
  │      - deny: [Template, Location]                            │
  │                                                              │
  │  RULE PROCESSING ORDER:                                      │
  │  ① Check rule 1: Is kind in [Component,API,Resource,...] ?   │
  │     → YES → ALLOW (stop processing)                          │
  │  ② Check rule 2: Is kind in [Template,Location] ?            │
  │     → YES → DENY (stop processing)                           │
  │  ③ No rules matched → ALLOW (default)                        │
  │                                                              │
  │  VISUAL:                                                     │
  │  New entity ──► 🔍 Rule 1 (allow list) ──► match? ──► ✅ IN  │
  │                     │                                        │
  │                     └── no match? ──► 🔍 Rule 2 (deny) ──► ❌ │
  │                                            │                 │
  │                                            └── no match? ✅  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The catalog rules allow only Component, API, Resource, System, and Domain kinds. Template entities (used by the Scaffolder) are denied from catalog display — they live in a separate template registry. This keeps the catalog clean and focused on actual services and infrastructure.

Catalog Q107 of 200
How does the Catalog handle large organizations (10,000+ entities)?
A) The catalog is limited to 5,000 entities — you need multiple Backstage instances beyond that
B) Large catalogs work out of the box with no configuration — Backstage auto-scales
C) You optimize with: database-backed search (PostgreSQL/Elasticsearch instead of in-memory Lunr), increased processing concurrency limits, entity filtering (only ingest needed kinds), increased Node.js heap, and pagination in the UI
D) Large catalogs require migrating to a cloud-managed Backstage instance — self-hosted can't handle 10,000+ entities
✅ Correct Answer: C

Backstage scales to 10,000+ entities but needs configuration. Key optimizations: (1) Database-backed search — switch from in-memory Lunr to PostgreSQL or Elasticsearch (handles millions of entities), (2) Processing concurrency — increase catalog.processingConcurrency to parallelize, (3) Entity filtering — only ingest needed kinds via catalog rules, (4) Node.js heap — increase to 4-8GB with NODE_OPTIONS=--max-old-space-size=8192, (5) Pagination — the catalog UI automatically pages results.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Scaling the Catalog — Production Optimizations
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SCALING LEVERS FOR LARGE CATALOGS:                          │
  │                                                              │
  │  🔍 SEARCH BACKEND (biggest impact):                         │
  │  search:                                                     │
  │    backend: pg  ← PostgreSQL instead of in-memory Lunr       │
  │                                                              │
  │  ⚡ PROCESSING CONCURRENCY:                                   │
  │  catalog:                                                    │
  │    processingConcurrency: 6   ← Default: 3, increase for     │
  │                                  faster ingestion            │
  │                                                              │
  │  🧠 MEMORY (Node.js heap):                                   │
  │  NODE_OPTIONS=--max-old-space-size=8192  ← 8GB heap          │
  │                                                              │
  │  🎯 ENTITY FILTERING:                                        │
  │  catalog:                                                    │
  │    rules:                                                    │
  │      - allow: [Component, API, Resource]  ← Only what you need│
  │                                                              │
  │  📊 EXPECTED PERFORMANCE:                                    │
  │  1,000 entities  → Default config works fine                 │
  │  10,000 entities → pg search + 4GB heap + concurrency=5      │
  │  50,000 entities → pg search + 8GB heap + concurrency=10     │
  │  100,000+        → Elasticsearch + 16GB heap + batching      │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: With ~200 entities across 50 repos, the anihpj catalog is well within default limits. The team uses the default in-memory Lunr search with no performance issues. If the org grows to 1,000+ services, they'll switch to PostgreSQL-backed search.

Catalog Q108 of 200
What happens when a catalog-info.yaml is deleted or moved?
A) On the next refresh cycle, the entity becomes "orphaned" (no source location). By default, orphaned entities remain in the catalog but show a warning. You can configure auto-deletion of orphaned entities or manually remove them
B) The entity is immediately deleted from the catalog — Backstage monitors all catalog-info.yaml files in real-time
C) Nothing happens — once ingested, entities persist forever regardless of source file changes
D) The entity's status changes to "deprecated" automatically and is removed after 30 days
✅ Correct Answer: A

When a catalog-info.yaml is deleted from the source repo, the next refresh cycle detects the missing location and marks the entity as orphaned. Orphaned entities still appear in the catalog but show a warning icon. This is a safety feature — it prevents accidentally deleting critical entity data. You can configure catalog.orphanStrategy to auto-delete orphans after a grace period, or manually remove them via the catalog API.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Orphaned Entity Lifecycle
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  DELETION FLOW:                                              │
  │                                                              │
  │  ① Developer deletes catalog-info.yaml from repo             │
  │  ② Next refresh cycle: "Where's catalog-info.yaml?"          │
  │  ③ Backstage: "Source location is gone → mark as ORPHANED"   │
  │  ④ Entity still visible in catalog with ⚠️ warning badge     │
  │                                                              │
  │  ORPHAN STRATEGY (configurable):                             │
  │  catalog:                                                    │
  │    orphanStrategy:                                           │
  │      keep: true           ← Keep orphans indefinitely        │
  │      deleteAfterDays: 7   ← Auto-delete after 7 days         │
  │                                                              │
  │  MANUAL DELETION:                                            │
  │  DELETE /api/catalog/locations/:locationId                   │
  │  → Removes the entity immediately                            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When a service is decommissioned, the team first marks it as lifecycle: deprecated in its catalog-info.yaml, waits for consumers to migrate, then removes the YAML file. The entity becomes orphaned and is manually cleaned up after confirming no remaining dependencies.

Catalog Q109 of 200
How do you add custom metadata validations for entities?
A) Add validation rules in app-config.yaml under `catalog.validations` — YAML-based rules
B) Use the Backstage UI at /catalog/admin/validations to create custom validation forms
C) Custom validations aren't supported — Backstage only validates the standard schema fields
D) Create a custom Catalog Processor that implements the `validateEntityKind` method. Register it in the backend. The processor receives every entity and can reject invalid ones with specific error messages
✅ Correct Answer: D

Custom validations are added by creating a custom Catalog Processor that hooks into the ingestion pipeline. The processor implements validateEntityKind(entity) and returns true (valid) or false (rejected). This is where you enforce organization-specific rules like "all Components must have a PagerDuty annotation" or "production services must declare a runbook link." The processor is registered in the backend's catalog initialization.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Custom Entity Validator — Processor Pattern
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  CUSTOM VALIDATOR PROCESSOR:                                 │
  │                                                              │
  │  class AnihpjValidator implements CatalogProcessor {          │
  │    getProcessorName() { return 'AnihpjValidator'; }          │
  │                                                              │
  │    async validateEntityKind(entity) {                        │
  │      // Rule 1: All production Components need PagerDuty     │
  │      if (entity.kind === 'Component' &&                      │
  │          entity.spec.lifecycle === 'production') {           │
  │        const pd = entity.metadata.annotations?.[             │
  │          'pagerduty.com/integration-key'];                   │
  │        if (!pd) return false; // REJECT — no PagerDuty!      │
  │      }                                                       │
  │      return true; // VALID                                   │
  │    }                                                         │
  │  }                                                           │
  │                                                              │
  │  REGISTRATION (packages/backend/src/plugins/catalog.ts):     │
  │  builder.addProcessor(new AnihpjValidator());                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team created a custom validator that requires all production lifecycle components to have both a PagerDuty integration key annotation AND a TechDocs reference. This ensures every production service has alerting and documentation — enforced at the catalog level.

Catalog Q110 of 200
How do you set up a multi-tenant Catalog?
A) Deploy separate Backstage instances per tenant — multi-tenancy isn't supported within one instance
B) Use namespaces to segment entities per team/organization. Combine with catalog rules to restrict visibility per user/group. RBAC (permissions framework) controls who can view/edit entities in each namespace
C) Use the `catalog.tenants` config to define tenant boundaries and auto-assign entities
D) Backstage is single-tenant by design — multi-tenancy is on the roadmap but not yet available
✅ Correct Answer: B

Multi-tenancy in Backstage is achieved through the combination of namespaces + catalog rules + RBAC (permissions framework). Namespaces provide logical separation (team-a vs team-b entities). Catalog rules control what gets ingested. The permissions framework then controls which users/groups can READ or EDIT entities in each namespace. This is NOT true multi-tenancy with complete isolation, but a practical approach that works for most organizations.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Tenant Catalog Architecture
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SINGLE BACKSTAGE INSTANCE — LOGICAL SEGMENTATION            │
  │                                                              │
  │  ┌─────────────────┐  ┌─────────────────┐                    │
  │  │ Namespace: team-a│  │ Namespace: team-b│                   │
  │  │                  │  │                  │                   │
  │  │ jobpost-api      │  │ billing-api      │                   │
  │  │ jobpost-web      │  │ payments-svc     │                   │
  │  │ postgres-db      │  │ billing-db       │                   │
  │  └────────┬────────┘  └────────┬────────┘                   │
  │           │                    │                              │
  │     ┌─────▼─────┐        ┌─────▼─────┐                       │
  │     │ RBAC:      │        │ RBAC:      │                       │
  │     │ team-a can │        │ team-b can │                       │
  │     │ READ/WRITE │        │ READ/WRITE │                       │
  │     │ namespace  │        │ namespace  │                       │
  │     │ team-a     │        │ team-b     │                       │
  │     └───────────┘        └───────────┘                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  catalog:                                                    │
  │    rules:                                                    │
  │      - allow: [Component]                                    │
  │        metadata.namespace: [team-a, team-b]                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Currently the team uses a single default namespace. If the platform expands to support multiple product teams, they'll create team-platform, team-ml, and team-mobile namespaces, each with RBAC-restricted visibility.

Catalog Q111 of 200
What problem do Software Templates solve?
A) They eliminate the manual, error-prone process of creating new services — instead of reading wikis, copying boilerplate, configuring CI/CD, and writing K8s manifests manually, a developer fills out ONE form and gets a complete, production-ready service with everything pre-configured
B) They provide pre-built UI components for React developers — like a component library
C) They generate documentation from code comments automatically — like JSDoc but for Backstage
D) They serve as version control templates for Git repositories — like GitHub template repos
✅ Correct Answer: A

Software Templates automate the "golden path" to production. Before templates, creating a new service required: reading wiki pages (often outdated), copying boilerplate from another repo (inheriting old patterns), configuring CI/CD (easy to misconfigure), writing Kubernetes manifests (security risks), setting up monitoring, and registering in the service catalog — 4+ hours of manual work. With templates, a developer fills out one form (service name, team, language) and Backstage generates everything in seconds.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Before vs After Software Templates
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ❌ BEFORE TEMPLATES (manual, 4+ hours):                     │
  │  ① Search wiki: "How to create a service?"                   │
  │  ② Copy boilerplate from another repo                        │
  │  ③ Edit 20+ files manually (Dockerfile, k8s/*.yaml, CI)     │
  │  ④ Create GitHub repo, push code                             │
  │  ⑤ Configure CI/CD pipeline (GitHub Actions)                 │
  │  ⑥ Write Kubernetes manifests (often with errors)            │
  │  ⑦ Set up monitoring dashboard (Grafana)                     │
  │  ⑧ Configure PagerDuty alert routing                         │
  │  ⑨ Register manually in service catalog                      │
  │  ⏱️ Time: 4-8 hours     ❌ Error-prone     ❌ Inconsistent    │
  │                                                              │
  │  ✅ AFTER TEMPLATES (automated, 2 minutes):                  │
  │  ① Open Backstage → Click "Create"                           │
  │  ② Fill form: service name, team, language, port              │
  │  ③ Click "Create" → Backstage generates everything:          │
  │     • GitHub repo with correct structure                     │
  │     • Dockerfile (multi-stage, non-root)                     │
  │     • Kubernetes manifests (Deployment, Service, Ingress)    │
  │     • CI/CD pipeline (GitHub Actions, pre-configured)        │
  │     • catalog-info.yaml (auto-registered in catalog)         │
  │     • Monitoring + PagerDuty config                          │
  │  ⏱️ Time: 2 minutes       ✅ Consistent     ✅ Best practices│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The "Create Django Service" template generates a complete Django project with settings.py, Dockerfile, K8s manifests, GitHub Actions CI/CD, catalog-info.yaml, and Grafana dashboard config — all from one form. What used to take a developer 4+ hours now takes 2 minutes.

Catalog Q112 of 200
What is a "golden path" in Backstage?
A) A network route that optimizes API traffic between microservices — like a service mesh
B) The most frequently used navigation path in the Backstage UI — tracked by analytics
C) A pre-built, approved, opinionated Software Template that encodes your organization's best practices. It's the "right way" to create a new service — all the infrastructure, security, and compliance decisions are baked in by the platform team, not left to individual developers
D) A deployment pipeline that uses canary releases — gradually routing traffic to new versions
✅ Correct Answer: C

A golden path is a concept from platform engineering: instead of giving developers 100 choices (which language? which CI? which Dockerfile? which monitoring?), the platform team provides ONE well-supported, opinionated path. Developers who follow the golden path get all the benefits (auto-scaling, monitoring, security scanning, compliance) automatically. Those who don't are "on their own" — they can still build things, but they lose the platform's support.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Golden Path — The Paved Road
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  GOLDEN PATH CONCEPT:                                        │
  │                                                              │
  │  🟡 GOLDEN PATH (paved, supported, automated):               │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │  Use Template → Auto-generated repo + CI/CD + K8s    │     │
  │  │  → Auto-monitoring + PagerDuty                       │     │
  │  │  → Auto-registered in catalog                        │     │
  │  │  → Security scanning included                        │     │
  │  │  → Compliance checks pass automatically              │     │
  │  │  ⏱️  2 minutes  |  ✅ Supported  |  ✅ Best practices │     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                                                              │
  │  ⚫ OFF THE PATH (manual, unsupported, risky):                │
  │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐     │
  │  │  Write everything from scratch                        │     │
  │  │  → Manual CI/CD setup                                │     │
  │  │  → Manual K8s manifests (security review required)    │     │
  │  │  → Manual monitoring setup                           │     │
  │  │  → May miss compliance requirements                  │     │
  │  │  ⏱️  4-5 days  |  ⚠️ Self-supported  |  ❌ Risky      │     │
  │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘     │
  │                                                              │
  │  SPOTIFY'S INSIGHT: "Make the right way the EASY way"        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The "Create Django Service" template IS the golden path. It encodes decisions like: use Python 3.12, multi-stage Docker builds, non-root containers, GitHub Actions for CI/CD, PostgreSQL with PgBouncer, and Prometheus metrics on /metrics. Developers get all of this for free just by using the template.

Catalog Q113 of 200
What are the required fields in a Template YAML definition?
A) `apiVersion` (backstage.io/v1beta3 for templates), `kind: Template`, `metadata:` (name, title, description, tags), `spec:` (type, parameters, steps). Templates are entities too — they follow the same YAML structure but with template-specific spec fields
B) Only `name`, `description`, and `steps` — the rest is optional
C) `version`, `name`, `owner`, `actions` — a completely different schema from entities
D) Templates don't use YAML — they're defined in TypeScript files using `createTemplate()`
✅ Correct Answer: A

Templates are entities themselves (kind: Template) and follow the same apiVersion + kind + metadata + spec structure as catalog entities. The key difference is in spec: templates have type: service (or website, library), parameters (JSON Schema for the input form), and steps (the actions to execute). Templates use apiVersion: backstage.io/v1beta3 (newer than v1alpha1 used by catalog entities).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Template YAML — Required Structure
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  apiVersion: backstage.io/v1beta3    ← Template API version  │
  │  kind: Template                       ← Entity kind          │
  │  metadata:                                                   │
  │    name: django-service               ← Unique template name │
  │    title: Create Django Service       ← Display name         │
  │    description: Scaffolds a new Django microservice          │
  │    tags: [python, django, recommended]                       │
  │  spec:                                                       │
  │    type: service                      ← What it creates      │
  │    owner: anihpj-team                                        │
  │    parameters:                         ← User input form     │
  │      - title: Service Configuration                         │
  │        properties:                                           │
  │          name:                                               │
  │            type: string                                      │
  │            title: Service Name                               │
  │            pattern: '^[a-z][a-z0-9-]*$'                      │
  │    steps:                              ← Actions to execute  │
  │      - id: fetch                                             │
  │        action: fetch:template                                │
  │        input:                                                │
  │          url: ./skeleton                                     │
  │      - id: publish                                           │
  │        action: publish:github                                │
  │        input:                                                │
  │          repoUrl: github.com?owner=anihpj&repo=${{name}}    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The "Create Django Service" template defines all required fields plus custom parameters for the Django port, database name, and whether to include Redis caching. The template YAML lives in the Backstage repo and is registered as a Template entity in the catalog.

Catalog Q114 of 200
What is the structure of a Template's spec section?
A) `spec:` contains only `type` and `lifecycle` — exactly like a Component entity
B) `spec.type` (what kind of component it creates), `spec.parameters` (JSON Schema for user input form with title + properties), `spec.steps` (ordered list of actions: fetch:template → publish:github → catalog:register), and optional `spec.output` (what to show after creation)
C) `spec:` contains a Dockerfile reference and build instructions — templates are essentially build configurations
D) `spec:` is a flat list of shell commands that run sequentially during template execution
✅ Correct Answer: B

The template spec has four main parts: (1) type — what kind of entity this template creates (service, website, library), (2) parameters — JSON Schema defining the input form fields the developer fills out, (3) steps — ordered list of scaffolder actions that execute sequentially (typically fetch skeleton → publish to GitHub → register in catalog), (4) output (optional) — what links to show after successful creation (GitHub repo URL, catalog entity link).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Template spec — The 4 Building Blocks
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  spec:                                                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  ① type: service        ← What kind of Component to create   │
  │                                                              │
  │  ② parameters:          ← Form fields for the developer      │
  │     - title: Service Config                                  │
  │       properties:                                            │
  │         name: { type: string, title: Name }                  │
  │         port:  { type: number, title: Port, default: 8000 }  │
  │         db:    { type: boolean, title: Include DB? }         │
  │                                                              │
  │  ③ steps:               ← Actions execute in order           │
  │     - id: fetch-template                                     │
  │       action: fetch:template   ← Get skeleton code           │
  │       input: { url: ./skeleton }                             │
  │     - id: publish                                            │
  │       action: publish:github   ← Create GitHub repo          │
  │       input: { repoUrl: ..., description: ... }              │
  │     - id: register                                           │
  │       action: catalog:register ← Add to catalog              │
  │       input: { catalogInfoUrl: ... }                         │
  │                                                              │
  │  ④ output:              ← Post-creation links                │
  │     links:                                                   │
  │       - title: Repository                                    │
  │         url: ${{ steps.publish.output.remoteUrl }}           │
  │       - title: Open in Catalog                               │
  │         entityRef: ${{ steps.register.output.entityRef }}    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Django service template's spec defines parameters for service name, port (default 8000), and database inclusion. Steps fetch the Django skeleton, publish to GitHub under the anihpj org, and auto-register the new service in the catalog. The output shows links to the new repo and catalog entry.

3.3 Templates & Scaffolder Actions — Building Golden PathsQ115-Q120

Catalog Q115 of 200
How do Template parameters work?
A) Parameters are hardcoded in the template TypeScript file — developers can't customize anything
B) Parameters use JSON Schema to define the input form. Each parameter has a type (string, number, boolean), optional validation (pattern, enum, min/max), and can use UI hints (ui:widget, ui:field) to render dropdowns, password fields, etc. Values are available in steps via `${{ parameters.name }}`
C) Parameters are shell environment variables that developers set before running the template
D) Parameters are defined in a separate .env file alongside the template YAML
✅ Correct Answer: B

Template parameters use JSON Schema (and its UI extensions) to define a dynamic form. When a developer opens the template, Backstage renders the form based on the schema. Parameters support validation (regex patterns, min/max values, enums), defaults, required/optional flags, and UI customization (dropdowns via ui:field: Select, password masking, multi-line text areas). Parameter values are injected into steps using ${{ parameters.name }} syntax.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Template Parameters — JSON Schema → Form
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  spec:                                                       │
  │    parameters:                                               │
  │      - title: Service Configuration                         │
  │        required: [name, port]                                │
  │        properties:                                           │
  │          name:                                               │
  │            type: string                                      │
  │            title: Service Name                               │
  │            pattern: '^[a-z][a-z0-9-]*$'                     │
  │            description: Lowercase, hyphens allowed           │
  │          port:                                               │
  │            type: number                                      │
  │            title: Service Port                               │
  │            default: 8000                                     │
  │            enum: [8000, 8080, 3000]                          │
  │          enableRedis:                                        │
  │            type: boolean                                     │
  │            title: Include Redis Cache?                       │
  │            default: true                                     │
  │          dbEngine:                                           │
  │            type: string                                      │
  │            title: Database Engine                            │
  │            enum: [postgresql, mysql, sqlite]                 │
  │            ui:field: Select   ← Renders as dropdown          │
  │                                                              │
  │  IN STEPS: ${{ parameters.name }}, ${{ parameters.port }}    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The "Create Django Service" template asks for service name (validated: lowercase + hyphens), port (default 8000), database engine (dropdown: PostgreSQL/MySQL/SQLite), and optional Redis caching (checkbox). These parameter values flow into the Django settings.py template and K8s manifests.

Catalog Q116 of 200
What are Template "steps" and what built-in actions exist?
A) Steps are manual instructions displayed to the developer — "Step 1: Create repo, Step 2: Push code"
B) Steps are shell scripts that run on the developer's local machine
C) Steps are ordered scaffolder actions that execute server-side. Built-in actions: `fetch:template` (scaffold from skeleton), `fetch:plain` (copy files), `publish:github` (create GitHub repo), `publish:gitlab`, `catalog:register` (add to catalog), `debug:wait` (pause for debugging). Custom actions can be added
D) Steps are Docker containers that run in sequence — each step is a separate container
✅ Correct Answer: C

Steps are the execution engine of the Scaffolder — they're an ordered list of actions that run sequentially on the Backstage backend. Each step has an id, action (which action to call), and input (parameters for that action). Steps can pass data between each other using ${{ steps.[id].output.[field] }}. If any step fails, the entire template execution fails and rolls back.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Built-in Scaffolder Actions
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BUILT-IN ACTIONS (always available):                        │
  │                                                              │
  │  📥 fetch:template   ← Scaffold from Cookiecutter/Nunjucks   │
  │     input: { url: './skeleton', values: {...} }              │
  │     → Renders template files with parameter values            │
  │                                                              │
  │  📥 fetch:plain      ← Copy files without templating         │
  │     input: { url: 'https://...', targetPath: './' }          │
  │                                                              │
  │  📤 publish:github   ← Create GitHub repository + push       │
  │     input: { repoUrl: 'github.com?owner=X&repo=Y', ... }     │
  │     → Creates repo, pushes generated code, sets branch       │
  │                                                              │
  │  📤 publish:gitlab   ← Create GitLab project + push           │
  │                                                              │
  │  📋 catalog:register ← Register entity in catalog            │
  │     input: { catalogInfoUrl: 'url-to-generated-yaml' }       │
  │                                                              │
  │  🐛 debug:wait       ← Pause execution for debugging         │
  │     input: { seconds: 30 }                                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Django template uses 3 steps: fetch:template (renders the Django skeleton), publish:github (creates the GitHub repo under anihpj org), and catalog:register (auto-registers the new service so it appears in the catalog immediately after creation).

What is the Template output after execution?

B) The output is a ZIP file downloaded to the developer's machine
C) The output is a Docker image pushed to the container registry
D) There is no output — the template just shows "Success" and the developer finds their repo manually

  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: After creating a new Django service, the success page shows links to the GitHub repo, the catalog entity page (where the developer can see their new service listed), and the GitHub Actions CI/CD pipeline (so they can watch the first build run).

How does the "Create New Django Service" template work at anihpj?

  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
  │                                                              │
  │  BACKSTAGE SCAFFOLDER (server-side, ~90 seconds):            │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐       │
  │  │  Step 1: fetch:template                           │       │
  │  │  → Render skeleton with Nunjucks:                 │       │
  │  │    settings.py.j2 → settings.py (port=8000, DB=pg)│       │
  │  │    Dockerfile.j2   → Dockerfile                   │       │
  │  │    k8s/deploy.yaml.j2 → deploy.yaml               │       │
  │  │                                                   │       │
  │  │  Step 2: publish:github                           │       │
  │  │  → Create anihpj/jobpost-api on GitHub            │       │
  │  │  → Push generated code to main branch             │       │
  │  │                                                   │       │
  │  │  Step 3: catalog:register                         │       │
  │  │  → Register Component:default/jobpost-api         │       │
  │  │  → Link to GitHub repo, enable TechDocs           │       │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
  │                                                              │
  │  RESULT: Complete, production-ready Django service!          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Every new Django microservice (like jobpost-notifications or jobpost-search) is created using this template. What used to take 4+ hours of manual setup now takes 2 minutes — and every service is consistent, secure, and follows best practices from day one.

Catalog Q119 of 200
What is the difference between fetch:template and fetch:plain?
A) `fetch:template` renders template files with variable substitution (Nunjucks/Cookiecutter) — replaces `${{ parameters.name }}` with actual values. `fetch:plain` copies files as-is without any templating — used for static assets like scripts or config files that don't need parameterization
B) fetch:template downloads from GitHub, fetch:plain downloads from local filesystem
C) fetch:template is for frontend code, fetch:plain is for backend code
D) There is no difference — they're aliases for the same action
✅ Correct Answer: A

The key difference is templating. fetch:template uses a template engine (Nunjucks or Cookiecutter) to process files — ${{ parameters.name }} placeholders are replaced with actual values. This is what you use for the main skeleton code. fetch:plain copies files verbatim without any processing — useful for static assets like shell scripts, Makefiles, or configuration templates that don't need parameterization. You can use both in the same template (e.g., fetch:template for code, fetch:plain for a utility script).

📝 Detailed Explanation
fetch:template vs fetch:plain
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  fetch:template (WITH templating):                           │
  │  Skeleton file: settings.py.j2                                │
  │    DATABASES = {                                              │
  │      'default': {                                            │
  │        'ENGINE': '${{ parameters.dbEngine }}',               │
  │        'NAME': '${{ parameters.name }}_db',                   │
  │      }                                                        │
  │    }                                                          │
  │  ↓ After rendering: settings.py                               │
  │    DATABASES = {                                              │
  │      'default': {                                            │
  │        'ENGINE': 'django.db.backends.postgresql',             │
  │        'NAME': 'jobpost-api_db',                              │
  │      }                                                        │
  │    }                                                          │
  │                                                              │
  │  fetch:plain (NO templating):                                │
  │  Source file: deploy.sh                                       │
  │    #!/bin/bash                                                │
  │    kubectl apply -f k8s/                                     │
  │  ↓ Copied as-is (no variable substitution)                    │
  │    #!/bin/bash                                                │
  │    kubectl apply -f k8s/                                     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Django template uses fetch:template for the Python code (settings.py, models.py) and K8s manifests (which need service name and port injected), and fetch:plain for static helper scripts like the deployment Makefile and pre-commit hooks.

Catalog Q120 of 200
How do you define user inputs in a Template?
A) User inputs are defined in a separate HTML form file that Backstage loads
B) Under `spec.parameters`, use JSON Schema with RJSF (React JSON Schema Form) extensions. Each parameter group has a `title` and `properties`. Each property defines type, title, description, default, and optional UI hints like `ui:widget`, `ui:field`, `ui:options`
C) User inputs are command-line arguments passed to `yarn create-app`
D) Inputs are defined in the Backstage database via the /admin/templates UI
✅ Correct Answer: B

Template inputs use JSON Schema with RJSF (React JSON Schema Form) — the same library Kubernetes uses for its web forms. You define parameters as groups (each with a title and properties object). Each property can specify type (string, number, boolean, array), title, description, default, enum (for dropdowns), and ui:widget hints (password for masked input, textarea for multi-line, radio for radio buttons, Select for dropdowns).

📝 Detailed Explanation
RJSF UI Widgets — Controlling Form Appearance
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  UI WIDGETS (control how the form renders):                  │
  │                                                              │
  │  ui:widget: password   → **** masked input                   │
  │  ui:widget: textarea   → Multi-line text area                │
  │  ui:widget: radio      → Radio button group                  │
  │                                                              │
  │  UI FIELDS (change the entire component):                    │
  │  ui:field: Select      → Dropdown select                     │
  │  ui:field: OwnerPicker  → Team/owner search picker           │
  │  ui:field: RepoUrlPicker → GitHub repository picker          │
  │                                                              │
  │  VALIDATION:                                                 │
  │  pattern: '^[a-z][a-z0-9-]*$'  → Regex validation           │
  │  minLength: 3, maxLength: 50   → String length limits        │
  │  minimum: 1024, maximum: 65535 → Number range limits         │
  │  enum: [postgresql, mysql]      → Allowed values (dropdown)  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Django template uses ui:field: Select for the database engine dropdown, ui:widget: password for any secret fields (though secrets are handled via environment variables in production), and regex pattern validation to enforce anihpj's naming conventions (lowercase, hyphens, max 30 chars).

3.4 Scaffolder Deep Dive — Custom Actions & Kubernetes IntegrationQ121-Q126

Catalog Q121 of 200
How does publish:github work?
A) It opens a browser tab to github.com/new — the developer creates the repo manually
B) It forks an existing template repo — the developer then renames it
C) It creates a new GitHub repository via the GitHub API, pushes the generated code from previous steps, sets the default branch, and can configure branch protection, topics, and description. Uses the GitHub token from app-config.yaml integrations
D) It uploads code as a GitHub Gist — not a full repository
✅ Correct Answer: C

publish:github uses the GitHub API (authenticated via the token in integrations.github) to create a new repository under your organization. It pushes all the files generated by previous steps (like fetch:template) as the initial commit. You can configure the repo name, description, visibility (private/public), default branch, topics, and even branch protection rules.

📝 Detailed Explanation
publish:github — Creating Repos Programmatically
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  publish:github action:                                      │
  │    input:                                                    │
  │      repoUrl: github.com?owner=anihpj&repo=jobpost-api       │
  │      description: Job posting REST API service               │
  │      visibility: private                                     │
  │      defaultBranch: main                                     │
  │      topics: [django, rest-api, production]                  │
  │      gitAuthorName: Backstage Scaffolder                     │
  │      allowMergeCommit: false   ← Only squash merges          │
  │      deleteBranchOnMerge: true ← Clean up branches           │
  │                                                              │
  │  WHAT HAPPENS:                                               │
  │  ① Backstage calls GitHub API: POST /orgs/anihpj/repos       │
  │  ② GitHub creates empty repo                                 │
  │  ③ Backstage pushes generated files as initial commit        │
  │  ④ Sets default branch to "main"                             │
  │  ⑤ Returns remoteUrl: https://github.com/anihpj/jobpost-api  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Every Django service created through the template gets its own private GitHub repo under the anihpj organization, with topics [django, rest-api] and branch protection enabled. The initial commit includes the full generated project.

Catalog Q122 of 200
How does catalog:register work in a template?
A) After code is generated and pushed, catalog:register reads the generated catalog-info.yaml and registers the new entity in the Backstage catalog. It returns an entityRef that can be used in output links. The entity appears in the catalog immediately (no need to wait for the next refresh cycle)
B) It sends an email to the catalog admin asking them to manually approve the new entity
C) It creates a pull request to add the entity to the catalog repository
D) It schedules the entity for the next refresh cycle — it won't appear for up to 30 minutes
✅ Correct Answer: A

catalog:register is the final step in most templates — it takes the generated catalog-info.yaml URL and immediately registers it in the catalog, bypassing the normal refresh cycle. This means the new entity appears in the catalog instantly after template execution, not up to 30 minutes later. The action returns an entityRef that can be used in spec.output.links to create an "Open in Catalog" button on the success page.

📝 Detailed Explanation
catalog:register — Instant Catalog Entry
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  catalog:register action:                                    │
  │    input:                                                    │
  │      catalogInfoUrl:                                        │
  │        ${{ steps.publish.output.remoteUrl }}/blob/main/      │
  │        catalog/catalog-info.yaml                             │
  │                                                              │
  │  WHAT HAPPENS:                                               │
  │  ① Backstage reads the catalog-info.yaml from the new repo   │
  │  ② Runs it through the processor chain (validate, stitch)    │
  │  ③ Writes directly to PostgreSQL → IMMEDIATE                 │
  │  ④ Returns entityRef: component:default/jobpost-api          │
  │                                                              │
  │  ⚡ IMMEDIATE vs SCHEDULED:                                  │
  │  catalog:register → Entity visible NOW                       │
  │  Normal refresh    → Entity visible in ≤30 minutes           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: After creating a new Django service, catalog:register immediately adds it to the Backstage catalog. The developer can click "Open in Catalog" on the success page and see their brand-new service listed instantly.

Catalog Q123 of 200
How do you create a custom Scaffolder action?
A) Custom actions are defined in app-config.yaml under `scaffolder.customActions`
B) Create a new package using `yarn backstage-cli plugin:create`, implement `createTemplateAction()` with an `id`, `schema` (JSON Schema for input validation), and `handler` (async function that does the work). Register it in the backend via `createBackendModule()`
C) Write a shell script and place it in the `scaffolder/actions/` directory — Backstage auto-discovers it
D) Custom actions can only be created by Spotify — the Scaffolder API is not extensible for external users
✅ Correct Answer: B

Custom scaffolder actions extend the template system with organization-specific logic. Each action has: (1) an id (unique name like anihpj:create-jira-ticket), (2) a schema (JSON Schema defining what input the action expects), and (3) a handler (async function that receives the input and a context object). Actions can call APIs, create cloud resources, send notifications — anything you can do in Node.js.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Custom Scaffolder Action — Pattern
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  import { createTemplateAction } from '@backstage/plugin-    │
  │    scaffolder-backend';                                      │
  │                                                              │
  │  export const createJiraTicketAction = () =>                 │
  │    createTemplateAction({                                    │
  │      id: 'anihpj:create-jira-ticket',                        │
  │      schema: {                                               │
  │        input: {                                              │
  │          type: 'object',                                     │
  │          required: ['project', 'title'],                     │
  │          properties: {                                       │
  │            project: { type: 'string', title: 'Jira Project' },│
  │            title:   { type: 'string', title: 'Issue Title' } │
  │          }                                                   │
  │        }                                                     │
  │      },                                                      │
  │      handler: async (ctx) => {                               │
  │        const { project, title } = ctx.input;                 │
  │        await createJiraIssue(project, title);                │
  │        ctx.logger.info(`Created Jira issue in ${project}`);  │
  │      }                                                       │
  │    });                                                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team created a custom action `anihpj:create-jira-ticket` that automatically creates a Jira ticket in the "Platform Engineering" project whenever a new Django service is scaffolded, ensuring every new service has an onboarding task assigned.

Catalog Q124 of 200
What is the scaffolder-backend-module-kubernetes?

D) A built-in module that adds Kubernetes-related scaffolder actions: `kubernetes:apply` (apply manifests to a cluster), `kubernetes:create-namespace`, and `kubernetes:deploy`. It allows templates to directly deploy to K8s as part of the scaffolding process

The scaffolder-backend-module-kubernetes extends the Scaffolder with Kubernetes-native actions. After generating code and publishing to GitHub, you can add a step that applies Kubernetes manifests directly to your cluster. Actions include: kubernetes:apply (kubectl apply), kubernetes:create-namespace, and kubernetes:deploy. This turns the Scaffolder from "just code generation" into a full deployment pipeline.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Kubernetes Scaffolder Actions
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  TEMPLATE WITH K8S DEPLOYMENT:                               │
  │                                                              │
  │  steps:                                                      │
  │    - id: fetch      → fetch:template (generate code)         │
  │    - id: publish    → publish:github (create repo)           │

  │        input:                                                │
  │          namespace: ${{ parameters.name }}                   │
  │    - id: deploy     → kubernetes:apply                       │
  │        input:                                                │
  │          manifests:                                          │
  │            - ${{ steps.fetch.output.dir }}/k8s/deploy.yaml   │

  │          clusterName: kubeadm-prod                           │
  │                                                              │
  │  RESULT: Code in GitHub + Entity in catalog + Pods in K8s!   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team extended the Django template with kubernetes:apply — after generating code, creating the GitHub repo, and registering in the catalog, the template automatically deploys the new service to the kind-staging cluster (ksw → 4) for immediate testing.

Catalog Q125 of 200
How do you write the skeleton code for a complete template?
A) Write the code in a single TypeScript file using the `createSkeleton()` API
B) Create a `template/` directory (or `skeleton/`) with the template files using Nunjucks syntax (`${{ values.name }}`). Include a `.template` suffix on files that need processing. Organize by subdirectory: `src/`, `k8s/`, `.github/`. The template YAML references this directory via `fetch:template`'s `url` input
C) Use the Backstage UI template editor at /create/templates/editor
D) Write the skeleton in JSON format — Backstage converts JSON to actual files
✅ Correct Answer: B

The skeleton is a directory of template files using Nunjucks syntax. Files that need parameter substitution use ${{ values.name }}, ${{ values.port }}, etc. Files that don't need processing (like static scripts) are copied as-is. The skeleton lives alongside the template YAML, referenced by fetch:template's url: ./template (relative path) or a public URL.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Template Skeleton Structure
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  templates/django-service/                                   │
  │  ├── template.yaml              ← Template definition        │
  │  └── skeleton/                  ← Code skeleton              │
  │      ├── src/                                               │
  │      │   ├── ${{ values.name }}/                            │
  │      │   │   ├── settings.py    ← Nunjucks: ${{ values.port }}│
  │      │   │   ├── urls.py                                    │
  │      │   │   └── wsgi.py                                    │
  │      │   └── manage.py                                      │
  │      ├── Dockerfile             ← Nunjucks: ${{ values.port }}│
  │      ├── k8s/                                               │
  │      │   ├── deploy.yaml        ← Nunjucks: ${{ values.name }}│
  │      │   └── service.yaml       ← Nunjucks: ${{ values.port }}│
  │      ├── .github/workflows/                                 │
  │      │   └── ci.yml             ← Nunjucks: ${{ values.name }}│
  │      ├── catalog-info.yaml      ← Nunjucks templated        │
  │      └── Makefile               ← Static (no templating)    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Django template skeleton uses Nunjucks to inject the service name, port, database engine, and Redis flag into settings.py, Dockerfile, K8s manifests, and catalog-info.yaml. The Makefile and pre-commit hooks are static — copied verbatim without templating.
