3.5 TechDocs & Production TroubleshootingQ127-Q136

Catalog Q127 of 200
What is TechDocs and what problem does it solve?
A) TechDocs is Backstage's documentation-as-code engine. It solves the "docs drift" problem — documentation lives in the same repo as the code (Markdown → MkDocs → beautiful site), so docs are versioned alongside code, reviewed in PRs, and never go stale because they're part of the development workflow
B) TechDocs is a cloud-hosted wiki that replaces Confluence — Backstage hosts it for you
C) TechDocs generates API documentation from code comments — like Javadoc or Sphinx
D) TechDocs is a search engine that indexes all your organization's documentation
✅ Correct Answer: A

TechDocs implements the docs-as-code philosophy: documentation lives in the same Git repository as the code it describes. Developers write docs in Markdown (the same format as READMEs), define structure in mkdocs.yml, and TechDocs builds a beautiful, searchable documentation site embedded directly in the Backstage entity page. Because docs are co-located with code, they're versioned together, reviewed in pull requests alongside code changes, and never drift out of sync.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Docs-as-Code — Documentation Lives WITH Code
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ❌ TRADITIONAL DOCS (drift, stale, disconnected):           │
  │  Code in GitHub → Docs in Confluence/Wiki                    │
  │  → Docs updated separately (or never)                        │
  │  → "Is this doc for v1 or v2?" — nobody knows                │
  │  → Docs review is separate from code review                  │
  │                                                              │
  │  ✅ TECHDOCS (docs-as-code, always in sync):                 │
  │  Same repo: code/ + docs/                                    │
  │  → Docs versioned with code (git tag v1.2 = docs for v1.2)  │
  │  → "Update the API? Update the docs in the same PR"          │
  │  → Docs review = code review (same PR, same reviewers)       │
  │  → Built and published automatically by Backstage            │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Every Django service's repo includes a docs/ folder with API reference, architecture decision records, and runbooks — all in Markdown. TechDocs builds these into a documentation site accessible from the entity page at backstage.anihpj.com/docs/default/component/jobpost-api.

Catalog Q128 of 200
How does TechDocs generate documentation from Markdown?
A) TechDocs renders Markdown directly in the browser using a JavaScript Markdown parser
B) TechDocs uses MkDocs (Python-based static site generator) with the `mkdocs-techdocs-core` plugin. It reads docs/*.md + mkdocs.yml → generates static HTML/CSS → publishes to storage (S3/GCS/local) → Backstage serves it. The build runs on the Backstage backend or in a separate Docker container
C) TechDocs converts Markdown to PDF and stores it in the Backstage database
D) TechDocs uses GitHub Pages — it commits rendered HTML back to the repo's gh-pages branch
✅ Correct Answer: B

TechDocs uses MkDocs, a Python-based static site generator, enhanced with the mkdocs-techdocs-core plugin. The build process: (1) Backstage detects the backstage.io/techdocs-ref annotation on an entity, (2) clones the repo or reads the docs directory, (3) runs MkDocs in Docker (or locally) to generate static HTML, (4) publishes the HTML to object storage (local, S3, GCS, or Azure), (5) serves it from the Backstage backend at /docs/:namespace/:kind/:name.

📝 Detailed Explanation
TechDocs Build Pipeline
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  SOURCE                BUILD                 PUBLISH          │
  │  ──────                ─────                 ───────          │
  │  jobpost-api/          MkDocs               Storage           │
  │  ├── docs/         ┌──────────┐       ┌──────────────┐      │
  │  │   ├── index.md  │ mkdocs   │──►    │ S3 / GCS /   │      │
  │  │   ├── api.md    │ build    │       │ local dir    │      │
  │  │   └── adrs/     │ (Docker  │       └──────┬───────┘      │
  │  └── mkdocs.yml    │ or local)│              │               │
  │                    └──────────┘              ▼               │
  │                                        ┌──────────┐        │
  │                                        │Backstage │        │
  │                                        │  serves  │        │
  │                                        │ /docs/...│        │
  │                                        └──────────┘        │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: TechDocs builds run in Docker on the Backstage backend. When a developer updates docs in the jobpost-api repo, the next catalog refresh detects the change and triggers a rebuild, publishing updated HTML to the anihpj-techdocs S3 bucket.

Catalog Q129 of 200
What is the TechDocs annotation and how is it used?
A) It's a comment in the code that TechDocs reads to generate API documentation
B) It's a label on the GitHub repo that enables TechDocs for that repository
C) It's a setting in the Backstage UI — you enable TechDocs per entity with a toggle button
D) `backstage.io/techdocs-ref` in the entity's catalog-info.yaml tells TechDocs where to find the documentation source. Values: `dir:.` (docs at repo root), `url:https://...` (external docs), or `github:anihpj/jobpost/tree/main/docs` (specific GitHub path)
✅ Correct Answer: D

The backstage.io/techdocs-ref annotation is the critical link between an entity and its documentation. It tells TechDocs WHERE to find the Markdown source. Common values: dir:. (docs at the repository root — most common), url:https://docs.example.com (external documentation site), or a specific path like github:anihpj/jobpost/tree/main/docs. Without this annotation, TechDocs won't know where to look — the entity page will show "No documentation available."

📝 Detailed Explanation
TechDocs Annotation — The Key to Documentation
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  catalog-info.yaml:                                          │
  │  metadata:                                                   │
  │    annotations:                                              │
  │      backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.   ← WHERE docs live    │
  │                                                              │
  │  REFERENCE TYPES:                                            │
  │  dir:.              → Docs at repository root                │
  │  dir:./docs         → Docs in docs/ subdirectory             │
  │  url:https://...    → External documentation URL             │
  │  github:anihpj/jobpost/tree/main/docs → Specific GitHub path │
  │                                                              │
  │  WITHOUT THIS ANNOTATION:                                    │
  │  Entity page → "No documentation available" 😢               │
  │                                                              │
  │  WITH THIS ANNOTATION:                                       │
  │  Entity page → TechDocs tab with full MkDocs site 🎉         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Every service entity has backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:. pointing to the repo root. This is where mkdocs.yml lives alongside the docs/ folder. Setting this annotation is one of the first things the Django template does.

Catalog Q130 of 200
What is the mkdocs.yml file and what does it contain?
A) MkDocs configuration file at the repo root. Defines: site_name, nav (navigation structure — which pages appear in which order), theme (mkdocs or material), plugins (mkdocs-techdocs-core, mermaid for diagrams), and markdown_extensions (admonitions, code highlighting). This file is REQUIRED for TechDocs to build
B) A YAML file that defines what Backstage catalogs should be scanned — unrelated to TechDocs
C) A Docker Compose file that runs MkDocs in a container alongside the application
D) A configuration file for Kubernetes — used to deploy MkDocs as a separate service
✅ Correct Answer: A

mkdocs.yml is the required configuration file for MkDocs-based TechDocs. It must be at the location referenced by the techdocs-ref annotation (typically repo root). It defines the documentation site's structure: the site name, navigation hierarchy (which Markdown files appear in which order in the sidebar), the theme, enabled plugins (mkdocs-techdocs-core is mandatory for Backstage compatibility), and Markdown extensions (like Mermaid diagrams or admonition callouts).

📝 Detailed Explanation
mkdocs.yml — Required Structure
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  site_name: jobpost-api Documentation                        │
  │  site_description: REST API for the Job Posting platform     │
  │                                                              │
  │  nav:                                                        │
  │    - Home: index.md                                          │
  │    - API Reference: api-reference.md                         │
  │    - Architecture: architecture.md                           │
  │    - Runbooks:                                               │
  │      - Database Outage: runbooks/database-outage.md          │
  │      - High Latency: runbooks/high-latency.md                │
  │    - ADRs:                                                   │
  │      - ADR-001: adrs/001-use-postgres.md                     │
  │      - ADR-002: adrs/002-use-redis.md                        │
  │                                                              │
  │  plugins:                                                    │
  │    - techdocs-core        ← REQUIRED for Backstage           │
  │                                                              │
  │  markdown_extensions:                                        │
  │    - admonition           ← !!! note callouts                │
  │    - pymdownx.superfences ← Nested code blocks               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Each Django service's mkdocs.yml defines its own navigation — API reference, architecture docs, runbooks, and ADRs. The Django template auto-generates a basic mkdocs.yml so every new service has documentation structure from day one.

Catalog Q131 of 200
What are ADRs and how do they appear in Backstage?
A) ADRs are API Dependency Records — they track which APIs each service consumes
B) ADRs are Automated Deployment Reports — generated after each deployment
C) Architecture Decision Records — Markdown files that document WHY a technical decision was made (context, options considered, decision, consequences). They live in docs/adrs/ and appear in TechDocs under the navigation defined in mkdocs.yml
D) ADRs are Access Delegation Rules — they control who can access which Backstage plugins
✅ Correct Answer: C

Architecture Decision Records (ADRs) capture the context and reasoning behind technical decisions. Each ADR follows a standard format: Title, Status (proposed/accepted/deprecated), Context (what problem are we solving?), Decision (what did we choose?), and Consequences (what are the trade-offs?). Because ADRs are just Markdown files in the repo, they're versioned with the code and rendered beautifully by TechDocs. Six months later, when someone asks "why did we choose PostgreSQL over MongoDB?", the answer is in the ADR.

📝 Detailed Explanation
ADR Format — Standard Template
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # ADR-003: Use Redis for Caching and Task Queue              │
  │                                                              │
  │  Status: Accepted                                            │
  │  Date: 2024-01-15                                            │
  │                                                              │
  │  ## Context                                                  │
  │  Job posting data needs sub-100ms response times. Django's   │
  │  default cache is in-memory (not shared across pods). We     │
  │  also need a task queue for async email notifications.       │
  │                                                              │
  │  ## Decision                                                 │
  │  Use Redis 7 for BOTH caching and Celery task queue.         │
  │  Single infrastructure component, two use cases.             │
  │                                                              │
  │  ## Alternatives Considered                                  │
  │  - Memcached: Good for caching, no task queue support        │
  │  - RabbitMQ: Good for tasks, overkill for caching            │
  │  - PostgreSQL LISTEN/NOTIFY: No persistence for tasks        │
  │                                                              │
  │  ## Consequences                                             │
  │  ✅ One Redis cluster vs two different infra components      │
  │  ✅ Shared operational knowledge (one thing to learn)        │
  │  ⚠️  Cache eviction could impact task queue under memory press│
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The jobpost-api repo contains ADRs in docs/adrs/ — decisions about using PostgreSQL, Redis, Django REST Framework, and multi-stage Docker builds are all documented. New team members read the ADRs to understand WHY the architecture is the way it is, not just WHAT it is.

Catalog Q132 of 200
How do you add runbooks to TechDocs?
A) Runbooks are created in the Backstage UI using a special /runbooks editor
B) Create Markdown files in docs/runbooks/ with step-by-step incident response procedures. Add them to mkdocs.yml navigation under "Runbooks". Each runbook covers a specific failure scenario with diagnostic commands, mitigation steps, and escalation paths
C) Runbooks are linked from PagerDuty — they must be hosted on PagerDuty's platform
D) Runbooks are auto-generated from Kubernetes events when an incident occurs
✅ Correct Answer: B

Runbooks are operational guides that tell on-call engineers exactly what to do when something breaks. They're written in Markdown and live alongside the service code, organized in docs/runbooks/. Each runbook covers a specific scenario: database connection failure, high latency, disk full, memory leak. Because they're in TechDocs, they're versioned, searchable, and accessible directly from the entity page — no more digging through Confluence at 3 AM.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Runbook Example — Database Outage
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  # Runbook: Database Connection Failure                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  ## Symptoms                                                 │
  │  - PagerDuty alert: "jobpost-api error rate > 5%"            │
  │  - Grafana: PostgreSQL connection pool exhausted             │
  │  - kubectl logs: "connect ECONNREFUSED postgres-svc:5432"    │
  │                                                              │
  │  ## Immediate Mitigation (5 min)                             │
  │  ① Check if PostgreSQL pod is Running                        │
  │     kubectl get pods -l app=postgres -n backstage            │
  │  ② If not Running: check logs                                │
  │     kubectl logs deployment/postgres -n backstage --tail=50  │
  │  ③ If Running but unreachable: check NetworkPolicy           │
  │     kubectl get networkpolicy -n backstage                    │
  │                                                              │
  │  ## Escalation                                               │
  │  - If DB pod is CrashLoopBackOff → escalate to DBA team      │
  │  - If disk full → escalate to Infrastructure team            │
  │  - PagerDuty escalation policy: anihpj-db-oncall             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Each Django service has runbooks in docs/runbooks/ covering database outages, Redis failures, high API latency, and deployment rollbacks. During an incident, on-call engineers open the entity page, click TechDocs → Runbooks, and follow the step-by-step guide.

Catalog Q133 of 200
How does the TechDocs build process detect changes?
A) During each catalog refresh cycle, Backstage checks if the entity's source repo has new commits since the last TechDocs build. If the docs have changed (or never been built), it triggers a rebuild. Builds are also triggered manually via the "Rebuild Docs" button on the entity page
B) TechDocs uses GitHub webhooks for real-time change detection — builds trigger within seconds of a push
C) Developers must manually run `yarn techdocs:build` on their local machine after each docs change
D) TechDocs checks for changes once daily at midnight — builds are not triggered during the day
✅ Correct Answer: A

TechDocs uses the catalog refresh cycle to detect documentation changes. On each refresh, Backstage checks the Git commit SHA of the entity's source repo. If it differs from the last built version, a rebuild is triggered. Additionally, developers can manually trigger a rebuild via the "Rebuild Docs" button on the entity page's TechDocs tab — useful when you've just pushed docs and don't want to wait for the refresh cycle.

📝 Detailed Explanation
TechDocs Change Detection
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  AUTO DETECTION (catalog refresh cycle):                     │
  │  ① Catalog refresh runs (every 30 min default)               │
  │  ② For each entity with techdocs-ref annotation:             │
  │     → Check Git commit SHA of source repo                    │
  │     → Compare with SHA of last successful build              │
  │     → If different → TRIGGER REBUILD                         │
  │  ③ Build runs (MkDocs in Docker)                             │
  │  ④ New HTML published to storage                             │
  │                                                              │
  │  MANUAL TRIGGER (immediate):                                  │
  │  Entity page → TechDocs tab → "Rebuild Docs" button          │
  │  → Triggers build immediately (no waiting for refresh)       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: When developers push docs changes to the jobpost-api repo, the next catalog refresh (within 30 minutes) detects the new commit and rebuilds. For urgent doc fixes, they use the "Rebuild Docs" button on the entity page in Backstage.

Catalog Q134 of 200
Where can TechDocs store generated documentation?
A) Only on the local filesystem of the Backstage server
B) Only in the Backstage PostgreSQL database as BLOB storage
C) Only in GitHub repositories — TechDocs commits rendered HTML back to a docs-built branch
D) Local filesystem (dev), Google Cloud Storage (GCS), AWS S3, or Azure Blob Storage — configured via `techdocs.publisher.type`. For production, object storage (S3/GCS/Azure) is recommended for durability and multi-replica access
✅ Correct Answer: D

TechDocs supports multiple storage backends for generated documentation. In development, the local filesystem is fine (simple, no cloud dependency). In production, object storage (S3, GCS, Azure Blob) is strongly recommended because: (1) multiple Backstage replicas can access the same docs, (2) durability — data survives pod restarts, (3) scalability — object storage handles large documentation sets efficiently.

📝 Detailed Explanation
TechDocs Storage Options
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  techdocs:                                                   │
  │    publisher:                                                │
  │      type: 'awsS3'    ← Storage backend choice               │
  │      awsS3:                                                  │
  │        bucketName: anihpj-techdocs                           │
  │        region: us-east-1                                     │
  │                                                              │
  │  STORAGE BACKENDS:                                           │
  │  📁 local         → Local filesystem (dev only)              │
  │  🪣 awsS3         → AWS S3 bucket                            │
  │  🪣 googleGcs     → Google Cloud Storage                     │
  │  🪣 azureBlob     → Azure Blob Storage                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  WHY OBJECT STORAGE IN PROD:                                 │
  │  ✅ Multiple Backstage replicas read same docs               │
  │  ✅ Survives pod restarts (stateless Backstage)              │
  │  ✅ Built-in replication and durability                       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Production TechDocs uses AWS S3 for storage. Dev environments use local filesystem. The publisher config is environment-specific — app-config.production.yaml sets S3 while app-config.yaml defaults to local.

Catalog Q135 of 200
What makes a good Software Template?
A) Maximum flexibility — templates should expose every possible configuration option as a parameter
B) Minimal parameters — just a name field, everything else is hardcoded
C) Opinionated best practices baked in (security, monitoring, CI/CD), sensible defaults so developers don't need to make every decision, clear parameter descriptions, validation to prevent mistakes, and post-creation output links so developers know where their new service is
D) Templates should be exact replicas of the last service created — copy-paste is the gold standard
✅ Correct Answer: C

A good template follows the "make the right way the easy way" principle. It bakes in best practices (non-root containers, health checks, metrics endpoints, security scanning) so developers get them automatically. It provides sensible defaults (port 8000, PostgreSQL, include Redis) so developers don't face decision paralysis. But it also offers customization (changing the port, choosing a different DB) for legitimate variations. The key insight: constrain the choices that cause problems, expose the choices that differentiate services.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Good Template Design — The Balance
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  🟢 FORCE (baked in, no choice needed):                      │
  │  • Multi-stage Docker builds                                 │
  │  • Non-root container user                                   │
  │  • Prometheus /metrics endpoint                              │
  │  • GitHub Actions CI/CD                                      │
  │  • catalog-info.yaml auto-registration                       │
  │  → These are ALWAYS right. No need for developer input.      │
  │                                                              │
  │  🟡 DEFAULT (sensible default, can override):                │
  │  • Port: 8000 (changeable)                                   │
  │  • Database: PostgreSQL (can choose MySQL/SQLite)            │
  │  • Redis: include (can opt out)                              │
  │  → Smart defaults reduce decision fatigue.                   │
  │                                                              │
  │  🔵 ASK (must be specified by developer):                    │
  │  • Service name                                              │
  │  • Owning team                                               │
  │  • Description                                               │
  │  → These genuinely vary per service.                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The Django template bakes in security (non-root, distroless), monitoring (Prometheus endpoint), and CI/CD (GitHub Actions). It defaults to PostgreSQL and Redis (the standard anihpj stack) but allows MySQL for legacy integrations. The only truly required inputs are the service name and owning team.

Catalog Q136 of 200
How do you test a template before publishing?
A) Templates can't be tested — publish to production and fix issues as developers report them
B) Run Backstage locally with `yarn dev`, navigate to /create, and execute the template to a test GitHub org. Verify the generated code, test the CI/CD pipeline, and check the catalog entry. Fix issues, re-test, then promote to production only when it works end-to-end
C) Use the `yarn template:validate` command — it runs static analysis on the template YAML
D) Templates have a "dry run" mode that simulates execution without creating real resources
✅ Correct Answer: B

Template testing follows a staged promotion pipeline — never test templates directly in production: (1) Local development — run Backstage locally with yarn dev, configure it to use a test GitHub organization (NOT the production org), navigate to /create, and execute the template end-to-end. Verify every step: the generated code is correct (correct Dockerfile, correct k8s manifests, correct Django settings), the CI/CD pipeline in the test repo runs green, the catalog entry appears with correct metadata, and the TechDocs build succeeds. Fix issues, re-test, iterate until everything works perfectly. (2) Staging environment — deploy the tested template to the staging Backstage instance (kind-cl2), execute it (publishing to a staging GitHub org), and perform a full integration test including other plugins that interact with the new entity (metrics, CI/CD, PagerDuty). (3) Production — only after staging validation passes, deploy the template to the production Backstage (kubeadm). The template is now available to all developers with confidence that it works correctly. This staged approach catches problems early — broken templates in production frustrate developers and erode trust in the portal.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Template Testing — Staged Promotion Pipeline
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  ① LOCAL DEV (Laptop — yarn dev)                            ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  Backstage → /create → Execute template                     ║
  ║  → Publishes to: test-github-org/anihpj-test (NOT prod!)    ║
  ║  → Verify: ✅ Code correct  ✅ CI/CD green                   ║
  ║           ✅ Catalog entry   ✅ TechDocs built               ║
  ║  → Fix issues → Re-test → Repeat until perfect              ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  ② STAGING (kind-cl2 — ksw → 4)                             ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  Deploy updated template → staging Backstage                 ║
  ║  → Execute → Publishes to: staging-github-org               ║
  ║  → Integration test: Metrics plugin, CI/CD plugin, PagerDuty║
  ║  → Verify all plugins work with the new entity              ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  ③ PRODUCTION (kubeadm — ksw → 1)                           ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  Deploy to production Backstage                              ║
  ║  → Template available to ALL developers                     ║
  ║  → Confidence: tested locally + staging = works correctly   ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

For anihpj/jobpost: New templates (like the Django service template) are first tested on a developer's laptop with yarn dev, publishing to a test GitHub org (anihpj-test). Once the generated Django project has the correct structure, passes CI, and registers in the catalog, the template is promoted to the staging Backstage on kind-cl2. After a full integration test there (metrics plugins, CI/CD plugins, PagerDuty integration all work with the new entity), the template is deployed to the production Backstage on kubeadm. Template modifications follow the same pipeline — a PR changes the template YAML, the reviewer tests locally, and only after staging validation does it reach production. This ensures developers always have working, tested templates available in /create.

🟣 Category 4: Customizing Backstage (32%)Q137-Q200

🟣 Advanced: Plugins, React Code & Theming

This is the LARGEST domain (32%) — and where Backstage truly shines. You'll learn about frontend vs backend plugins, customizing existing plugins, writing React code in the Backstage app, using Material UI components for consistent design, and building/extending the plugin ecosystem. For anihpj/jobpost, this is where the team built anihpj-theme, anihpj-templates, and custom scaffolder actions.

Q137-Q200 64 Questions 🟣 Advanced

4.1 Plugins — Frontend vs Backend, Structure & CreationQ137-Q152

Customizing Q137 of 200
What is a Backstage plugin made of?
A) A single React component file — plugins are just UI widgets
B) A Docker container that runs alongside Backstage and communicates via gRPC
C) A TypeScript package that can contain: a frontend half (React components, extensions, API clients) AND/OR a backend half (Express routers, database access, service logic). Plugins use `createPlugin()` (frontend) and `createBackendPlugin()` (backend) from @backstage/core
D) A YAML configuration file that Backstage reads to generate UI and API endpoints
✅ Correct Answer: C

A Backstage plugin is a TypeScript package that extends Backstage's functionality. It has a well-defined structure: (1) Frontend half — React components rendered at specific routes or as entity page tabs, using createPlugin() and createRoutableExtension(), (2) Backend half — Express.js routers, database operations, and external API calls, using createBackendPlugin(). A plugin can be frontend-only (like a theme), backend-only (like a catalog provider), or both (like the GitHub plugin).

📝 Detailed Explanation
Plugin Anatomy — Two Halves, One Package
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  plugins/my-plugin/                                          │
  │  ├── package.json       ← @backstage/plugin-my-plugin        │
  │  ├── src/                                                   │
  │  │   ├── index.ts       ← Public API exports                 │
  │  │   ├── plugin.ts      ← createPlugin() — frontend instance │
  │  │   ├── components/    ← React components (frontend)        │
  │  │   │   └── MyWidget.tsx                                   │
  │  │   ├── api/          ← API client (frontend → backend)    │
  │  │   └── routes.ts     ← Routable extensions                │
  │  └── dev/              ← Development harness                 │
  │                                                              │
  │  FRONTEND PLUGIN (React):                                    │
  │  export const myPlugin = createPlugin({                      │
  │    id: 'my-plugin',                                          │
  │    routes: {                                                 │
  │      root: rootRoute,                                       │
  │    },                                                        │
  │  });                                                         │
  │                                                              │
  │  BACKEND PLUGIN (Express):                                   │
  │  export const myBackendPlugin = createBackendPlugin({        │
  │    pluginId: 'my-plugin',                                    │
  │    register(env) {                                           │
  │      env.registerInit({ deps: {...}, init: async ({...})     │
  │        => { /* Express routes, DB, services */ } });          │
  │    },                                                        │
  │  });                                                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The anihpj-theme plugin is frontend-only (React components + theme provider). The anihpj-templates plugin has both halves: frontend (custom template picker UI) and backend (custom scaffolder actions for Django-specific operations).

Customizing Q138 of 200
How do you create a frontend plugin?
A) Run `yarn backstage-cli plugin:create` → choose "frontend" → it scaffolds: src/plugin.ts (createPlugin), src/index.ts (exports), src/components/ (React components), and src/routes.ts (routing). Then `yarn add --cwd packages/app @internal/plugin-name` and export from plugins.ts
B) Create a new React app with create-react-app and mount it inside an iframe in Backstage
C) Write a single .tsx file and place it in packages/app/src/plugins/ — Backstage auto-discovers it
D) Use the Backstage Plugin Builder UI at /admin/plugins/create — a visual plugin creator
✅ Correct Answer: A

Creating a frontend plugin follows a standard workflow: (1) yarn backstage-cli plugin:create scaffolds the plugin package with correct structure, (2) implement React components using Backstage's component library and hooks, (3) add the plugin as a dependency to packages/app, (4) export the plugin from packages/app/src/plugins.ts, (5) the plugin's routable extensions are now available at the configured routes. Hot reload during yarn dev supports instant feedback.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Frontend Plugin Creation — Step by Step
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① SCAFFOLD:                                                │
  │  yarn backstage-cli plugin:create                            │
  │  → Choose "frontend" plugin                                  │
  │  → Enter plugin ID: anihpj-dashboard                         │
  │  → Generates:                                                │
  │    plugins/anihpj-dashboard/                                 │
  │    ├── src/plugin.ts    ← createPlugin({ id: '...' })       │
  │    ├── src/index.ts     ← Exports for consumers              │
  │    ├── src/components/  ← Your React components              │
  │    └── src/routes.ts    ← Routable extensions               │
  │                                                              │
  │  ② IMPLEMENT:                                               │
  │  Write React components in src/components/                   │
  │  Use @backstage/core-plugin-api hooks                        │
  │  Use @backstage/core-components for UI                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  ③ REGISTER:                                                │
  │  packages/app/package.json:                                  │
  │    "@internal/plugin-anihpj-dashboard": "workspace:^"        │
  │  packages/app/src/plugins.ts:                                │
  │    export { anihpjDashboardPlugin } from                     │
  │      '@internal/plugin-anihpj-dashboard';                    │
  │                                                              │
  │  ④ RUN: yarn dev → Plugin appears in Backstage!             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team created anihpj-dashboard as a frontend plugin showing team-specific metrics: services owned, deployment frequency, open PRs, and PagerDuty incident counts — all on one custom dashboard page.

Customizing Q139 of 200
How do you create a backend plugin?
A) Write an Express.js app separately and proxy requests from Backstage to it
B) Use `createBackendPlugin()` with `createBackendModule()`. Define a plugin ID, register init handlers with dependencies (logger, database, HTTP router), create Express routes, and add to packages/backend/src/index.ts via `backend.add(import(...))`
C) Backend plugins are just database migration scripts — they don't contain application logic
D) Create a Lambda function and configure Backstage to invoke it via the proxy plugin
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backend plugins are created using createBackendPlugin() and the new backend system's module pattern. The plugin declares its dependencies (HTTP router, database, logger, config), registers initialization handlers, creates Express routes, and connects to databases or external APIs. It's added to the backend via backend.add(import('...')) in packages/backend/src/index.ts.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Backend Plugin — Module Pattern
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  plugins/anihpj-backend/src/plugin.ts:                       │
  │                                                              │
  │  export const anihpjPlugin = createBackendPlugin({           │
  │    pluginId: 'anihpj',                                       │
  │    register(env) {                                           │
  │      env.registerInit({                                      │
  │        deps: {                                               │
  │          http: coreServices.httpRouter,                      │
  │          logger: coreServices.logger,                        │
  │          database: coreServices.database,                    │
  │          config: coreServices.rootConfig,                    │
  │        },                                                    │
  │        async init({ http, logger, database, config }) {      │
  │          const router = await http.use('/api/anihpj');       │
  │          router.get('/health', (req, res) => {               │
  │            res.json({ status: 'ok' });                       │
  │          });                                                 │
  │          logger.info('Anihpj backend plugin initialized');   │
  │        },                                                    │
  │      });                                                     │
  │    },                                                        │
  │  });                                                         │
  │                                                              │
  │  REGISTRATION (packages/backend/src/index.ts):               │
  │  backend.add(import('@internal/plugin-anihpj-backend'));     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The anihpj-templates backend plugin registers custom scaffolder actions (anihpj:create-jira-ticket, anihpj:configure-monitoring) and exposes an API endpoint at /api/anihpj/metrics that the frontend dashboard plugin consumes.

Customizing Q140 of 200
What is the difference between a frontend plugin and a backend plugin?
A) There is no difference — all Backstage plugins contain both frontend and backend code
B) Frontend plugins run in Docker, backend plugins run in Kubernetes
C) Frontend plugins are written in JavaScript, backend plugins are written in Python
D) Frontend plugins render UI in the browser (React components, routes, entity page tabs). Backend plugins run server-side (Express routes, database operations, external API calls). They communicate through Backstage's plugin API — frontend calls backend via `@backstage/core-plugin-api`'s `useApi()` and `createApiRef()`
✅ Correct Answer: D

The frontend/backend split follows the client-server architecture. Frontend plugins run in the browser — they render React components, handle user interactions, and call backend APIs. Backend plugins run on the server — they process data, connect to databases and external services, and expose REST endpoints. The two halves communicate through Backstage's API system: the frontend defines an ApiRef and uses useApi() to call it; the backend provides the implementation.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Frontend ↔ Backend Communication
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BROWSER (Frontend Plugin)                                   │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐       │
  │  │  React Component                                 │       │
  │  │  │  useApi(anihpjApiRef).getMetrics()            │       │
  │  │  ▼                                               │       │
  │  │  API Client (fetch wrapper)                      │       │
  │  │  │  GET /api/anihpj/metrics                      │       │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
  │                         │                                    │
  │  ───────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────      │
  │                         │ HTTP Request                       │
  │                         ▼                                    │
  │  SERVER (Backend Plugin)                                     │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────┐       │
  │  │  Express Router                                  │       │
  │  │  │  router.get('/metrics', handler)              │       │
  │  │  ▼                                               │       │
  │  │  Business Logic (database query, API call)       │       │
  │  │  ▼                                               │       │
  │  │  Response: { services: 42, deployments: 150 }    │       │
  │  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────┘       │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The anihpj-dashboard frontend plugin calls the anihpj-backend plugin's /api/anihpj/metrics endpoint. The frontend renders charts; the backend queries PostgreSQL and the GitHub API to gather the raw data.

Customizing Q141 of 200
What is createPlugin() and what does it return?
A) It creates a new directory with plugin scaffolding — the same as `yarn backstage-cli plugin:create`
B) It's a database function that registers the plugin in PostgreSQL
C) It creates a frontend plugin instance with a unique `id`, optional `routes` (for page-level plugins), `apis` (API factories), and `extensions` (extension points). Returns a `BackstagePlugin` object that's exported and consumed by the Backstage app
D) It's a CLI command that publishes the plugin to the Backstage Plugin Marketplace
✅ Correct Answer: C

createPlugin() (from @backstage/core-plugin-api) is the entry point for every frontend plugin. It creates a plugin instance with: (1) a unique id (used for routing, API namespacing, and extension point scoping), (2) optional routes (defines where the plugin appears in the Backstage navigation), (3) optional apis (API factories that provide backend communication), and (4) extensions (extension points for other plugins to hook into).

📝 Detailed Explanation
createPlugin() — The Plugin Entry Point
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  import { createPlugin, createRoutableExtension }            │
  │    from '@backstage/core-plugin-api';                        │
  │                                                              │
  │  export const anihpjPlugin = createPlugin({                  │
  │    id: 'anihpj-dashboard',    ← Unique plugin identifier     │
  │    routes: {                                                 │
  │      root: rootRoute,         ← Main page route              │
  │    },                                                        │
  │    apis: [                                                  │
  │      createApiFactory({                                     │
  │        api: anihpjApiRef,                                   │
  │        deps: { fetchApi: fetchApiRef },                     │
  │        factory: ({ fetchApi }) => new AnihpjClient(fetchApi),│
  │      }),                                                     │
  │    ],                                                        │
  │  });                                                         │
  │                                                              │
  │  // Create a routable page extension:                        │
  │  export const AnihpjPage = anihpjPlugin.provide(            │
  │    createRoutableExtension({                                 │
  │      name: 'AnihpjPage',                                     │
  │      component: () => import('./components/Dashboard').then( │
  │        m => m.Dashboard),                                    │
  │      mountPoint: rootRoute,                                  │
  │    })                                                        │
  │  );                                                          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The anihpj-dashboard plugin uses createPlugin() to register at the /anihpj route in Backstage's sidebar navigation, with an API factory for backend communication. Developers access it by clicking "Anihpj" in the sidebar.

Customizing Q142 of 200
How do plugins communicate between frontend and backend?
A) Through Backstage's API system: the frontend defines an `ApiRef` (typed interface) and an `ApiFactory` (how to create the client). The backend implements Express routes. The frontend calls `useApi(myApiRef)` in React components to get a typed client that makes HTTP requests to the backend's routes
B) Through WebSockets — a persistent connection between browser and server
C) Through shared PostgreSQL tables — frontend reads data the backend wrote
D) Through the browser's localStorage — the backend writes to localStorage via a special API
✅ Correct Answer: A

Backstage's API system is the bridge between frontend and backend. It follows a three-part pattern: (1) ApiRef — a typed contract defining what methods are available (like a TypeScript interface), (2) ApiFactory — creates the concrete implementation (typically an HTTP client that calls the backend), (3) useApi() hook — React components call this to get the API client. The backend implements the actual routes that the client calls.

📝 Detailed Explanation
API System — Typed Frontend → Backend Bridge
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  FRONTEND:                                                   │
  │  ① Define the contract (ApiRef):                            │
  │  export const anihpjApiRef = createApiRef({      │
  │    id: 'plugin.anihpj.api',                                  │
  │  });                                                         │
  │                                                              │
  │  ② Create the factory (ApiFactory):                         │
  │  createApiFactory({                                         │
  │    api: anihpjApiRef,                                       │
  │    deps: { fetchApi: fetchApiRef },                         │
  │    factory: ({ fetchApi }) => new AnihpjClient({ fetchApi }),│
  │  })                                                          │
  │                                                              │
  │  ③ Use in components (React):                               │
  │  const api = useApi(anihpjApiRef);                          │
  │  const metrics = await api.getMetrics();                    │
  │                                                              │
  │  BACKEND:                                                    │
  │  ④ Implement the routes:                                    │
  │  router.get('/metrics', async (req, res) => {               │
  │    const data = await fetchMetricsFromDB();                  │
  │    res.json(data);                                          │
  │  });                                                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The anihpj-dashboard frontend uses useApi(anihpjApiRef) to get a typed client. This client calls GET /api/anihpj/metrics on the backend, which queries PostgreSQL for deployment counts, GitHub for PR stats, and PagerDuty for incident data — all returned as structured JSON to the dashboard.

Customizing Q143 of 200
How do you set up TechDocs with AWS S3 storage?
A) TechDocs automatically uses S3 — just install the AWS CLI on the Backstage server
B) Configure `techdocs.publisher.type: 'awsS3'` in app-config.yaml. Set `techdocs.publisher.awsS3.bucketName`, credentials (accessKeyId + secretAccessKey or IAM role), and region. Ensure the Backstage backend has network access to S3 and the IAM role has s3:PutObject permission
C) Use the Backstage UI at /admin/techdocs/settings to select S3 and enter your bucket name
D) TechDocs doesn't support S3 — only Google Cloud Storage is supported for cloud storage
✅ Correct Answer: B

S3 storage for TechDocs requires app-config.yaml configuration plus AWS credentials. The publisher type must be 'awsS3', with bucket name, region, and credentials (either access keys or IAM role via instance profile). The Backstage backend needs s3:PutObject, s3:GetObject, and s3:ListBucket permissions on the bucket. All generated HTML, CSS, and assets are stored in S3 with entity-specific path prefixes.

📝 Detailed Explanation
TechDocs S3 Configuration
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  techdocs:                                                   │
  │    publisher:                                                │
  │      type: 'awsS3'                                           │
  │      awsS3:                                                  │
  │        bucketName: anihpj-techdocs-prod                      │
  │        region: us-east-1                                     │
  │        credentials:                                          │
  │          accessKeyId: ${AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID}                   │
  │          secretAccessKey: ${AWS_SECRET_ACCESS_KEY}           │
  │        s3ForcePathStyle: false                               │
  │        endpoint: https://s3.us-east-1.amazonaws.com          │
  │                                                              │
  │  REQUIRED IAM PERMISSIONS:                                   │
  │  s3:PutObject    ← Upload generated HTML                     │
  │  s3:GetObject    ← Serve documentation to users              │
  │  s3:ListBucket   ← Check if docs already exist               │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Production TechDocs uses the anihpj-techdocs-prod S3 bucket in us-east-1. The IAM role attached to the kubeadm node has the required S3 permissions. Dev environments use local filesystem storage.

Customizing Q144 of 200
How do you trigger a TechDocs rebuild?
A) Via the Backstage UI: navigate to the entity page → TechDocs tab → click "Rebuild Docs" button. Alternatively, the catalog refresh cycle auto-detects changed docs and rebuilds. You can also trigger via API: POST /api/techdocs/rebuild
B) Delete the entity and re-register it — TechDocs only builds on first registration
C) Run `yarn techdocs:rebuild --entity=component:default/jobpost-api` on the Backstage server
D) Push an empty commit with "rebuild-docs" in the message — TechDocs webhooks detect this
✅ Correct Answer: A

TechDocs rebuilds can be triggered through three distinct paths: (1) UI Button (manual, immediate) — navigate to any entity page → TechDocs tab → click the "Rebuild Docs" button. This triggers an immediate rebuild of the MkDocs documentation from the entity's source repository. Use this when you need updated docs NOW (e.g., after an urgent doc fix). (2) Auto-detection (automatic, periodic) — during the catalog refresh cycle, Backstage detects Git commit changes in the entity's source repository. If the docs directory (docs/) or mkdocs.yml has changed, it automatically triggers a rebuild. This happens on the catalog refresh schedule (typically every few minutes). (3) API Call (programmatic) — POST /api/techdocs/rebuild with a JSON body { "entityRef": "component:default/jobpost-api" }. This is ideal for CI/CD pipelines: after merging a documentation PR, the CI job calls this API to rebuild docs immediately, rather than waiting for the next catalog refresh. The rebuild process runs MkDocs in Docker (or locally, depending on the techdocs.builder config) and publishes the generated HTML to the configured storage (local filesystem, GCS, S3, or Azure Blob).

📝 Detailed Explanation
TechDocs Rebuild — Three Trigger Paths
  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  ① UI BUTTON — Manual, Immediate                             ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  Entity Page → TechDocs Tab → [Rebuild Docs]                ║
  ║  → Builds NOW. Use after urgent doc fixes.                  ║
  ║  → Developer action, no waiting for refresh cycle           ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  ② AUTO-DETECTION — Periodic, Catalog Refresh                ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  Catalog Refresh → Check Git for changes in docs/           ║
  ║  → If mkdocs.yml or docs/* changed → Auto-rebuild           ║
  ║  → If no changes → Skip (save resources)                    ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ╔══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╗
  ║  ③ API CALL — Programmatic, CI/CD Integration                ║
  ╠══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╣
  ║  POST /api/techdocs/rebuild                                  ║
  ║  Body: { "entityRef": "component:default/jobpost-api" }      ║
  ║  → CI job calls after doc PR merge                           ║
  ║  → No waiting for catalog refresh cycle                     ║
  ╚══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════╝

  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  REBUILD PROCESS (all triggers lead to same flow):           │
  │  ① Clone entity's source repo at configured branch           │
  │  ② Run: mkdocs build (in Docker or locally)                  │
  │  ③ Publish generated HTML to storage (GCS/S3/Azure/local)   │
  │  ④ Update catalog: TechDocs URL now points to new build     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: Developers use the "Rebuild Docs" button after pushing urgent documentation fixes to the jobpost-api repo — docs update in under 30 seconds. The CI/CD pipeline triggers a rebuild via API call after merging any PR that touches docs/ or mkdocs.yml, ensuring docs are always up to date without waiting for the catalog refresh. The catalog refresh cycle (every 5 minutes) catches any docs changes that weren't triggered by CI (e.g., direct commits to main). TechDocs are stored in Google Cloud Storage (GCS) for the production cluster, with the staging cluster using local filesystem storage for faster iteration.

B) A drag-and-drop UI builder for creating documentation pages without writing Markdown

Customizing Q147 of 200
How do TechDocs handle multiple versions of documentation?
A) TechDocs automatically versions docs based on Git tags — each tag gets its own documentation site
B) TechDocs does NOT natively support versioning. The recommended approach is to document only the latest version (main branch). For multi-version docs, use separate entities or include version-specific pages within the same docs site (e.g., /v1/, /v2/ subdirectories)
C) TechDocs has a built-in version selector — configure versions in mkdocs.yml under `versions`
D) TechDocs integrates with Git tags — each tag creates a versioned docs site at /docs/v1.0/, /docs/v2.0/
✅ Correct Answer: B

TechDocs follows the "docs-as-code, latest-only" philosophy. By design, it documents the latest version (what's on the main branch). This avoids the complexity of maintaining documentation for multiple versions — a common pain point in traditional documentation systems. For services that genuinely need multi-version docs, the workaround is to use separate entities per version or organize version-specific content within the same docs site using subdirectories.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Multi-Version Documentation Strategy
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  RECOMMENDED: Latest only (docs-as-code)                     │
  │  docs/                                                       │
  │  ├── index.md          ← Current API reference (v2)          │
  │  ├── migration/        ← Migration guides from old versions  │
  │  │   └── v1-to-v2.md                                        │
  │  └── changelog.md      ← What changed in each version        │
  │                                                              │
  │  IF YOU NEED VERSIONED DOCS:                                 │
  │  Option A: Separate entities                                 │
  │  Component:default/jobpost-api     (latest)                  │
  │  Component:default/jobpost-api-v1  (legacy — deprecated)     │
  │                                                              │
  │  Option B: Subdirectories within same docs                   │
  │  docs/                                                       │
  │  ├── v1/api-reference.md                                    │
  │  └── v2/api-reference.md                                    │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The team follows the "latest only" approach. When jobpost-api v2 replaced v1, the old v1 entity was marked as deprecated with a migration guide, and the new v2 entity has its own fresh TechDocs site.

Customizing Q149 of 200
What is the TechDocs reader vs builder architecture?
A) Reader and builder are the same component — both run in the browser
B) Reader is a Python MkDocs server; builder is a Node.js CLI tool
C) Reader is the Backstage backend; builder is the developer's local machine
D) Builder generates static HTML from Markdown (runs MkDocs, server-side, produces HTML/CSS/JS). Reader displays the generated HTML in the Backstage UI (fetches from storage, renders in an iframe-like container). They're decoupled — you can build with Docker but serve from S3 across multiple Backstage replicas
✅ Correct Answer: D

TechDocs has a decoupled builder-reader architecture. The Builder runs server-side (on the Backstage backend or a separate build service), executes MkDocs to convert Markdown → static HTML, and publishes to object storage. The Reader is the frontend component that fetches the pre-built HTML from storage and renders it in the Backstage UI. This decoupling means builds can happen on one server while docs are served from S3 to multiple Backstage replicas — the builder and reader don't need to share a filesystem.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Builder-Reader Decoupling
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  BUILDER (server-side, runs MkDocs):                         │
  │  ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────┐    ┌──────────────┐           │
  │  │ Source   │ →  │ MkDocs   │ →  │ Object       │           │
  │  │ (GitHub) │    │ (Docker) │    │ Storage (S3) │           │
  │  └──────────┘    └──────────┘    └──────┬───────┘           │
  │                                         │                    │
  │  ───────────────────────────────────────┼─────────────────  │
  │                                         │                    │
  │  READER (browser, renders HTML):        │                    │
  │  ┌──────────────────────────┐           │                    │
  │  │  Backstage UI            │◄──────────┘                   │
  │  │  /docs/.../jobpost-api   │  fetches HTML from storage    │
  │  │  ┌────────────────────┐  │                               │
  │  │  │  Rendered MkDocs   │  │                               │
  │  │  │  site (HTML/CSS)   │  │                               │
  │  │  └────────────────────┘  │                               │
  │  └──────────────────────────┘                               │
  │                                                              │
  │  ✅ Builder can be on a different server than Reader         │
  │  ✅ Multiple Backstage replicas read from same S3 bucket     │
  │  ✅ Builder failure doesn't affect reading existing docs     │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The TechDocs builder runs as part of the Backstage backend on kubeadm. Built documentation is published to the anihpj-techdocs S3 bucket. Both production and staging Backstage instances read from the same S3 bucket, ensuring consistent documentation across environments.

Customizing Q150 of 200
How do you set up TechDocs for the anihpj/jobpost project?
A) TechDocs is enabled globally — no per-project setup needed, it auto-scans all repos
B) Submit a request to the platform team to add your repo to the TechDocs allowlist
C) Create docs/ directory with Markdown files + mkdocs.yml at repo root, add `backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:.` annotation to catalog-info.yaml, ensure the repo is in the catalog, and TechDocs auto-builds on the next refresh cycle
D) Install the TechDocs CLI on your local machine and run `techdocs publish` after each docs change
✅ Correct Answer: C

Setting up TechDocs for a project requires three things: (1) docs/ directory + mkdocs.yml at the repo root (or location specified by the annotation), (2) annotation backstage.io/techdocs-ref: dir:. in the entity's catalog-info.yaml (tells TechDocs WHERE to look), (3) the entity must be in the catalog (registered via GitHub Discovery or manual registration). Once these are in place, TechDocs automatically builds documentation on the next catalog refresh.

📝 Detailed Explanation
TechDocs Setup for anihpj/jobpost
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  ① PROJECT FILES (in GitHub repo):                           │
  │  jobpost-api/                                                │
  │  ├── mkdocs.yml          ← MkDocs configuration              │
  │  ├── docs/                                                   │
  │  │   ├── index.md        ← Documentation homepage            │
  │  │   ├── api-reference.md                                   │

  │  │   ├── adrs/           ← Architecture Decision Records    │

  │  └── catalog/                                                │

  │  ② catalog-info.yaml ANNOTATION:                             │

  │                                                              │
  │  ③ RESULT (automatic):                                       │

For anihpj/jobpost: Every Django service created through the template automatically gets a docs/ directory with a basic mkdocs.yml and catalog-info.yaml with the TechDocs annotation. Customizing Q151 of 200
What is a plugin's package.json structure?
A) Standard npm package.json — plugins don't need special fields
B) Must include `@backstage` scope, `backstage.role` field set to 'frontend-plugin', 'backend-plugin', or 'frontend-plugin-module'/'backend-plugin-module', standard fields like main/types/files, and dependencies on @backstage/core packages
C) Plugins use a custom `plugin.json` format — not standard package.json
D) package.json must include a `backstage-plugin` keyword and a `pluginId` field
✅ Correct Answer: B

Backstage plugins are standard npm packages with the @backstage scope or @internal for private plugins. The key distinguishing field is backstage.role: 'frontend-plugin' for UI plugins, 'backend-plugin' for server plugins, and '-module' variants for modules that extend existing plugins. This role tells the Backstage CLI how to build, test, and lint the package.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Plugin package.json — Key Fields
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐

  │      "role": "frontend-plugin"   ← KEY FIELD                  │
  │    },                                                        │
  │    "scripts": {                                              │

  │    },                                                        │
  │    "dependencies": {                                         │
  │      "@backstage/core-plugin-api": "^1.9.0",                │
  │      "@backstage/core-components": "^0.14.0",               │
  │      "@material-ui/core": "^4.12.0"                          │
  │    }                                                         │
  │  }                                                           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The anihpj-dashboard plugin uses `"role": "frontend-plugin"`, while the anihpj-templates backend uses `"role": "backend-plugin"`. These roles drive the build tooling — frontend plugins get Webpack bundling, backend plugins get TypeScript compilation.

Customizing Q152 of 200
How do you share code between plugins?
A) Copy-paste the code — each plugin should be completely independent
B) Use a shared PostgreSQL table that all plugins read from
C) Create a common package (e.g., `@internal/anihpj-common`) that exports shared types, utilities, and API clients. Both plugins depend on this common package. Use `createApiRef()` and `createApiFactory()` for shared API definitions
D) Plugins automatically share code through Backstage's plugin API — no additional setup needed

Code sharing between plugins follows the common package pattern. For anihpj/jobpost: The @internal/anihpj-common package defines shared types (AnihpjEntity, TeamMetrics, TemplateDefaults) and API definitions used by both the dashboard and backend plugins. This ensures the frontend and backend always agree on data structures.

4.2 Frontend Plugins — React Components, Routes & ExtensionsQ153-Q160

Customizing Q153 of 200
What are routable extensions in Backstage?
A) Routable extensions allow plugins to expose React components at specific routes. Uses `createRoutableExtension()` with lazy loading: `component: () => import('./Component').then(m => m.Component)`. Placed at `mountPoint` (a route ref) and render at that URL
B) Routable extensions are backend Express routes exposed to the frontend — REST API endpoints
C) Routable extensions are browser URL redirects configured in app-config.yaml
D) Routable extensions are npm packages installed globally via `yarn global add`
✅ Correct Answer: A

Routable extensions are how plugins add pages to the Backstage UI. They use createRoutableExtension() which takes: a name, a component (lazy-loaded via dynamic import for code splitting), and a mountPoint (a RouteRef defining where this page lives). When the user navigates to the route, the component is loaded and rendered. This lazy loading is critical for performance — plugin code only loads when the user visits the page.

📝 Detailed Explanation
Routable Extensions — Pages at Routes
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │  export const AnihpjPage = anihpjPlugin.provide(            │
  │    createRoutableExtension({                                 │
  │      name: 'AnihpjPage',                                    │
  │      component: () =>                                       │
  │        import('./components/Dashboard').then(               │
  │          m => m.Dashboard),        ← Lazy-loaded!            │
  │      mountPoint: rootRoute,        ← RouteRef               │
  │    })                                                        │
  │  );                                                          │
  │                                                              │
  │  ROUTING:                                                    │
  │  /anihpj → AnihpjPage renders                               │
  │  /catalog → CatalogPage renders                              │
  │  /create → ScaffolderPage renders                            │
  │  /docs → TechDocsPage renders                                │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

For anihpj/jobpost: The anihpj-dashboard plugin uses a routable extension to render at /anihpj. The dashboard component loads lazily — when a developer clicks "Anihpj" in the sidebar, the dashboard code is fetched and rendered.

Adding a tab to entity pages requires creating a component extension that renders within the entity context. The tab receives the entity data via the useEntity() hook, so your plugin can access the entity's metadata, spec, and annotations.

  │                                        │
  │                                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

Customizing Q155 of 200
What is the useEntity() hook?
A) A hook that creates a new entity in the catalog via the API
B) A hook for fetching a list of all entities from the catalog
C) A hook for updating entity metadata in the database
D) A React hook from `@backstage/plugin-catalog-react` that provides the current entity context to any component rendered on an entity page. Returns `{ entity }` with full metadata, spec, annotations, and relations. Only works within entity page layouts
✅ Correct Answer: D

useEntity() is the primary way entity page components access entity data. It returns the full entity object: entity.apiVersion, entity.kind, entity.metadata.name, entity.metadata.annotations, entity.spec.owner, entity.relations (resolved dependencies). It only works inside an EntityLayout context — using it elsewhere throws an error.
